Cumulants, Spreadability and the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff Series
Takahiro Hasebe, Franz Lehner

Abstract
. We define spreadability systems as a generalization of exchangeability systems in order to unify various notions of independence and cumulants known in noncommutative probability. In particular, our theory covers monotone independence and monotone cumulants which do not satisfy exchangeability. To this end we study generalized zeta and Möbius functions in the context of the incidence algebra of the semilattice of ordered set partitions and prove an appropriate variant of Faà di Bruno’s theorem. With the aid of this machinery we show that our cumulants cover most of the previously known cumulants. Due to noncommutativity of independence the behaviour of these cumulants with respect to independent random variables is more complicated than in the exchangeable case and the appearance of Goldberg coefficients exhibits the role of the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff series in this context. Moreover, we exhibit an interpretation of the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff series as a sum of cumulants in a particular spreadability system, thus providing a new derivation of the Goldberg coefficients.



2000 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46L53, Secondary 60C05, 17B01, 05A18

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background: independence and cumulants

Cumulants were introduced by Thiele in the late 19th century as a combinatorial
means to describe independence of classical random variables.
In free probability existence of cumulants was indicated by Voiculescu

[
Voi85
]
and described explicitly by Speicher

[
Spe94
]
.
Free cumulants are one of the cornerstones
in free probability, complementing the analytic machinery of Cauchy transforms,
see
[
NS06
]
for many applications.
Later on other kinds of cumulants were introduced in noncommutative
probability,
e.g.,
Boolean cumulants were defined in
[
SW97
]
in the context of Boolean independence
(see also
[
vW73
,
vW75
]
);
various kinds of
q
-deformed cumulants were considered in

[
Nic95
,
Ans01
]

in order to interpolate between classical and free cumulants (however no
q
-convolution has been found so far);
conditionally free cumulants were defined in
[
BLS96
]
which generalize both
free and Boolean cumulants.
The second-named author gave a unified theory of the cumulants mentioned above
in the framework of so-called
exchangeability systems

as a general notion of independence
[
Leh04
]
.
In the present paper we develop a yet more general framework which
comprises also Muraki’s monotone independence
[
Mur01
]
,
which is not covered by the approach of
[
Leh04
]
because
it does not satisfy exchangeability, the obstruction being that
monotone independence is sensitive to the order on random variables:

(O)

Independence of
X
and
Y
is not equivalent to independence of
Y
and
X
.

Hence in order to avoid misinterpretations we say “the (ordered) pair
(X,Y)
is
monotone independent” rather than “
X
and
Y
are monotone independent”.
This property sharply distinguishes monotone independence from classical, free,
and Boolean independences.
Despite lack of exchangeability, the first-named author
together with H. Saigo managed to define monotone cumulants
[
HS11b
,
HS11a
]

relying only on the property called
extensivity
defined below.
If we denote the monotone cumulants by
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{n}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
with
respect to a noncommutative probability space
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
,
then we have the moment-cumulant formula

(MC)

\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n}}\frac{1}{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!}K_{(\pi)}^{\mathrm{M}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

where the set

\mathcal{M}_{n}
of monotone partitions is a
subclass of ordered set partitions rather than set partitions.
The factor
\frac{1}{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!}
accounts for the number of possible
orderings of the blocks of the underlying set partition of
\pi

and cancels out in the case of exchangeability,
like classical, free or Boolean independence.

Cumulants carry essential information on independence, in particular the

vanishing
of mixed cumulants, that is, of cumulants with independent
entries, characterize independence
[
Leh04
, Prop. 3.5]
,
which is the major reason for their usefulness in free probability

[
NS06
]
.
More precisely, if a finite family of random variables

X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}
can be partitioned into two mutually independent
subfamilies
(in the general sense of Definition
2.12
below)
then

(V)

K_{n}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=0.

As a consequence, cumulants are additive, that is, the cumulant of the sum of two independent tuples

(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
and

(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n})
decomposes as

(1.1)

K_{n}(X_{1}+Y_{1},X_{2}+Y_{2},\dots,X_{n}+Y_{n})=K_{n}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})+K_{n}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}).

By contrast, because of property (
O
), monotone
cumulants do not satisfy additivity and thus mixed cumulants do not necessarily
vanish. Instead, they satisfy the weaker notion of
extensivity
:
if
\{(X_{1}^{(j)},X_{2}^{(j)},\dots,X_{n}^{(j)})\}_{j=1}^{\infty}

is a sequence of monotone independent random vectors such that

(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\overset{d}{=}(X_{1}^{(j)},X_{2}^{(j)},\dots,X_{n}^{(j)})

for any
j\geq 1
, then

(E)

K_{n}^{\mathrm{M}}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\dots,N.X_{n})=NK_{n}^{\mathrm{M}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}),

where
N.X_{i}=X_{i}^{(1)}+X_{i}^{(2)}+\cdots+X_{i}^{(N)}
is the sum of i.i.d. copies.

Extensivity is strictly weaker than the property of vanishing of mixed cumulants,
but extensivity (together with some other properties) still suffices
to prove uniqueness of cumulants even in the case of exchangeability.
Therefore extensivity is a natural generalization of the property of
vanishing of mixed cumulants.

1.2.
Main objectives of the present paper

The first goal of this paper is the unification of second-named author’s
approach to cumulants based on exchangeability systems and first-named author’s
monotone cumulants based on universal products of states.
The second-named author’s definition of cumulants includes
q
-deformed
cumulants as well as tensor (or classical), free and Boolean cumulants. On the
other hand, the approach of the first-named author and Saigo comprises monotone
cumulants as well as tensor, free and Boolean cumulants, but not
q
-deformed
cumulants. In the present paper
we establish a unified theory based on the concept of spreadability
which has been considered recently by Köstler
[
Kös10
]
in the noncommutative context. Similar to the transition from symmetric to quasisymmetric functions,
the concept of
spreadability systems
naturally arises as a generalization of
exchangeability systems and allows to unify various
kinds of independence and cumulants, including conditionally monotone
independence
[
Has11
]
and two other generalized
notions of independence
[
Has10
]
, with a generalization of the moment-cumulant
formula (
MC
) and the property of extensivity
(
E
).

Our approach is combinatorial on the basis of
ordered set partitions
.
An ordered set partition is defined as an ordered
sequence
of disjoint subsets
whose union is the entire set (say
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
).
We first investigate the structure of the semilattices
\mathcal{OP}_{n}

of ordered set partitions (with respect to dominance order)
and show that they locally look like the lattices
\mathcal{I}_{n}
of interval partitions,
in the sense that any interval in
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
is isomorphic to an interval
in
\mathcal{I}_{k}
for an appropriate
k
.
This property is crucial for the study of multiplicative functions
in the incidence algebra of ordered set partitions and we establish
an isomorphism with the composition algebra of generating
functions analogous to the well known formula of Faà di Bruno.
In particular, we obtain the fundamental convolution identity
for the generalized zeta and Möbius functions.

Our interest in ordered set partitions was stipulated by the appearance of
monotone partitions in the classification of independence
[
Mur03
]

and their role for monotone cumulants
[
HS11b
]
,
but presently it turns out that the structure of ordered set partitions is
actually easier to describe than that of monotone partitions.
For example we do not have a good understanding of the structure of intervals

[\sigma,\pi]\subseteq\mathcal{M}_{n}
for
\sigma,\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and in particular
the values of the Möbius function remain mysterious.

The second goal is the application of our results to
free Lie algebras
.
Connections of free probability to formal groups have been pointed out early by
Voiculescu
[
Voi85
]
and more recently by Friedrich and
McKay
[
FM15
]
and
in the context of monotone probability by
Manzel and Schürmann
[
MS17
]
;
see also an approach via shuffle algebras

[
EFP18
]
.

Here we obtain new formulations of some well known identities in terms of
ordered set partitions and cumulants.
In particular our cumulants turn out to coincide with the homogenous components
of the
Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula
, if an appropriate spreadability
system is chosen. Thus a new derivation of the coefficients of the
Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula (“Goldberg coefficients”)
drops out as a by-product of our results on spreadability systems.

We hope that our results will stimulate more connections to combinatorics,
in particular Hopf algebras and noncommutative quasi-symmetric functions

[
BZ09
]
.
Our proofs only use elementary and at times tedious calculations, yet
we suspect that many of our results have been obtained in different contexts
before and that those with the right knowledge will find easier and more
conceptual proofs.
After a first draft of the present paper was published,
this direction was taken up in
[
LNT20
]
.

1.3.
Main results and structure of the paper

Most definitions and results of the present paper are expressed in the language
of ordered set partitions.
In order to avoid a large overhead and streamline the presentation all relevant
material concerning these and related objects has been collected in
Appendix
A
.
This includes in particular the description of intervals in the poset of
ordered set partitions, incidence algebras, multiplicative functions, generalized zeta functions and the corresponding Möbius functions.

In Section
2
, we define spreadability systems
(Definition
2.2
) and give examples coming from the four (or
five) universal products of linear maps (Section
2.3
).
Roughly speaking, a

spreadability system
for a noncommutative probability space
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)

consists of a larger space
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi})
containing
copies
X^{(i)},i\in\mathbb{N},
of each element
X\in\mathcal{A}
whose joint
distribution in invariant under spreading, i.e., such that

(1.2)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(j_{1})}X_{2}^{(j_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(j_{n})})

whenever the entries of the multiindices

(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
and
(j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{n})

are in one-to-one correspondence via an order preserving map;
in other words, whenever the kernel ordered set partitions

\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
and
\kappa(j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{n})

(Definition
A.10
) coincide.
In this case the common value of (
1.2
) only depends
on said ordered set partition
\pi=\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
and is
denoted by

(1.3)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}).

In typical examples (like the ones presented in Section
2.3
),

\mathcal{U}
is the free product or tensor product of copies of
\mathcal{A}
,

\tilde{\varphi}
is a universal product (e.g., the free product)
of the copies of
\varphi
, and
X^{(i)}
is the
natural embedding of
X
into
\mathcal{U}
as the
i
th component; then
(
1.2
) holds trivially.
The main idea is to regard
\{X^{(i)},i\in\mathbb{N}\}
as i.i.d. copies of the random variable
X
and condition (
1.2
) gives rise to a generalized notion of i.i.d. sequences
\mathcal{A}^{(i)}:=\{X^{(i)}:X\in\mathcal{A}\},i\in\mathbb{N}
of subalgebras of
\mathcal{U}
.
Using this, we can induce a notion of independence on the original algebra

\mathcal{A}
by generalizing the following elementary observation from classical probability:
two random variables
X
and
Y
are independent if and only if the joint distribution of the
random vector
(X,Y)
is the same as that of
(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)})
,
where
(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)})
and
(X^{(2)},Y^{(2)})
are i.i.d. copies of

(X,Y)
.
More precisely, our algebraic notion of independence
can be formulated in lattice theoretic terms as follows:
a sequence of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
, where
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, is said to be

\mathcal{S}
-independent
if for any tuple of indices

(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
,
any tuple of random variables
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

with
X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{j}}

and any ordered set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, we have

(1.4)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}),

where
\curlywedge
is roughly the usual operation
\wedge
for the underlying set partitions, equipped with the lexicographic order on blocks, see Definition
A.19
.
For several specific spreadability systems this definition reproduces previous
notions of independence (Proposition
2.18
).

In Section
3
, we define cumulants associated to a
spreadability system and express cumulants in terms of moments and vice
versa (Theorem
3.11
). More precisely, we adapt Rota’s

dot operation
from umbral calculus
[
RT94
]

for our purpose and write

N.X:=X^{(1)}+X^{(2)}+\cdots+X^{(N)},

for “the sum of i.i.d. random variables”.
Using the notation

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})}):=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}),

we show that for each
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}

the partitioned expectation
\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\dots,N.X_{n})
is a
polynomial in
N
and the coefficient of
the lowest order term
N^{|\pi|}
is the
\pi
-cumulant with respect to the
spreadability system
\mathcal{S}
, denoted by
K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
.
These coefficients satisfy the fundamental properties of
cumulants listed in Definition
3.5

and are related to the partitionend moments
(
1.3
)
by
generalized Möbius inversion
,

(1.5)

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}K_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\widetilde{\zeta}(\sigma,\pi),

where
\widetilde{\mu}
and
\widetilde{\zeta}
are generalized Möbius and
zeta functions, respectively (see Definition
A.28
).
In the
special case of the maximal element
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
these coefficients
evaluate to
\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\hat{1}_{n})=\frac{(-1)^{n-|\sigma|}}{|\sigma|}
and
\zeta(\sigma,\hat{1}_{n})=\frac{1}{|\sigma|!}
, cf. formula (
MC
).

These concepts have a certain affinity to formal group laws
and exhibit a Lie algebraic flavour, which is
confirmed by the considerations in Section
6
.

We also prove extensivity (Proposition
3.16
) and uniqueness of
cumulants (Theorem
3.6
). The previous moment-cumulant formulas for the free, tensor, Boolean, monotone and c-monotone cases in the
literature are listed as special cases (Propositions
3.24
and
3.26
); for instance,
the partitioned cumulants
K_{\pi}^{\rm M}
associated with the monotone spreadability system are shown to satisfy multiplicativity

(1.7)

K^{\mathrm{M}}_{\pi}=\begin{cases}0,&\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{n},\\
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{(\pi)},&\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n},\end{cases}

which, together with (
1.6
), implies the known formula
(
MC
).
While associativity of monotone independence is crucial for the proof of
(
1.7
),
this property is not required
for the construction of cumulants for general spreadability systems.

In Section
4
we establish recursive differential
equations for the time evolution of moments. This generalizes for example the
complex Burgers equation in free probability
[
Voi86
]
. Motivations for this section come especially from the effective use of differential equations in monotone probability theory.

In Sections
5
and
6
we encounter the “Goldberg coefficients” in two different ways whose connections are still unclear.
In Section
5
,
we compute
mixed cumulants
, i.e.,
we express cumulants of random variables which split into “independent subsets” in terms of lower order cumulants (Theorem
5.6
):
a sequence of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
, where

I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, is
\mathcal{S}
-independent if and only if for any tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
, any random variables
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}}\times\mathcal{A}_{i_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{i_{n}}
and any ordered set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, we have

(1.8)

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})-\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}K_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,g(\tau,\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n}),\pi)=0,

where
g(\tau,\eta,\pi)
is what we call the Goldberg coefficient. In the special case
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
it is given by

(1.9)

g(\tau,\eta,\hat{1}_{n})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{q_{1}!q_{2}!\cdots q_{r}!}\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des_{\eta}(\tau)}(1+x)^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}P_{q_{j}}(x)\,dx,&\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta},\\
0,&\bar{\tau}\not\leq\bar{\eta},\end{cases}

where
r
and
q_{j}
are certain integers determined by
\tau
and
\eta
,

\des_{\eta}(\tau),\asc_{\eta}(\tau)

are the numbers of descents and ascents of the multiset permutations
naturally associated with
(\eta,\tau)
.

P_{q}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{q}k!\,S(q,k)\,x^{k-1}

are the homogeneous Euler polynomials and
S(q,k)
are the Stirling numbers of
the second kind.
For general
\pi
,
g(\tau,\eta,\pi)
is either zero or the product of
g(\tau\rfloor_{P},\eta\rfloor_{P},\hat{1}_{|P|})
over
P\in\pi
.
Formula (
1.8
) sheds light on the gap between extensivity (
E
)
and the vanishing property (
V
) and reveals the role of
the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula.

In Section
6
we present an
operator-valued “unshuffle” spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}

which is interesting from a combinatorial point of view:
The corresponding
cumulants reproduce the homogeneous components of the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff
formula; in particular, the specialization of our cumulants
to free algebras corresponds to Lie projectors.
The definition of the spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
is rather simple:

\mathcal{A}
is any unital algebra,
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
is the
identity map,
\mathcal{U}
is the infinite tensor product of the copies of

\mathcal{A}
, and
X^{(i)}=1^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes X\otimes 1^{\otimes\infty}\in\mathcal{U}
is the natural embedding of
X\in\mathcal{A}
into
\mathcal{U}
as the

i
-th tensor factor. The linear map
\tilde{\varphi}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{A}
is given by concatenation of words:

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}\otimes X_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n}\otimes 1^{\infty}):=X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n}
.
Consequently, the mixed expectation yields a rearrangement (“unshuffle”)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}):=X_{P_{1}}X_{P_{2}}\cdots X_{P_{k}},

where
\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})=(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{k})
and
X_{P}
is the
ordered product of
X_{p}
’s over the elements
p\in P
, see (
2.1
).

For this specific spreadability system,
\mathcal{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
-independence
of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
turns out to be equivalent to the
commutativity
[\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{j}]=0
for
i\neq j
(Remark

6.9
). The second cumulant
K_{\hat{1}_{2}}(X,Y)
is
the commutator
\frac{1}{2}[X,Y]
.

Finally we give a new derivation of the coefficients of the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula
(also known as “Goldberg coefficients”) using the moment-cumulant formulas
(
1.5
) and (
1.6
) for the particular spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
.
To this end specialize
\mathcal{A}
to the free associative algebra generated by a
set of noncommuting variables
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots
,
fix
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
q_{j},i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
for
j\in[r]
such that
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
for
j\in[r-1]
,
then the coefficient of the monomial

a_{i_{1}}^{q_{1}}a_{i_{2}}^{q_{2}}\cdots a_{i_{r}}^{q_{r}}

appearing in
\log(e^{a_{1}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})
is given by

(1.10)

\frac{1}{q_{1}!q_{2}!\cdots q_{r}!}\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des(\underline{i})}(1+x)^{\asc(\underline{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}P_{q_{j}}(x)\,dx,

where
\underline{i}
stands for the sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
and
P_{q}(x)
are the homogeneous Euler polynomials already encountered in (
1.9
).

In Section
7
we briefly discuss the central limit theorem associated to
spreadability systems satisfying a certain singleton condition.

We conclude the paper with a few open problems.
One of them is to find an explanation or unified proof of the surprisingly coinciding formulas (
1.9
) and (
1.10
).

2.
Spreadability systems and independence

2.1.
Notation and terminology for noncommutative probability

From now on we denote by
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
associative algebras over the field

\mathbb{C}
. Elements of
\mathcal{A}
are called
random variables
, and elements of
\mathcal{A}^{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
are called

random vectors
. An (algebraic)
\mathcal{B}
-valued
expectation
is a linear map

\varphi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}

and we call the pair
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
an
(algebraic)

\mathcal{B}
-valued noncommutative probability space
(
\mathcal{B}
-ncps).
In the case where
\mathcal{B}
is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
and
\varphi

is a
\mathcal{B}
-module map in the sense that

\varphi(bab^{\prime})=b\,\varphi(a)\,b^{\prime}
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}
and

b,b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}
, the map
\varphi
is called
conditional
expectation
. This property will however not be crucial in the context of the
present paper.

Remark 2.1
.

(i)

In order to be able to include Boolean and monotone
products and some other examples (Section
2.3
)
we do not a priori assume the unitality. In some examples
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are naturally unital; then we also assume that
\varphi
is also unital, i.e.,
\varphi(1_{\mathcal{A}})=1_{\mathcal{B}}
and also that involved subalgebras of
\mathcal{A}
contain the unit of
\mathcal{A}
.

(ii)

Usually the involved algebras are
\ast
-algebras and the linear maps satisfy positivity.
However positivity is not essential here and we stick to the algebraic

\mathcal{B}
-valued setting, which allows to include the interesting
example of Lie polynomials in Section
6
.

We say that two sequences
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty},(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{A}

have
the same distribution
if

\varphi(X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\dotsm X_{i_{n}})=\varphi(Y_{i_{1}}Y_{i_{2}}\dotsm Y_{i_{n}})

for any tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
, and in this case we
write
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\overset{d}{=}(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
.

Alternatively, in the categorical approach (see, e.g.,
[
MS17
]
),
a random variable is a unital homomorphism
\iota:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}

from some unital algebra
\mathcal{D}
into
\mathcal{A}
.
This definition extends also to
random vectors.
Indeed, given a random vector
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
, we get a homomorphism
\iota:\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}
defined by
\iota(x_{i})=X_{i}
, where
\mathcal{D}
is the nonunital algebra freely generated by noncommuting indeterminates
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}
.
Let
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\varphi_{1})
,

(\mathcal{A}_{2},\varphi_{2})
be two
\mathcal{B}
-ncps such that
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
take
values in a common algebra
\mathcal{B}
. Sequences of random variables

(\iota_{1}^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\Hom(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{1}),(\iota_{2}^{(j)})_{j=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\Hom(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{A}_{2})
have

the same distribution
if

\varphi_{1}(\iota_{1}^{(i_{1})}(x_{1})\iota_{1}^{(i_{1})}(x_{2})\dotsm\iota_{1}^{(i_{n})}(x_{n}))=\varphi_{2}(\iota_{2}^{(i_{1})}(x_{1})\iota_{2}^{(i_{2})}(x_{2})\cdots\iota_{2}^{(i_{n})}(x_{n}))

for any
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and any
x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\in\mathcal{D}
. In this case we write

(\iota_{1}^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty}\overset{d}{=}(\iota_{2}^{(j)})_{j=1}^{\infty}.

Given
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and

P=\{p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{k}\}\subseteq[n]
with
p_{1}<p_{2}<\cdots<p_{k}
,
it will be convenient to introduce the notation

(2.1)

X_{P}:=X_{p_{1}}X_{p_{2}}\cdots X_{p_{k}}

for the ordered product. For a
k
-linear functional
M\colon\mathcal{A}^{k}\to\mathcal{B}
, we denote

(2.2)

M(X_{P}):=M(X_{p_{1}},X_{p_{2}},\ldots,X_{p_{k}}).

Recall that the tensor product has the universal property that any multilinear map

T:\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}

has a unique lifting to a linear map

\tilde{T}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{B}

such that on rank 1 tensors we have
\tilde{T}(a_{1}\otimes a_{2}\otimes\dots\otimes a_{n})=T(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n})
. We will
tacitly identify
T
with
\tilde{T}
in
order to simplify notation.

2.2.
Spreadability systems

In this subsection we introduce the notation necessary to generalize
the notions of partitioned moment and cumulant functionals of
[
Leh04
]

from the exchangeable setting to the spreadable setting.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps.

1.

A
spreadability system
for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
is a triplet

\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
satisfying the
following properties:

(i)

(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi})
is a
\mathcal{B}
-valued ncps.

(ii)

\iota^{(i)}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{U}
is a homomorphism
such that
\varphi=\tilde{\varphi}\circ\iota^{(i)}
for
each
i\geq 1
. For simplicity,
\iota^{(i)}(X)
is denoted by
X^{(i)}
,
X\in\mathcal{A}
, and we denote by
\mathcal{A}^{(i)}
the image
of
\mathcal{A}
under
\iota^{(i)}
.

(iii)

The identity

(2.3)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(h(i_{1}))}X_{2}^{(h(i_{2}))}\cdots X_{n}^{(h(i_{n}))})

holds for any
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, any
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and any
order preserving
map
h:\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}\}\to\mathbb{N}
, that is,
i_{p}<i_{q}
implies
h(i_{p})<h(i_{q})
.

2.

A triplet
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
is called an

exchangeability system
if, in addition to (i), (ii) above, eq. (
2.3
) holds for any
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, any
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
and any
permutation

h\in\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}:=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

Remark 2.3
.

1.

In some examples where
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\varphi
are unital, the extended algebra
\mathcal{U}
is also unital and
\iota^{(i)}
are unit-preserving. To be specific, this applies to the tensor and free spreadability systems in Section
2.3
and the unshuffle spreadability system in Section
6
.

2.

It is easy to see that condition (
2.3
) can be rephrased as follows:

(\iota^{(1)},\iota^{(2)},\dots)\overset{d}{=}(\iota^{(n_{1})},\iota^{(n_{2})},\dots)

for any strictly increasing sequence
(n_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
. This
is the definition given in
[
Kös10
]
.

3.

It is straightforward to extend the definition of exchangeability
systems (resp., spreadability systems) from
\mathbb{N}

to an arbitrary set (resp., arbitrary totally ordered set).

Definition 2.4
.

(i)

Using the concept of kernel partition from Definition
A.10
the condition of spreadability
(
2.3
)
is equivalent to the requirement that

(2.4)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(j_{1})}X_{2}^{(j_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(j_{n})})

holds whenever the kernels
coincide, i.e.,
\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})=\kappa(j_{1},j_{2},\ldots,j_{n})
,
see Definition
A.10
.
That is, the expectation (
2.3
)
only depends on the ordered kernel set partition
\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
.
Thus for every ordered set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}

we can define a multilinear functional

\varphi_{\pi}\colon\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
by choosing any representative sequence

(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
with
\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})=\pi
and setting

(2.5)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}).

The invariance (
2.4
) ensures that
this definition is consistent and does not depend on the choice of the representative.

(ii)

This generalizes the corresponding notions from exchangeability systems
[
Leh04
]
:
given an exchangeability system
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
, we can define a multilinear functional
\varphi_{\pi}
,
this time for any set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
,

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}),

where
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
is any representative such that
\overline{\kappa}(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=\pi
, see Definition
A.10
.

Remark 2.5
.

The algebra
\mathcal{U}
and the homomorphisms
(\iota^{(i)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of a
spreadability system can always be chosen to be the tensor algebra of the
copies of
\mathcal{A}
(see (
2.7
) below) with the natural
embeddings, respectively, in the following sense.
Given a spreadability system
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
for a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
, we set
\hat{\mathcal{U}}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{i}
where
\mathcal{A}_{i}
are copies of
\mathcal{A}
and set
\hat{\iota}^{(i)}\colon\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\hat{\mathcal{U}}
to be the natural embeddings. Then we can define
\hat{\varphi}\colon\hat{\mathcal{U}}\to\mathcal{B}
by setting

\hat{\varphi}(\hat{\iota}^{(i_{1})}(X_{1})\hat{\iota}^{(i_{2})}(X_{2})\cdots\hat{\iota}^{(i_{n})}(X_{n})):=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

for every
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}
with
i_{k}\neq i_{k+1}~(k\in[n-1])
and

X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, giving rise to the spreadability system
\hat{\mathcal{S}}=(\hat{\mathcal{U}},\hat{\varphi},(\hat{\iota}^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. Now
(\hat{\iota}^{(i)})_{i\geq 1}\overset{d}{=}(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1}
and
thus replacing
\mathcal{S}
with
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
does not change basic results; in
particular, the cumulants for
\mathcal{S}
and for
\hat{\mathcal{S}}
are identical, see
Theorem
3.11
.

In the majority of the examples below we choose
\mathcal{U}
to be the tensor algebra,
in a few cases however, it is more natural to choose another
\mathcal{U}
.
A notable exception is the case of unital algebras, where
the unital free product
\ast_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}
and the infinite tensor product

\bigotimes_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}
are more convenient.

2.3.
Examples from universal products of linear functionals

Spreadability systems typically arise as universal products of linear
functionals defined on free products of
\ast
-algebras.
Universal products can be formulated for various categories of noncommutative
probability spaces, e.g., the category of

\ast
-algebras with restricted states (i.e., such that the unital extension to the
unitization is positive) or the category of unital
\ast
-algebras with states.
In the present paper we concentrate
on combinatorial aspects and skip questions about positivity. On the other hand,
recently multiple linear functionals on single algebras turned into focus which
give rise to nontrivial spreadability systems.
We therefore consider the
category
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}
consisting of objects
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
, where

\mathcal{A}
is an algebra and
\varphi=(\varphi^{1},\dots,\varphi^{d})
is a

d
-tuple of
\mathbb{C}
-valued linear functionals on
\mathcal{A}
.
An arrow

f\colon(\mathcal{D},\psi^{1},\dots,\psi^{d})\to(\mathcal{A},\varphi^{1},\dots,\varphi^{d})

in this category is an algebra homomorphism
f\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{A}
such that
\varphi^{i}\circ f=\psi^{i}
for all
i\in[d]
.

A
universal product
is a bifunctor
\odott
on
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}
of the form

\left((\mathcal{A}_{1},\varphi_{1}),(\mathcal{A}_{2},\varphi_{2})\right)\mapsto(\mathcal{A}_{1},\varphi_{1})\odott(\mathcal{A}_{2},\varphi_{2})=(\mathcal{A}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{2},\varphi_{1}\odott\varphi_{2}),

where
\mathcal{A}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{2}
is the coproduct (also called the nonunital
free product) in the category of associative algebras. This means that
\odot
is a binary operation on
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}
such that

(U0)

(\varphi_{1}\odott\varphi_{2})\circ(f_{1}\sqcup f_{2})=(\varphi_{1}\circ f_{1})\odott(\varphi_{2}\circ f_{2})

for any arrows
f_{k}\colon\mathcal{D}_{k}\to\mathcal{A}_{k},k=1,2
, where
f_{1}\sqcup f_{2}
is the canonical arrow
\mathcal{D}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{D}_{2}\to\mathcal{A}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{2}
.
For a universal product
\odott
some of the following conditions are often imposed
[
MS17
]
.

(U1)

Restriction property
:
(\varphi_{1}\odott\varphi_{2})\circ\iota_{k}=\varphi_{k}
for each
k\in\{1,2\}
, where
\iota_{k}
is the canonical embedding
\mathcal{A}_{k}\to\mathcal{A}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

(U2)

Associativity
:
(\varphi_{1}\odott\varphi_{2})\odott\varphi_{3}=\varphi_{1}\odott(\varphi_{2}\odott\varphi_{3})

under the natural isomorphism of
(\mathcal{A}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{2})\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{3}
and
\mathcal{A}_{1}\sqcup(\mathcal{A}_{2}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{3})
.

(U3)

Factorization
on length two (“stochastic
independence”):

(\varphi_{1}\odott\varphi_{2})(ab)=\varphi_{1}(a)\varphi_{2}(b)=(\varphi_{1}\odott\varphi_{2})(ba)
for all
a\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

(U4)

Symmetry
:
\varphi_{1}\odott\varphi_{2}=\varphi_{2}\odott\varphi_{1}
under the natural isomorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{2}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}\sqcup\mathcal{A}_{1}
.

Under condition
(U2)
, the product
\odott_{i\in[p]}\varphi_{i}
can be naturally defined on
\sqcup_{i\in[p]}\mathcal{A}_{i}
for any
p\in\mathbb{N}
.
The following property is satisfied by many examples.

(U5)

Universal coefficients
: there exists a family of complex numbers

\{u^{j}(\sigma,f;\pi):j\in[d],n\in\mathbb{N},\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n},\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\sigma\leq\bar{\pi},f\colon\sigma\to[d]\}

such that for every
p\in\mathbb{N}
,
(\mathcal{A}_{i},\varphi_{i})\in\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}~(i\in[p])
,
n\in\mathbb{N},i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in[p]
and
X_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{k}}~(k\in[n])
, we have for
j\in[d]

\left(\odott_{i\in[p]}\varphi_{i}\right)^{j}(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}\end{subarray}}\sum_{f\colon\,\sigma\to[d]}u^{j}(\sigma,f;\pi)\prod_{S\in\sigma}\varphi_{i(S)}^{f(S)}(X_{S}),

where
\pi:=\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
and
i(S)
is the common value
i_{k}
for
k\in S
(this number is independent of a choice of
k\in S
because
\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}
and
k\mapsto i_{k}
is constant on each block of
\pi
).

In fact, condition
(U5)
for
p=2
only is sufficient: then associativity implies condition
(U5)
for general
p
.

Remark 2.6
.

For
d=1
a binary operation
\odott
satisfying conditions (
U0
)–
(U3)
and
(U5)
is called a
quasi-universal product
in
[
Mur02
]
. (Note that (
U0
) easily follows from
(U5)
).
On the other hand, a binary operation
\odott
satisfying conditions (
U0
)–
(U3)
is called a
natural product

[
Mur03
]
.
The main result of
[
Mur03
]
is that
(U5)
follows from (
U0
)–
(U3)
, i.e., a natural product is a quasi-universal product.

Remark 2.7
.

A more general setup was discussed in

[
MS17
]
, where the algebras are allowed to
have an additional structure of
faces
. The theory of cumulants is also
developed in
[
MS17
]
for universal
products with multistates and multifaces. It seems that some modifications
are needed in order to adapt the notion of
spreadability system to the structure of faces but this issue is not discussed in the present paper.

The universal products with
(U1)
–
(U4)
for
d=1
were classified by Ben Ghorbal and Schürmann into three types: tensor, free, Boolean
[
BGS02
]
. The universal products with
(U1)
–
(U3)
for
d=1
were then classified by Muraki
[
Mur03
]
into five types: monotone and anti-monotone products in addition to the above three.
The anti-monotone product is essentially the reversion of the monotone product and therefore omitted from the discussion below.
With
(U1)
–
(U2)
for
d=1
, more examples arise and according to

[
Lac15
, p. 7]
and
[
GL15
, p. 3]
the classification is not yet complete.
On the other hand, no classification results are known for
d\geq 2
.

Given a universal product with
(U1)
and
(U2)
for
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}

one can construct a
d
-tuple of (
\mathbb{C}
-valued) spreadability systems in the following
way: for a single object
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
of
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}
, take
countably many copies
(\mathcal{A}_{i},\varphi_{i})=(\mathcal{A},\varphi),i\in\mathbb{N}
and
set

(2.6)

(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi}^{j}):=\left(\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{i},\left(\odott_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{i}\right)^{j}\right)

for each

j\in[d]
with the natural embeddings
\iota^{(i)}\colon\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{U},i\in\mathbb{N}
. Note here that the coproduct over
\mathbb{N}
can be represented
as the tensor algebra

(2.7)

\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}\\
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}\text{~for all~}j\in[n-1]\end{subarray}}\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{A}_{i_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{i_{n}}

and
\odott_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{i}
is naturally defined due to
associativity. Condition
(U1)
readily implies that
\varphi_{i}^{j}=\tilde{\varphi}^{j}\circ\iota^{(i)}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
and functoriality of
\odott
yields

(2.8)

\left(\odott_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi_{i}\right)\circ\left(\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}\right)=\odott_{i\in\mathbb{N}}(\varphi_{i}\circ f_{i})

for all arrows
f_{i}\colon\mathcal{D}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}_{i},i\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\sqcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}f_{i}\colon\sqcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{D}_{i}\to\sqcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{A}_{i}
is the canonical arrow. Equation (
2.8
) guarantees the invariance (
2.3
), and therefore
\mathcal{S}_{\odott}^{j}:=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi}^{j},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
is a spreadability system for the ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi^{j})
for every
j\in[d]
. Moreover, it becomes an exchangeability system if
\odott
is symmetric.
The preceding construction works for both unital and non-unital algebras.

Each universal product
\odott
gives rise to a notion of independence.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\odott
be
a universal product for
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}
satisfying conditions
(U1)
and

(U2)
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algebra and
\varphi=(\varphi^{1},\dots,\varphi^{d})
be a tuple of
\mathbb{C}
-linear functionals on
\mathcal{A}
.

(i)

A sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in[p]}
of subalgebras of
\mathcal{A}
is said to be
\odott
-independent
if the identity

\varphi^{j}\circ\tilde{\bigsqcup_{i\in[p]}}\gamma_{i}=\left[\odott_{i\in[p]}(\varphi\circ\gamma_{i})\right]^{j}\quad\text{on}\quad\bigsqcup_{i\in[p]}\mathcal{A}_{i},

holds for all
j\in[d]
, where
\gamma_{i}\colon\mathcal{A}_{i}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{A}
is the embedding for
i\in[p]
and
\tilde{\sqcup}_{i\in[p]}\gamma_{i}
is the canonical arrow
\sqcup_{i\in[p]}\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}
.

If we further assume
(U5)
the above definition is equivalent to the
requirement that for any
n\in\mathbb{N},i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in[p],X_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{k}}~(k\in[n])
we have

(2.9)

\varphi^{j}(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}\end{subarray}}\sum_{f\colon\,\sigma\to[d]}u^{j}(\sigma,f;\pi)\prod_{S\in\sigma}\varphi^{f(S)}(X_{S}),\qquad j\in[d].

(ii)

For an arbitrary totally ordered set
I
,
a family of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
is called
\odott
-independent
if any finite subfamily is
\odott
-independent in the sense above.
If the universal product
\odott
in addition satisfies
(U4)

then one can define
\odott
-independence for a family of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
with
any index set
I
because one can define
\odott_{i\in I}(\varphi\circ\iota^{(i)})
for any index set
I
.

(iii)

A family
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
of subsets of
\mathcal{A}
with totally ordered index set
I
is said to be
\odot
-independent if the family of subalgebras
\mathcal{A}_{i}
generated by
S_{i}
is
\odot
-independent.

After these general considerations we briefly discuss some explicit examples.

2.3.1.
Tensor exchangeability system

Our first example of a universal product is the tensor product
\otimes
of unital algebras and unital linear
functionals.
Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a unital ncps,

\mathcal{U}
the algebraic
infinite tensor product
\mathcal{U}:=\otimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}
and

\tilde{\varphi}:=\otimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\varphi
be the infinite tensor product
of copies of
\varphi
.
Let
\iota^{(j)}
be the embedding of
\mathcal{A}
into the
j
th tensor
component:

\iota^{(j)}(X):=1^{\otimes(j-1)}\otimes X\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes\cdots.

Then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
is an exchangeability system
for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
, which we call the
tensor exchangeability
system
. In order to emphasize that
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}
is a spreadability system, we may write
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{T}}
instead of
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{T}}
and call
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{T}}
the tensor spreadability system.

A family of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
is
\otimes
-independent if for every
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in I
and
X_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{k}},k\in[n]
we have

(2.10)

\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\prod_{P\in\bar{\kappa}(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}\varphi(X_{P}).

In other words, the universal coefficients for the tensor spreadability system are given by
u^{1}(\bar{\pi};\pi)=1
for all
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and
u^{1}(\sigma;\pi)=0
for all
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{\bar{\pi}\}
. Note that we here omit
f
because it is unique.

2.3.2.
Free exchangeability system

The reduced free product of unital linear
functionals is another example of a universal
product.
Let
\mathcal{U}:=\ast_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}
be the unital free
product of infinitely many copies of a unital algebra
\mathcal{A}

and let
\tilde{\varphi}:=\ast_{i=1}^{\infty}\varphi
be the free product
of copies of a unital linear functional
\varphi

[
Avi82
,
Voi85
]
.
Let
\iota^{(i)}
be the embedding of
\mathcal{A}
into the
i
th
component
\mathcal{A}
of
\mathcal{U}
. Then
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{F}}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
(or we may write
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{F}}
when emphasizing the spreadability)
is an exchangeability system for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
, called the
free exchangeability (or spreadability) system
.

2.3.3.
Boolean exchangeability system

The Boolean product

\tilde{\varphi}=\diamond_{i=1}^{\infty}\varphi
is defined on the
nonunital free product (i.e. the tensor algebra)

\mathcal{U}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}
by the following rule
[
Boż86
]
: if
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and

i_{k}\neq i_{k+1}
for any
1\leq k\leq n-1
, then

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\varphi(X_{1})\,\varphi(X_{2})\cdots\varphi(X_{n}).

As before,
\iota^{(j)}
is the
embedding of
\mathcal{A}
into the
j
th component
\mathcal{A}
of

\mathcal{U}
.
The triplet
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{B}}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
(or we may write
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{B}}
) is called the
Boolean exchangeability (or spreadability) system
.

2.3.4.
Monotone spreadability system

Let
(\mathcal{U},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
be as in Section
2.3.3
.
The monotone product

\tilde{\varphi}=\triangleright_{i=1}^{\infty}\varphi
is defined on

\mathcal{U}
by the following recursive rules
[
Mur00
]
: for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
,

(i)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})})=\varphi(X_{1})
;

(ii)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\varphi(X_{1})\,\tilde{\varphi}(X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

if

n\geq 2
and
i_{1}>i_{2}
;

(iii)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n-1}^{(i_{n-1})})\,\varphi(X_{n})

if

n\geq 2
and
i_{n}>i_{n-1}
;

(iv)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{j-1}^{(i_{j-1})}X_{j+1}^{(i_{j+1})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})\,\varphi(X_{j})

if
n\geq 3
,
2\leq j\leq n-1
and
i_{j-1}<i_{j}>i_{j+1}
.

Then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{M}}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
is called the
monotone spreadability system
for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.
It is a proper spreadability system, i.e., it does not satisfy exchangeability.

2.3.5.
Conditionally monotone spreadability system

Let
(\mathcal{U},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
be as in Section
2.3.3
.
The conditionally monotone product is an associative universal product for
d=2
.
The infinite conditionally monotone product
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},\tilde{\psi})=\triangleright_{i=1}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\varphi,\psi)

is defined as follows
[
Has11
]
.

•

\tilde{\psi}=\triangleright_{i=1}^{\infty}\psi
is the monotone product of
\psi
according to Section
2.3.4
;

•

\tilde{\varphi}
is determined by rules

(i)
–
(iii)
as in the monotone case from
Section
2.3.4

but with the last rule modified into

(iv’)

\begin{multlined}\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})\\
=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{j-1}^{(i_{j-1})})\,(\varphi(X_{j})-\psi(X_{j}))\,\tilde{\varphi}(X_{j+1}^{(i_{j+1})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})\\
+\psi(X_{j})\,\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{1})}\cdots X_{j-1}^{(i_{j-1})}X_{j+1}^{(i_{j+1})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})\end{multlined}

if
n\geq 3
,

2\leq j\leq n-1
and
i_{j-1}<i_{j}>i_{j+1}
.

Then
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{CM}}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
is a
spreadability system for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)

which does not satisfy exchangeability.
It is called the
c-monotone spreadability system
.

Remark 2.9
.

More examples may be extracted from associative universal products in
[
BLS96
,
Has10
]
for
d=2
or
d=3
, but we omit them here.

2.3.6.
V
-monotone spreadability system

Recently Dacko introduced
the concept of
V
-monotone
independence and constructed a corresponding
V
-monotone product of
probability spaces
[
Dac20
]
.
These notions are based on the notion of
V
-shaped sequences and partitions.
A sequence of numbers
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}
is called
V
-shaped

if there exists an index
1\leq r\leq n
such that

i_{1}>i_{2}>\dots>i_{r}<i_{r+1}<\dots<i_{n}
.
Given a
unital

\mathbb{C}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
, the
V
-monotone product
\tilde{\varphi}=\ovee_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\varphi
is
defined on the
nonunital
free product
\mathcal{U}:=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}

and characterized by the following factorization properties. Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
denote the embedded image
\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
of
\mathcal{A}
with unit denoted by

I_{i}
and
\varphi_{i}
be the induced linear functional
\varphi\circ(\iota^{(i)})^{-1}
on
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
Y_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{j}}
,
j=1,2,\dots,n
be arbitrary elements.

(i)

\tilde{\varphi}(Y_{1}Y_{2}\dotsm Y_{n})=0
whenever
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
for all
j

and
\varphi_{i_{j}}(Y_{j})=0
.

(ii)

In addition, for every
j\in[n]
,

\tilde{\varphi}(Y_{1}Y_{2}\dotsm Y_{j-1}I_{i_{j}}Y_{j+1}\dotsm Y_{n})=\begin{cases}\tilde{\varphi}(Y_{1}Y_{2}\dotsm Y_{j-1}Y_{j+1}\dotsm Y_{n})&\text{if $(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{j})$
is $V$-shaped,}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

whenever
\varphi_{i_{1}}(Y_{1})=\varphi_{i_{2}}(Y_{2})=\dots=\varphi_{i_{j-1}}(Y_{j-1})=0
.

It is shown in
[
Dac20
]
that
associativity does not hold; yet identity (
2.8
) holds
and the
V
-monotone product gives rise to a spreadability system.

2.4.
Spreadability systems with calculation rules

Motivated by Axiom
(U5)
in the previous subsection, we are lead to the following class of spreadability systems which provides various examples.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be
a spreadability system for a
\mathbb{C}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. For a set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we define a multiplicative extension

\varphi_{(\pi)}
of
\varphi
by setting

(2.11)

\displaystyle\varphi_{(\pi)}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}):=\prod_{P\in\pi}\varphi(X_{P})\quad\text{for}\quad X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A},

where we used notation (
2.1
) for the ordered product
X_{P}
.

Then
\mathcal{S}
is said to have a
calculation rule
if there exists a family of complex numbers

\mathbf{C}=\{s(\sigma;\pi)\in\mathbb{C}:n\in\mathbb{N},\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n},\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}\}
such that the equality

(2.12)

\varphi_{\pi}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\\
\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}\end{subarray}}s(\sigma;\pi)\,\varphi_{(\sigma)}

holds as functionals on
\mathcal{A}^{n}
for all
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Note that we can always take
s(\{1\};(1))=1
because of
\tilde{\varphi}\circ\iota^{(i)}=\varphi
.

Remark 2.11
.

1.

It is straightforward to see that a spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\odot}^{1}

constructed from a universal product
\odot
on
\operatorname{AlgP}_{1}
subject to axioms

(U1)
,
(U2)
and
(U5)
has a calculation rule with
s(\sigma;\pi):=u^{1}(\sigma;\pi)
(where
f
is omitted because there is a unique

f\colon\sigma\to[1]
).

2.

Actually we can construct such a spreadability system for any given family of constants
\mathbf{C}=\{s(\sigma;\pi)\in\mathbb{C}:n\in\mathbb{N},\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n},\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}\}
with
s(\{1\};(1))=1
and any given
\mathbb{C}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. Let
\mathcal{U}
be the coproduct (i.e., the tensor algebra) of the
countable copies of
\mathcal{A}
in the category of associative algebras and

\iota^{(i)}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{U}
the natural embedding as the
i
th component. Then we can define a linear functional
\tilde{\varphi}
on
\mathcal{U}
as follows: for each tuple
X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}~(1\leq j\leq n)
and indices
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
with
i_{k}\neq i_{k+1}
(
k\in[n-1]
) we set

(2.13)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\\
\sigma\leq\bar{\kappa}(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\end{subarray}}s(\sigma;\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n}))\,\varphi_{(\sigma)}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}).

The fact that the value of (
2.13
) depends only on the partition
\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
induced by the sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
yields that
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{C}}:=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
is indeed a spreadability system.

3.

Muraki’s example
[
Mur13
]
yields a spreadability system with
calculation rule coming from a nonassociative universal product.
The V-monotone spreadability system is also such an example. We will investigate other instructive examples later in Examples
2.19
and
3.27
.

4.

It is worth mentioning that there are spreadability
systems without calculation rules. For instance, the algebra generated by left and right creation operators acting
on
q
-Fock space gives rise to an exchangeability system

[
Leh05
]
, but it was shown in
[
vLM96
]

that in this case the individual distributions of independent elements with respect to the vacuum expectation
is not sufficient to determine the joint distribution
with respect to
(\varphi_{\pi})_{\pi}
,
and in particular there is no “
q
-convolution”. The c-monotone spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\rm CM}
also does not have a calculation rule since the evaluation of
\varphi_{\pi}
depends on both
\varphi
and
\psi
in general.

2.5.
\mathcal{S}
-independence

Let us first recall the notion of independence associated to exchangeability systems
[
Leh04
, Definition 1.8]
.
Roughly speaking independence of a pair
(X,Y)
means that
the joint distribution of
(X,Y)
coincides with the joint distribution of
(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)})
, where the couples

(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)})
and
(X^{(2)},Y^{(2)})
are exchangeable copies of the couple
(X,Y)
.
This property can be reformulated in a lattice theoretical way as follows.

Definition 2.12

(
\mathcal{E}
-independence)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be an exchangeability system for a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.

(i)

Subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
are said to be

\mathcal{E}
-independent
if for any tuple of indices
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
, any tuple of random variables
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
with
X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{j}}
and any set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we have

(2.14)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi\wedge\bar{\kappa}(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}).

(ii)

Subsets
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
are said to be

\mathcal{E}
-independent
if the algebras
\mathcal{A}_{i}
generated by

S_{i}
are

\mathcal{E}
-independent. In particular, a family

\bigl((X_{1}(i),X_{2}(i),\dots,X_{n}(i))\bigr)_{i\in I}
of random vectors
is said to be

\mathcal{E}
-independent
if the subalgebras
\mathcal{A}_{i}

generated by its respective entries
\{X_{1}(i),X_{2}(i),\dots,X_{n}(i)\}

are
\mathcal{E}
-independent.

Remark 2.13
.

In the case where
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{U}
and
\varphi,\iota^{(i)}
are unital,
it is more natural to assume that the subalgebras
\mathcal{A}_{i}
contain the unit
of
\mathcal{A}
.
The same applies to Definition
2.14
.

In order to generalize independence from exchangeability systems to spreadability systems
we replace set partitions by ordered set partitions.
This time independence of an ordered pair
(X,Y)
means that
the joint distribution of
(X,Y)
coincides with the joint distribution of

(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)})

(but not necessarily
(X^{(2)},Y^{(1)})
),
where the couples

(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)})
and
(X^{(2)},Y^{(2)})
are spreaded copies of the couple
(X,Y)
.

Definition 2.14

(
\mathcal{S}
-independence)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a spreadability system for a given

\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. Let
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.

(i)

A sequence of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
is said to be

\mathcal{S}
-independent
if for any tuple of indices

(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
,
any tuple of random variables
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

with
X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{j}}

and any ordered set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, we have

(2.15)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}).

(ii)

A sequence of subsets
(S_{i})_{i\in I}
is said to be

\mathcal{S}
-independent
if the sequence of subalgebras
\mathcal{A}_{i}
generated by
S_{i}
is
\mathcal{S}
-independent.
In particular, a sequence of random vectors
((X_{1}(i),X_{2}(i),\dots,X_{n}(i)))_{i\in I}
is said to be
\mathcal{S}
-independent
if the sequence of subalgebras
\mathcal{A}_{i}
generated by
\{X_{1}(i),X_{2}(i),\dots,X_{n}(i)\}
is
\mathcal{S}
-independent.

Example 2.15
.

For two subalgebras
\mathcal{S}
-independence reads as follows.
A pair of subalgebras

(\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2})
of
\mathcal{A}
is

\mathcal{S}
-independent if the following condition holds.
Given elements
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\cup\mathcal{A}_{2}
,
let
\rho=(B_{1},B_{2})
be an ordered set partition of the index set
[n]
such that

X_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
for
i\in B_{1}
and
X_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{2}
for
i\in B_{2}
.
Then for any ordered set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
we have

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi\rfloor_{B_{1}}\pi\rfloor_{B_{2}}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}).

Remark 2.16
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be an
exchangeability system for a given
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. It can be
regarded as a spreadability system and we denote it by
\mathcal{S}
. Then, obviously, a sequence of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathcal{E}
-independent in the sense of Definition
2.12
if and only if it is
\mathcal{S}
-independent in the sense of Definition
2.14
.

Remark 2.17
.

1.

With the notation introduced in Definition
3.3
below,
equation (
2.15
) may be rewritten as

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})}).

This condition means that the random vectors
(X_{k})_{k\in[n]}
and

(X_{k}^{(i_{k})})_{k\in[n]}
have the same “distribution” with respect to

(\varphi_{\pi})_{\pi}
. This is compatible with the concept of spreadability,
which is to regard the sequence
(\iota^{(j)}(\mathcal{A}))_{j\in I}
as
independent copies
\mathcal{A}
constructed in
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi})
.

2.

An alternative natural definition of
\mathcal{S}
-independence would be to require the simplified condition that for any
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
and any

X_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{j}},j\in[n]
,

(2.16)

\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}),

i.e., requiring (
2.15
) only for
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
.
In fact, for the spreadability systems associated with a large class of
universal products,
(
2.16
) implies the
\mathcal{S}
-independence, see Proposition

2.18
.
In general however, condition (
2.16
) does not imply
\mathcal{S}
-independence, see Example
2.19
.

3.

In the case of exchangeability,
\mathcal{E}
-independence is equivalent to the
vanishing of mixed cumulants (see Proposition
5.1
).

The following result shows that the definitions of
\mathcal{E}
- and
\mathcal{S}
-independence coincide with the usual definitions for typical examples, e.g. tensor, free, Boolean, monotone, c-free and c-monotone independences. Associativity is crucial.

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\odott
be a universal product in the category
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}
satisfying
(U1)
,
(U2)
and
(U5)
. Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi^{1},\varphi^{2},\dots,\varphi^{d})
be an object in
\operatorname{AlgP}_{d}
and
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of subalgebras of
\mathcal{A}
with
I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
. Then
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
is
\odot
-independent if and only if it is
\mathcal{S}_{\odott}^{j}
-independent for all
j\in[d]
, where

\mathcal{S}_{\odott}^{j}
is the spreadability system for the
\mathbb{C}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi^{j})
constructed in Section
2.3
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
is
\odot
-independent. This means that the identity

(2.17)

\varphi_{\pi}^{j}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}^{j}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

holds for
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
, any
j\in[d]
, any tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
and any tuple of random variables
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

with
X_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{k}}
, cf. (
2.9
). The goal is to verify (
2.17
) for any ordered set partition
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.

Let us denote
\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=(Q_{1},Q_{2},\dots,Q_{q})
. Then
\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=(P_{1}\cap Q_{1},P_{1}\cap Q_{2},\dots,P_{1}\cap Q_{q},P_{2}\cap Q_{1},\dots,P_{p}\cap Q_{q})
. The RHS of (
2.17
) is exactly the value
\varphi^{j}(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})
when

(\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{1}\cap Q_{1}},\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{1}\cap Q_{2}},\dots,\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{1}\cap Q_{q}},\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{2}\cap Q_{1}},\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{2}\cap Q_{2}},\dots)

is assumed to be
\odot
-independent. The associativity of
\odot
allows us to compute the RHS of (
2.17
) in the following two steps:

(a)

First compute
\varphi^{j}(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})
assuming that
(\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{1}},\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{2}},\dots,\{X_{i}\}_{i\in P_{p}})
is
\odot
-independent. The result is exactly the RHS of (
2.9
).

(b)

Then compute each factor
\varphi^{f(S)}(X_{S})
by additionally assuming that

(\{X_{i}\}_{i\in S\cap Q_{1}},\{X_{i}\}_{i\in S\cap Q_{2}},\dots,\{X_{i}\}_{i\in S\cap Q_{q}})

is
\odot
-independent.

This is exactly how the LHS of (
2.17
) is computed, so that (
2.17
) holds as desired.

Conversely, suppose that
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
is
\mathcal{S}_{\odott}^{j}
-independent for all
j\in[d]
. Formula (
2.17
) for
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
is exactly the desired independence relation (
2.9
).
∎

Example 2.19
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a
\mathbb{C}
-ncps and let
\mathcal{U}:=\sqcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}
be the coproduct in the category of algebras (see (
2.7
)) and
\iota^{(i)}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{U}
be the natural embedding as
i^{\rm th}
component. We define a linear functional
\tilde{\varphi}
on
\mathcal{U}
by, for each
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
with
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
for all
j=1,2,\dots,n-1
, setting

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\begin{cases}\varphi(X_{1})&\text{~if $n=1$},\\
\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2})&\text{~if $n=2$},\\
0&\text{~if $n\geq 3$}.\end{cases}

Then we get an exchangeability system
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}})
for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
with calculation rule.

For
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
it is easy to see that

(2.18)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\begin{cases}\varphi_{(\pi)}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})&\text{~if~$\pi\in\mathcal{I}_{n}$ with $|\pi|\leq 2$,}\\
0&\text{~otherwise}.\end{cases}

Suppose that subalgebras
\mathcal{B}_{1},\mathcal{B}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
are
\mathcal{E}
-independent. Then for
X\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
and
Y\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
we have

\varphi_{\pi}(X,Y)=\varphi_{\pi\wedge\bar{\kappa}(1,2)}(X,Y)

and the case
\pi=\hat{1}_{2}
yields
\varphi(XY)=\varphi(X)\varphi(Y)
.
Moreover, independence implies

\varphi_{\pi}(X,Y,X)=\varphi_{\pi\wedge\bar{\kappa}(1,2,1)}(X,Y,X).

On the other hand,
we infer from (
2.18
) that
for
\pi=\{\{1,2\},\{3\}\}
the left hand side equals
\varphi(XY)\varphi(X)=\varphi(X)^{2}\varphi(Y)
, while the RHS equals zero.
This is only possible if either
\varphi\rfloor_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}=0
or

\varphi\rfloor_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}=0
holds,
so that only the trivial examples satisfy
\mathcal{E}
-independence. However, nontrivial subalgebras
\{\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}\}
satisfying condition (
2.16
) exist because (
2.16
) simply reads: if
X_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{i_{j}}
then for
\pi:=\bar{\kappa}(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})

(2.19)

\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})=\begin{cases}\varphi_{(\pi)}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})&\text{~if~$\pi\in\mathcal{I}_{n}$ with $|\pi|\leq 2$,}\\
0&\text{~otherwise}.\end{cases}

For example we can start from any
\mathbb{C}
-ncps
(\mathcal{C}_{i},\varphi_{i}),i=1,2
and then construct
\varphi
on
\mathcal{A}:=\mathcal{C}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{C}_{2}
so that (
2.19
) holds.

3.
Cumulants for spreadability systems

3.1.
Definition and uniqueness of cumulants

Cumulants provide a powerful tool to describe independence of random
variables.
Let us first recall the case of exchangeability systems.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be an
exchangeability system for a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. Cumulants are functionals satisfying the
the following requirements.

(E1)

Multilinearity
: Cumulants are multilinear functionals
K_{\pi}\colon\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}
, indexed
by set partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(E2)

Universality
:
There are universal coefficients
c(\sigma,\pi)\in\mathbb{C}
such that

(3.1)

\varphi_{\pi}=K_{\pi}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma<\pi\end{subarray}}c(\sigma,\pi)K_{\sigma}.

(E3)

Vanishing of mixed cumulants
:
given a family
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n}
and
a partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
such that some block of
P\in\pi
can be partitioned

P=P^{\prime}\dot{\cup}P^{\prime\prime}
nontrivially in such a way that

\{X_{i}\mid i\in P^{\prime}\}
and
\{X_{i}\mid i\in P^{\prime\prime}\}
are
\mathcal{E}
-independent,
then

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=0.

Equivalently, if
\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
partitions the family
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

into
mutually
\mathcal{E}
-independent subfamilies,
then
K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=0
unless
\pi\leq\rho
.

Remark 3.2
.

(i)

Additivity
.
As a consequence of

(E1)

and

(E3)

cumulants of sums of independent random variables are additive:
Denote by
K_{n}
the cumulant functional
K_{\hat{1}_{n}}
, then

(3.2)

K_{n}(X_{1}+Y_{1},X_{2}+Y_{2},\dots,X_{n}+Y_{n})=K_{n}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})+K_{n}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}).

(ii)

The system of equations (
3.1
) is in triangular form and
can be solved recursively and transformed into the equivalent system

K_{\pi}=\varphi_{\pi}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma<\pi\end{subarray}}\tilde{c}(\sigma,\pi)\varphi_{\sigma}

where the matrix
\tilde{c}(\sigma,\pi)
is inverse to
c(\sigma,\pi)
.

In
[
Leh04
]
the second named author established
a unified theory of cumulants for exchangeability systems
based on a kind of finite Fourier transform,
known as Good’s formula in the mathematics literature
[
Goo75
]

and Cartier’s formula for the so-called
Ursell functions
in the physics literature

[
Per75
,
Sim93
]
.
This approach apparently fails in the present, non-exchangeable setting;
however in their study of monotone cumulants
[
HS11a
,
HS11b
]

the first named author and Saigo found a good replacement in Rota’s dot operation
from umbral calculus
[
RT94
]
,
i.e., a weak version of (
3.2
),
which we take as a starting point for the definition of cumulants in full
generality in Definition
3.5
below.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a spreadability system for a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.

(i)

Given a noncommutative random variable
X\in\mathcal{A}

and a finite subset
A\subseteq\mathbb{N}
we define

\delta_{A}(X)=\sum_{i\in A}X^{(i)}

i.e., the sum of i.i.d. copies of
X
.
In the case
A=[N]
we will also write
\delta_{N}(X)
and frequently
abbreviate it using Rota’s
dot operation

N.X:=X^{(1)}+X^{(2)}+\cdots+X^{(N)}

whenever it is convenient.

(ii)

We extend the partitioned functionals
\varphi_{\pi}
to
\bigcup\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
by setting

(3.3)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\ldots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})}):=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}),

and

(3.4)

\varphi_{\pi}(N_{1}.X_{1},N_{2}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{n}.X_{n}):=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{N_{1}}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{N_{2}}\dots\sum_{i_{n}=1}^{N_{n}}\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\ldots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})}).

Remark 3.4
.

Note that (
3.3
) is actually an abuse of notation,
because
\varphi_{\pi}
is defined for elements of
\mathcal{A}
only
(see (
2.5
));
here we pretend that

\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty},\tilde{\varphi})

can be interpreted as a spreadability system for the algebra

\mathcal{A}^{(1,2,\dots,N)}
generated by the images

\mathcal{A}^{(1)},\mathcal{A}^{(2)},\dots,\mathcal{A}^{(N)}
.
This is true in the case of universal product construction in (
2.6
)
but needs justification otherwise;
yet (
3.3
) is well-defined
and convenient to keep notation manageable.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps and
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
a
spreadability system for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.

Cumulants
are multilinear functionals
K_{\pi}
indexed
by ordered set partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
which satisfy the following axioms.

(S1)

Multilinearity
: Cumulants are multilinear functionals
K_{\pi}\colon\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathcal{B}
, indexed
by ordered set partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(S2)

Universality
:
There are universal coefficients
c(\sigma,\pi)\in\mathbb{C}
such that
for every
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}

\varphi_{\pi}=K_{\pi}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma<\pi\end{subarray}}c(\sigma,\pi)K_{\sigma}

or, equivalently,
there are universal coefficients
\tilde{c}(\sigma,\pi)\in\mathbb{C}
such that

(3.5)

K_{\pi}=\varphi_{\pi}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma<\pi\end{subarray}}\tilde{c}(\sigma,\pi)\varphi_{\sigma}.

(S3)

Extensivity
:
given a family
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n}
and
an ordered partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,

(3.6)

K_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\ldots,N.X_{n})=N^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}),

where
the extension of
K_{\pi}
to arguments from
\bigcup\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
is defined via formula
(
3.4
)
and
(
3.5
)
as

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\ldots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\tilde{c}(\sigma,\pi)\,\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\ldots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\tilde{c}(\sigma,\pi)\,\varphi_{\sigma\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})

and thus

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(N_{1}.X_{1},N_{2}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{n}.X_{n})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\tilde{c}(\sigma,\pi)\,\varphi_{\sigma}(N_{1}.X_{1},N_{2}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{n}.X_{n}),

where the diagonal coefficients are
\tilde{c}(\pi,\pi)=1
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Cumulants are uniquely determined by axioms
(S1)
–
(S3)
from
Definition
3.5
.

Proof.

Assume that there are two sets of cumulants
K_{\pi}
and
K^{\prime}_{\pi}
satisfying
axioms
(S1)
–
(S3)
with universal coefficients
c(\sigma,\pi)
and

c^{\prime}(\sigma,\pi)
, respectively. Then the axioms imply that

\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\ldots,N.X_{n})\\
\begin{aligned} &=N^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})+\sum_{\sigma<\pi}N^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert}K_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,c(\sigma,\pi)\\
&=N^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}K^{\prime}_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})+\sum_{\sigma<\pi}N^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert}K^{\prime}_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,c^{\prime}(\sigma,\pi)\end{aligned}

for any
N\in\mathbb{N}

and the coefficients of the leading term
N^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
must coincide.
∎

3.2.
Construction of Cumulants via factorial Möbius and zeta functions

In this subsection, we assume that
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
is a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps and
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
is a
spreadability system for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.

Spreadability implies that the value
(
3.3
)
is invariant under order preserving changes of the indices;
however, for partitioned expectations invariance holds under the weaker
assumption that the relative order of indices is preserved on every individual block.
We will only be concerned with the following particular case and therefore
refrain from formulating this fact in full generality.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
be an ordered set partition.
Fix a block
P
of
\pi
and a number
m\in\mathbb{N}

and let
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
,
(i^{\prime}_{1},i^{\prime}_{2},\dots,i^{\prime}_{n})
be
n
-tuples
such that

i^{\prime}_{k}=\begin{cases}i_{k}+m&\text{if $k\in P$}\\
i_{k}&\text{if $k\not\in P$}\end{cases}

Then

\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i^{\prime}_{1},i^{\prime}_{2},\dots,i^{\prime}_{n})
.

Remark 3.8
.

Note that without performing the quasi-meet operation in the lemma above
the kernel partitions

\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})

and
\kappa(i^{\prime}_{1},i^{\prime}_{2},\dots,i^{\prime}_{n})

may well be nontrivial permutations of each other or even

\bar{\kappa}(i_{1}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime},\dots,i_{n}^{\prime})\neq\bar{\kappa}(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
.

Theorem 3.9
.

For any ordered set partitions
\rho\leq\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and numbers

N_{1},N_{2},\ldots\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(3.7)

\varphi_{\rho}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})=\sum_{\sigma\leq\rho}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\gamma_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi),

with
\gamma_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)
as in Definition
A.28
and
\pi(i)
as in Definition
A.8
(ii)
.
Specializing to the case
\rho=\pi
yields

(3.8)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pi}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\beta_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\pi)

and this is a polynomial in
N_{1},\dots,N_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
without constant term.
Furthermore, specializing to the case
N:=N_{1}=N_{2}=\cdots
entails

\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\ldots,N.X_{n})=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\beta_{N}(\sigma,\pi),

which is a polynomial in
N
consisting of monomials of degree at least
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert
.

Proof.

We begin with (
3.8
) and then verify the general case (
3.7
). The proof boils down to the enumeration of the set

S_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\pi):=\{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})\in[N_{\pi(1)}]\times[N_{\pi(2)}]\times\cdots\times[N_{\pi(n)}]:\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})=\sigma\}.

Pick an arbitrary block

P_{i}=\{p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{k}\}
of
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots)
. There are
\binom{N_{i}}{\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}})}
possible
ways to choose a tuple
(i_{p_{1}},i_{p_{2}},\ldots,i_{p_{k}})
such that

\kappa(i_{p_{1}},i_{p_{2}},\ldots,i_{p_{k}})

defines the partition
\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}}
:
for each block of
\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}}
we have to choose
a distinct label from
[N_{i}]
respecting the order prescribed by the
labels of the blocks of
\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}}
.
That is, we have to choose a subset of
[N_{i}]
of cardinality

\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}})
.
This can be done for every block of
\pi
independently
and thus

(3.9)

\#S_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}\binom{N_{i}}{\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}})}=\beta_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\pi).

If
N_{i}<\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}})
for some
i
, then
\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
can never be equal to
\sigma
, and so
the cardinality of
S_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\pi)
is 0, in accordance
with the generally adopted convention that the generalized
binomial coefficient
\binom{N}{k}
is zero when
N<k
.

The general formula (
3.7
) requires the enumeration of the set

S_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)=\{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})\in[N_{\pi(1)}]\times[N_{\pi(2)}]\times\cdots\times[N_{\pi(n)}]:\rho\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})=\sigma\}.

This is similar to
S_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho)
; the difference is that the blocks of
\rho
contained in a block
P_{i}\in\pi
are endowed with the common number
N_{i}
. This results in the product formula

\#S_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}S_{N_{i}}(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}},\rho\rfloor_{P_{i}}),

which equals
\gamma_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)
from Remark
A.29
(iii)
.

∎

Definition 3.10
.

Given an ordered set partition
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{k})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}

with
k
blocks,
we define the
partitioned cumulant

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
to be the coefficient of

N_{1}N_{2}\dotsm N_{k}
in the polynomial
(
3.8
).

The next theorem shows that this definition
is a natural generalization of
[
Leh04
, Definition 2.6]
, cf. Corollary
3.18
below.

Theorem 3.11

(Moment-cumulant formulas)
.

For any
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, we have

(3.10)

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi),

(3.11)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}K_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\widetilde{\zeta}(\sigma,\pi)

with
\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{\zeta}
as in Definition
A.28
.

Proof.

For
0<k<N
,
there is no constant term in
\binom{N}{k}=\frac{N(N-1)\cdots(N-k+1)}{k!}

(regarded as a polynomial in
N
), and the coefficient of its linear term is

\frac{(-1)(-2)\cdots(-k+1)}{k!}=\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}
.
Therefore the coefficient of
N_{1}\cdots N_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
in
\beta_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}\binom{N_{i}}{\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}})}
is equal to
\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert-\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert}}{[\sigma:\pi]}
. Note that
\sum_{P\in\pi}\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P})=\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert
.
Comparing with (
A.3
) the claimed formula (
3.10
) follows.
The same argument holds true if we look at the monomial
N^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}

when
N_{1}=N_{2}=\cdots=N
.

The inverse formula (
3.11
) expressing moments in terms of
cumulants
is an immediate consequence of the fact that the modified Möbius function

\widetilde{\mu}

is the inverse of the modified zeta function
\widetilde{\zeta}

(Corollary
A.32
).

∎

3.3.
Verification of cumulant axioms

In this subsection, we keep the assumption that
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
is a

\mathcal{B}
-ncps and
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
is a
spreadability system for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.

Theorem 3.12
.

The cumulants defined in Definition
3.10
verify the axioms
(S1)
–
(S3)
from
Definition
3.5
.

Axiom
(S1)
– multilinearity and
(S2)
– universality are immediate
consequences of the relations (
3.10
)
and (
3.11
). It remains to show axiom
(S3)
– extensivity, which is proven in Proposition
3.16
in a slightly generalized form.
For this, we iterate the construction (
3.4
).

Definition 3.13
.

For
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and
M_{i},N_{i}\in\mathbb{N},i=1,2,\dots,n
, we define

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{1}.(N_{1}.X_{1}),M_{2}.(N_{2}.X_{2}),\ldots,M_{n}.(N_{n}.X_{n}))\\
:=\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})\in[M_{1}]\times[M_{2}]\times\cdots\times[M_{n}]}\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})}(N_{1}.X_{1},N_{2}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{n}.X_{n}).

Remark 3.14
.

We would like to alert the reader that the formal definition of the expectation

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{1}.(N_{1}.X_{1}),M_{2}.(N_{2}.X_{2}),\ldots,M_{n}.(N_{n}.X_{n}))

is not necessarily related
to “
M.(N.X)
” (which formally would represent the element

(N.X)^{(1)}+(N.X)^{(2)}+\cdots+(N.X)^{(M)},

i.e.,
“the sum of i.i.d. copies of
N.X
”)
as an element of an enlarged space,
obtained, e.g., by iterating a product construction.
Yet we will show below that the vector

(M_{\pi(1)}N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})
and
the virtual vector “
(M_{\pi(1)}.(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1}),\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}.(N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}))
” have the same distribution with respect to
\varphi_{\pi}
.
This property implies extensivity of cumulants, see Proposition
3.16
below.
It is a consequence of the associativity of the corresponding universal product in the case of classical, free, monotone and Boolean independence
[
HS11a
]
.
For general spreadability systems it holds on a formal level
(Lemma
3.15
), even when it comes from a nonassociative
universal product, like the example of V-monotone independence from
Section
2.3.6
.

Lemma 3.15
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
X_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
M_{i},N_{i}\in\mathbb{N},i=1,2,\dots,\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert
.

(i)

The value

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}.(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1}),\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}.(N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}))
is
a polynomial in the variables
M_{i},N_{i},i=1,2,\dots,\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert
, and
