K_{\pi}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})
is the coefficient of
M_{1}\cdots M_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
.

(ii)

The dot operation gives rise to an action of the
multiplicative semigroup
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})\\
=\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}.(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1}),\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}.(N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})).

Proof.

(i)
Let
\underline{M}:=(M_{1},\dots,M_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert},0,0,\dots)\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
and similarly
\underline{N}\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
.
Then the following expansion holds:

(3.12)

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}.(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1}),\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}.(N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}))\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in[M_{\pi(1)}]\times\cdots\times[M_{\pi(n)}]}\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in[M_{\pi(1)}]\times\cdots\times[M_{\pi(n)}],\\
\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})=\sigma\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\sigma}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\varphi_{\sigma}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})\,\beta_{\underline{M}}(\sigma,\pi),\end{aligned}

where we used the identity (
3.9
). Hence
\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}.(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1}),\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}.(N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}))
is a polynomial in
M_{i},N_{i},i=1,2,\dots,\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert
,
and from the proof of Theorem
3.11

we infer that the coefficient of
M_{1}\cdots M_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
is equal to
K_{\pi}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})
.

(ii)
We proceed with the computation of (
3.12
):

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}.(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1}),M_{\pi(2)}.(N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2}),\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}.(N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}))\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{\rho\leq\pi}\varphi_{\rho}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})\,\beta_{\underline{M}}(\rho,\pi)\\
&=\sum_{\rho\leq\pi}\sum_{\sigma\leq\rho}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\gamma_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)\,\beta_{\underline{M}}(\rho,\pi)\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\rho\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\gamma_{\underline{N}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)\,\beta_{\underline{M}}(\rho,\pi)\biggr)\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})(\gamma_{\underline{N}}\otriangle\beta_{\underline{M}})(\sigma,\pi)\\
&=\sum_{\sigma\leq\pi}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\beta_{\underline{M}\circ\underline{N}}(\sigma,\pi)\\
&=\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},M_{\pi(2)}N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2},\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}),\end{aligned}

where (
3.7
) was used in the second equality and Corollary
A.32
was used in the next to last line.
∎

Proposition 3.16
.

The cumulants from
Definition
3.10
satisfy extensivity (
3.6
) and more generally,

K_{\pi}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2},\ldots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})=N_{1}N_{2}\cdots N_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}).

Proof.

By definition,
\varphi_{\pi}
applied to the same arguments has the expansion

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},M_{\pi(2)}N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2},\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})\\
=\Biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}M_{i}N_{i}\Biggr)K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})+(\text{sum of monomials in $M_{i}N_{i}$ of higher degrees}).

On the other hand, from Lemma
3.15
(i)
we infer

\varphi_{\pi}(M_{\pi(1)}.(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1}),M_{\pi(2)}.(N_{\pi(2)}.X_{2}),\ldots,M_{\pi(n)}.(N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}))\\
=M_{1}M_{2}\cdots M_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}K_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\ldots,N.X_{n})\\
+(\text{sum of monomials in $M_{i}$ of higher degree}).

We have thus computed the expectation in two ways and
it follows from Lemma
3.15
(ii)
that the coefficients of
M_{1}\cdots M_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
coincide.
∎

3.4.
Examples

Let us now briefly review instances of moment-cumulant formulas
coming from noncommutative notions of independence in the light
of Theorem
3.11
. Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps and
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a
spreadability system for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.

3.4.1.
Cumulants for exchangeability systems

Let start with the verification that the definition of cumulants for spreadability
systems is consistent with the previous definition for exchangeability systems
from
[
Leh04
]
.

Proposition 3.17
.

If a spreadability system
\mathcal{S}
satisfies exchangeability,
then both the partitioned expectations and cumulants are invariant under permutations of the
blocks.
More precisely,
for any partition
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{k})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and permutation
h\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,

\varphi_{\pi}=\varphi_{h(\pi)}
and

K_{\pi}=K_{h(\pi)}
, where
h(\pi)=(P_{h(1)},P_{h(2)},\dots,P_{h(k)})
,
Thus
\varphi_{\pi}
and
K_{\pi}
are completely determined by the underlying (unordered) set partition
\bar{\pi}
.

Proof.

Invariance of
\varphi_{\pi}
under permutations of the blocks of
\pi
is an immediate consequence of
exchangeability and this invariance extends to the extension (
3.3
).
Indeed let
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{k})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
,
then for any
h\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
there exists a permutation
g\in\mathfrak{S}_{\infty}

such that

h(\pi)\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=g(\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}))
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\varphi_{h(\pi)}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{h(\pi)\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{g(\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}))}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})}).

Consequently also
\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\dots,N.X_{n})=\varphi_{h(\pi)}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\dots,N.X_{n})
and,
by definition,
K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=K_{h(\pi)}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
.
∎

From the above observation and with the help of
Proposition
A.30

we can recover the moment-cumulant formulas in the exchangeable case

[
Leh04
, Definition 2.6 and Proposition 2.7]
.

Corollary 3.18
.

If a spreadability system
\mathcal{S}
satisfies exchangeability
then for
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we simply write
K_{\pi}
for the partitioned
cumulant functional uniquely determined in Proposition
3.17
.
Then for any
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we have

(3.13)

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\sigma,\pi),

(3.14)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}K_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n}),

where
\mu_{\mathcal{P}}
is the Möbius function on the posets
\mathcal{P}_{n},n\in\mathbb{N}
, see (
A.1
).

3.4.2.
Some moment-cumulant formulas of low order

Example 3.19

(Cumulants in terms of moments)
.

We will write the ordered kernel set partition
\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})

simply as the multiset permutation
i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{n}
. For example,

211=(\{2,3\},\{1\})
.
Examples of Theorem
3.11
are given by

\displaystyle K_{1}(X)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{1}(X),

\displaystyle K_{11}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{11}(X,Y)-\frac{1}{2}(\varphi_{12}(X,Y)+\varphi_{21}(X,Y)),

\displaystyle K_{12}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{12}(X,Y),

\displaystyle K_{21}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{21}(X,Y),

\displaystyle K_{111}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{111}-\frac{1}{2}(\varphi_{112}+\varphi_{121}+\varphi_{122}+\varphi_{211}+\varphi_{212}+\varphi_{221})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{3}(\varphi_{123}+\varphi_{132}+\varphi_{213}+\varphi_{231}+\varphi_{312}+\varphi_{321}),

\displaystyle K_{112}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{112}-\frac{1}{2}(\varphi_{123}+\varphi_{213}),

where for the sake of compactness the arguments
(X,Y,Z)
are omitted
in the last two formulas.

Example 3.20

(Moments in terms of cumulants)
.

Examples of Theorem
3.11
are given by

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}(X)

\displaystyle=K_{1}(X),

\displaystyle\varphi_{11}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=K_{11}(X,Y)+\frac{1}{2!}(K_{12}(X,Y)+K_{21}(X,Y)),

\displaystyle\varphi_{12}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=K_{12}(X,Y),

\displaystyle\varphi_{21}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=K_{21}(X,Y),

\displaystyle\varphi_{111}

\displaystyle=K_{111}+\frac{1}{2!}(K_{112}+K_{121}+K_{122}+K_{211}+K_{212}+K_{221})

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{3!}(K_{123}+K_{132}+K_{213}+K_{231}+K_{312}+K_{321}),

\displaystyle\varphi_{112}

\displaystyle=K_{112}+\frac{1}{2!}(K_{123}+K_{213}),

where
(X,Y,Z)
are omitted in the last two formulas for simplicity.

3.4.3.
Cumulants for universal products

We verify that the cumulants for associative universal products satisfy factorization properties. Here we further assume that
\mathcal{B}=\mathbb{C}
, but it will not be difficult for readers familiar with operator-valued
independence
[
Spe98
,
Pop08
,
HS14
,
Ske04
,
Mło02
]
to
generalize the results to general
\mathcal{B}
-valued conditional expectations in the cases of free, Boolean
and monotone products and spreadability systems.

Definition 3.21
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
the
n
-linear maps on
\mathcal{A}

K_{n}:=K_{\hat{1}_{n}},\qquad\varphi_{n}:=\varphi_{\hat{1}_{n}}

are called the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
cumulant and expectation functional,
respectively.
The latter is simply

\varphi_{n}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{n})

for
X_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
.
Following notation (
3.3
),
we also write

\varphi_{n}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}).

We extend these functionals
multiplicatively to set partitions and
ordered set partitions as follows.

For set partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
we define multiplicative extensions

\varphi_{(\pi)}
of
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
K_{(\pi)}
of
(K_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

by setting

\displaystyle\varphi_{(\pi)}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n}):=\prod_{P\in\pi}\varphi_{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}(Y_{P})\qquad\text{and}\qquad K_{(\pi)}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n}):=\prod_{P\in\pi}K_{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}(Y_{P})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\text{for}\quad Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})\quad\text{or}\quad Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}\in\mathcal{A},

where we use notation (
2.2
) for multilinear functionals.
We also define
\varphi_{(\pi)}:=\varphi_{(\bar{\pi})}
and
K_{(\pi)}:=K_{(\bar{\pi})}
for ordered set partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.

These multiplicative extensions are important for the understanding of
cumulants arising from universal products.
Note that the above definition of
\varphi_{(\pi)}
reduces to
(
2.11
) when
Y_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and the use of the same
notation does not cause any conflict.

It turns out that for specific spreadability systems certain cumulant
functionals vanish identically. This is a consequence of a certain factorization
property which is satisfied by these spreadability systems and which is
subsumed in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
be an ordered set partition.

(i)

Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a spreadability system for some
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. Assume that there exists a family of complex numbers

\{s(\sigma;\pi):\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}\}

such that

(3.15)

\varphi_{\pi}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\bar{\pi}\end{subarray}}s(\sigma;\pi)\varphi_{(\sigma)}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n})

holds for any tuple of random variables
Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=s(\bar{\pi};\pi)K_{(\pi)}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

for any tuple
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
.

(ii)

Let
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a spreadability
system for some
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.
Assume that there exist coefficients

\{t(\rho;\pi):\rho\in\mathcal{OP}_{n},\bar{\sigma}<\bar{\pi}\}

such that

\varphi_{\pi}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\bar{\rho}<\bar{\pi}\end{subarray}}t(\rho;\pi)\varphi_{\rho}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n})

holds for any tuple of random variables
Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
.
Then the cumulant
K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=0
for any tuple
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
.

Remark 3.23
.

Suppose the expansion (
3.15
)
holds for all ordered set partitions
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, then
\mathcal{S}
is a
spreadability system with calculation rule (
2.13
) (e.g. take
Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}\in\iota^{(1)}(\mathcal{A})
).
Now the expansion (
3.15
) extends the calculation rule to the variables

Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots
in
\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
.
Such an extension is not automatic; we give a counterexample in Example
3.27
.

Proof of Lemma
3.22
.

(i)
Choosing
Y_{k}=\iota^{(i_{k})}(X_{k})
and taking the sum of (
3.15
) over all indices
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in[N]
we get formula (
3.15
) for
Y_{k}=N.X_{k}
as well. The desired conclusion is an immediate consequence by comparing the coefficients of
N^{|\pi|}
together with the definition of cumulants (Definition
3.10
).
The point is that, since the number of blocks of any
\sigma<\bar{\pi}
is strictly larger than the number of blocks of
\pi
, the coefficient of
N^{|\pi|}
vanishes in
\varphi_{(\sigma)}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\dots,N.X_{n})
.
(ii)
is proven in a similar way.
∎

Proposition 3.24
.

(i)

Let
K^{\mathrm{T}}_{\pi}
be the cumulants associated to the tensor spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{T}}
. Then

K^{\mathrm{T}}_{\pi}=K^{\mathrm{T}}_{(\pi)},\qquad\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}.

(ii)

Let
K^{\mathrm{F}}_{\pi}
be the cumulants associated to the free spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{F}}
. Then

K^{\mathrm{F}}_{\pi}=\begin{cases}0,&\pi\notin\mathcal{ONC}_{n},\\
K^{\mathrm{F}}_{(\pi)},&\pi\in\mathcal{ONC}_{n}\end{cases}

where by
\mathcal{ONC}_{n}
we denote the set of ordered noncrossing partitions,
see Definition
A.14

(i)
.

(iii)

Let
K^{\mathrm{T}}_{\pi}
be the cumulants associated to the Boolean spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{B}}
. Then

K^{\mathrm{B}}_{\pi}=\begin{cases}0,&\pi\notin\mathcal{OI}_{n},\\
K^{\mathrm{B}}_{(\pi)},&\pi\in\mathcal{OI}_{n}\end{cases}

where by
\mathcal{OI}_{n}
we denote the set of ordered interval partitions,
see Definition
A.14

(ii)
.

(iv)

Let
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{\pi}
be the cumulants associated to the monotone spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Then

K^{\mathrm{M}}_{\pi}=\begin{cases}0,&\pi\notin\mathcal{M}_{n},\\
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{(\pi)},&\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n}\end{cases}

where by
\mathcal{M}_{n}
we denote the set of monotone partitions,
see Definition
A.14

(iii)
.

These results combined with the general moment-cumulant formula (Theorem
3.11
) reproduce the known formulas.

Proof.

Items
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
satisfy exchangeability
and are covered in
[
Leh04
]
(note that our cumulants are invariant under the permutation of the blocks of
\pi
, see Proposition
3.17
);
alternatively these cases follow from Lemma
3.22
and the arguments below.

We are left with case
(iv)
.
Let
u_{\rm M}(\sigma;\pi)
be the universal coefficients from Axiom

(U5)
for monotone independence
(where
f\colon\sigma\to[1]
is unique and hence omitted).
We can prove (
3.15
) for
s(\sigma;\pi):=u_{\mathrm{M}}(\sigma;\pi)

using the technique from the proof of
Proposition
2.18
.
Indeed,
associativity allows us to deduce the identity

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

following the two steps
(a)
and
(b)
there
and the resulting value is exactly the RHS of
(
3.15
) with
Y_{k}=X_{k}^{(i_{k})}
.
It is known that
u_{\rm M}(\bar{\pi};\pi)=1
for
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and

u_{\rm M}(\bar{\pi};\pi)=0
for
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{n}
, see

[
Mur02
, Prop. 3.2]
and the claimed formula follows.
∎

Remark 3.25
.

Other examples of noncrossing cumulants come from the c-free
exchangeability systems of
[
BLS96
]
, see
[
Leh04
, Section 4.7]
.
Lemma
3.22

(ii)
provides a new
proof that crossing cumulants vanish.

Let us consider next c-monotone spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{CM}}
.
Recall from Section
2.3.5
that the construction
of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{CM}}
is based on an algebra
\mathcal{A}
with two linear
functionals
\varphi,\psi
.

Proposition 3.26
.

Let
K^{\mathrm{CM}}_{\pi}
be the
\pi
-cumulant associated to the c-monotone spreadability
system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{CM}}
defined in Section
2.3.5
and

K^{{\mathrm{M}},\psi}_{n}
the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
monotone cumulant functional with
respect to the linear map
\psi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Then

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{CM}}_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{P\in\mathrm{Outer}(\pi)}K^{\mathrm{CM}}_{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}(X_{P})\prod_{P\in\mathrm{Inner}(\pi)}K^{{\mathrm{M}},\psi}_{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}(X_{P}),&\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n},\\
0,&\pi\notin\mathcal{M}_{n}\end{cases}

with
\mathrm{Outer}(\pi)
and
\mathrm{Inner}(\pi)
the sets of outer blocks of
\pi
and inner blocks of
\pi
, respectively; see Definition
A.3

(ii)
.

Proof.

We use two spreadability systems: the c-monotone spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{CM}}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
and the monotone spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{M}}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\psi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i=1}^{\infty})
for
(\mathcal{A},\psi)
.
The multiplicative extension
\varphi_{(\pi)}
of
\varphi
is now modified to

\displaystyle\varphi_{(\pi),\psi}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n}):=\prod_{P\in\mathrm{Outer}(\pi)}\varphi_{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}(Y_{P})\prod_{P\in\mathrm{Inner}(\pi)}\psi_{\left\lvert P\right\rvert}(Y_{P})

\displaystyle\qquad\text{for}\quad\pi\in\mathcal{ONC}_{n},\quad Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})\quad\text{or}\quad Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{n}\in\mathcal{A},

which apparently coincides with
\varphi_{(\pi)}
if
\varphi=\psi
.
By the definition of c-monotone spreadability system, there exist universal constants

u_{\mathrm{CM}}(\sigma,f;\pi)\in\mathbb{C}
depending only on
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n},\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}

with
\bar{\pi}>\sigma
and a “2-coloring”
f
of the blocks of
\sigma
,
i.e. a function
f\colon\sigma\to\{1,2\}
, such that for any
Y_{j}\in\mathcal{A}

\varphi_{\pi}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n})=\varphi_{(\pi),\psi}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n})\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\sigma<\bar{\pi}\\
f\colon\,\sigma\to\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}u_{\mathrm{CM}}(\sigma,f;\pi)\Bigl(\prod_{f(S)=1}\varphi_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}(Y_{S})\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{f(S)=2}\psi_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}(Y_{S})\Bigr)

if
\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n}
and

\varphi_{\pi}(Y_{1},Y_{2},\ldots,Y_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n},\sigma<\bar{\pi}\\
f\colon\,\sigma\to\{1,2\}\end{subarray}}u_{\mathrm{CM}}(\sigma,f;\pi)\Bigl(\prod_{f(S)=1}\varphi_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}(Y_{S})\Bigr)\Bigl(\prod_{f(S)=2}\psi_{\left\lvert S\right\rvert}(Y_{S})\Bigr)

if
\pi\notin\mathcal{M}_{n}
.
Similar to the monotone case, thanks to associativity and
the arguments from the proof of Proposition
2.18
,
this formula holds also for
Y_{j}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A}),j\in[n]
. Then the arguments from Lemma
3.22
and Proposition
3.24
applies.
∎

Associativity played a key role in the proof of Propositions

3.24
and
3.26
above. The following example shows that indeed
the assumptions of Lemma
3.22
can fail for nonassociative universal products.

Example 3.27
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a
\mathbb{C}
-ncps and let
\mathcal{U}:=\sqcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}
be the coproduct in the category of algebras (see (
2.7
)) and
\iota^{(i)}\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{U}
be the natural embedding as
i^{\mathrm{th}}
component. We define a linear functional
\tilde{\varphi}
on
\mathcal{U}
by, for each
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
and
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
with
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
for all
j=1,2,\dots,n-1
, setting

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})})=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if there are $k\neq\ell$ such that $i_{k}=i_{\ell}$},\\
\prod_{i=1}^{n}\varphi(X_{i})&\text{~otherwise}.\end{cases}

Then we get an exchangeability system
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}})
for
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.

For
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
it is easy to see that

(3.16)

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\begin{cases}\varphi_{(\pi)}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})&\text{~if~}\pi\in\mathcal{I}_{n},\\
0&\text{~otherwise}\end{cases}

holds for any tuple
X_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
; however this formula does not extend
to mixed tuples
Y_{i}\in\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
. For example, if
\pi=\{\{1,3\},\{2\}\}
then

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(1)},X_{2}^{(2)},X_{3}^{(3)})=\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2})\varphi(X_{3})=\varphi(X_{1}^{(1)})\varphi(X_{2}^{(2)})\varphi(X_{3}^{(3)})

but

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(1)},X_{2}^{(1)},X_{3}^{(1)})=0\neq\varphi(X_{1}^{(1)})\varphi(X_{2}^{(1)})\varphi(X_{3}^{(1)})

in general.

Next we shall compute some cumulants. Straightforward calculations yield

\displaystyle\varphi_{\hat{1}_{1}}(N.X_{1})

\displaystyle=N\varphi(X_{1}),

\displaystyle\varphi_{\hat{1}_{2}}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2})

\displaystyle=N\varphi(X_{1}X_{2})+N(N-1)\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2}),

\displaystyle\varphi_{\hat{1}_{3}}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},N.X_{3})

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}N\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3})+N(N-1)\varphi(X_{1}X_{2})\varphi(X_{3})\\
+N(N-1)\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2}X_{3})\\
+N(N-1)(N-2)\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2})\varphi(X_{3}),\end{multlined}

so that the coefficients of
N
yield the cumulants

\displaystyle K_{1}(X_{1})

\displaystyle=\varphi(X_{1}),

\displaystyle K_{2}(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle=\varphi(X_{1}X_{2})-\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2}),

\displaystyle K_{3}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})

\displaystyle=\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3})-\varphi(X_{1}X_{2})\varphi(X_{3})-\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2}X_{3})+2\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2})\varphi(X_{3}).

On the other hand, for
\pi=\{\{1,3\},\{2\}\}
we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},N.X_{3})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
i_{1}=i_{3}\end{subarray}}\sum_{i_{1}=i_{3}}\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},X_{3}^{(i_{3})})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
i_{1}\neq i_{3}\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},X_{3}^{(i_{3})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
i_{1}=i_{3}\end{subarray}}\underbrace{\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})}_{\text{$=0$}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\\
i_{1}\neq i_{3}\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\hat{0}_{3}}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})

\displaystyle=N^{2}(N-1)\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2})\varphi(X_{3}),

so that

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})=-\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2})\varphi(X_{3}),

which does not vanish in general although the underlying set partition
\pi
is not interval. This does not contradict Lemma
3.22

because condition (
3.15
) is not satisfied.
Indeed, the only possible identity would be

\varphi_{\{13/2\}}(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})=c\varphi_{\{1/2/3\}}(Y_{1},Y_{2},Y_{3})

for every
Y_{j}\in\bigcup\iota^{(i)}(\mathcal{A})
.
However the combinations

\varphi_{\{13/2\}}(X_{1}^{(1)},X_{2}^{(1)},X_{3}^{(1)})=\varphi_{\{13/2\}}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})=0

and

\varphi_{\{13/2\}}(X_{1}^{(1)},X_{2}^{(2)},X_{3}^{(3)})=\varphi_{\{1/2/3\}}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})=\varphi(X_{1})\varphi(X_{2})\varphi(X_{3})

show that there is no universal constant
c
satisfying
the requirement.

Remark 3.28
.

We already verified in Section
2.3.6
that
Dacko’s V-monotone independence
[
Dac20
]
satisfies the axioms of a
spreadability system, but is non-associative.
On the other hand, it also
falls into the framework of the tree operad of Jekel and Liu

[
JL20
]
with the corresponding cumulants
and it is an interesting question how these cumulants are related.

4.
Partial cumulants and differential equations

Neither the defining formula (Definition
3.10
)
nor the Möbius formula (Theorem
3.11
) are suitable
for the efficient calculation of cumulants of higher orders.
In the case of exchangeability systems recursive formulas are available
which are more adequate for this purpose; see
[
Leh04
, Proposition 3.9]
.
In the classical case, the recursion reads as follows:

K_{n}^{\mathrm{T}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\IE X_{1}X_{2}\dotsm X_{n}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\subsetneqq[n]\\
1\in A\end{subarray}}K^{\mathrm{T}}_{\left\lvert A\right\rvert}(X_{i}:i\in A)\IE\prod_{j\in A^{c}}X_{j}.

In the univariate case this is the familiar formula

\kappa_{n}=m_{n}-\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\binom{n-1}{k-1}\kappa_{k}m_{n-k}

which for normal random variables specifies to

Stein’s method
.

In the free case the recursive formula reads

(4.1)

K_{n}^{\mathrm{F}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi(X_{1}X_{2}\dotsm X_{n})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}A\subsetneqq[n]\\
1\in A\end{subarray}}K^{\mathrm{F}}_{\left\lvert A\right\rvert}(X_{i}:i\in A)\,\varphi_{\intmax(A^{c})}(X_{j}\mid j\in A^{c})

(see Definition
A.5
)
which has been recently considered under the name of “splitting process”
in the realm of combinatorial Hopf algebras
[
EFP16
]
.

Turning to our general setting we note that already in the case of monotone
probability we lack a simple recursive formula;
however the first author and Saigo
[
HS11a
,
HS11b
]
found
a good replacement in terms of differential equations.
Differential equations also play a major role in free probability,
for example the complex Burgers’ equation and its generalizations
appear in the context of free Lévy processes
[
Voi86
]
.
In this section we will unify these differential equations from the point of
view of spreadability systems.

4.1.
Recursive differential equations for evolution of moments

Throughout this subsection, let
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\in\mathbb{N}})
be a spreadability system for a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.
For
\pi=(P_{1},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
we have observed in Theorem
3.9

that
\varphi_{\pi}(N_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\dots,N_{\pi(n)}.X_{n})
is a polynomial in

N_{1},\dots,N_{p}
and we may formally replace
N_{1},\cdots,N_{p}
with real
numbers
t_{1},\dots,t_{p}
.
Thus we obtain formal multivariate moment polynomials

\varphi^{\underline{t}}_{\pi}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}):=\varphi_{\pi}(t_{\pi(1)}.X_{1},\dots,t_{\pi(n)}.X_{n}),\qquad\underline{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.

We will now establish recursive differential equations for these moment polynomials.
By Definition
3.10
, our cumulants are given by

(4.2)

K_{\pi}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\left.\frac{\partial^{p}}{\partial t_{1}\partial t_{2}\dotsm\partial t_{p}}\right\rvert_{\underline{t}=(0,\dots,0)}\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}).

In order to get recursive differential equations we need a refinement of cumulants
which we call
partial cumulants
.
They are obtained by taking the derivatives in (
4.2
) one at a time.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n},\underline{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{p},j\in[p]
. We define the
partial cumulant
to be the polynomial

K_{\pi,P_{j}}^{(t_{1},\dots,t_{j-1},1,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\right\rvert_{t_{j}=0}\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}).

Applying the binomial formula to specific blocks
similar to the proofs of Theorems
3.9

and
3.11

it is easy to derive the following explicit expression for the partial cumulants.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
\underline{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
j\in[p]
.
Then

K_{\pi,P_{j}}^{(t_{1},\dots,t_{j-1},1,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\\
=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{P_{j}}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},\sigma,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}^{(t_{1},\dots,t_{j-1},1,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\,\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\hat{1}_{P_{j}}).

We are now ready to establish partial differential equations for the evolution
of partitioned moments.

Theorem 4.3
.

For
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,

\underline{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{p})
and
j\in[p]
we have

(4.3)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\varphi^{\underline{t}}_{\pi}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subseteq P_{j}}K_{(P_{1},\dots,P_{j-1},A,P_{j}\setminus A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p}),A}^{(t_{1},\dots,t_{j-1},1,t_{j},t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})

(4.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subseteq P_{j}}K_{(P_{1},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}\setminus A,A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p}),A}^{(t_{1},\dots,t_{j-1},t_{j},1,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}).

Proof.

The main ingredient here is the invariance principle of Lemma
3.7
.
Recall the delta/dot operation from Definition
3.3
(i)
,
tensor notations from Section
2.1
and recall that for
any partition
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
the multilinear map
\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{n}\to\mathbb{C}
is identified with the linear lifting

\tilde{\varphi}_{\pi}^{\underline{t}}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathbb{C}
.
Similar to (
2.2
),
for a linear map
L\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
we
we adopt the notation

L^{\otimes P}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\otimes n}\to\mathcal{B}^{\otimes n}
for the linear map

L^{\otimes P}=I\otimes I\otimes\dots\otimes L\otimes I\otimes\dots\otimes L\otimes I\otimes\dots\otimes L\otimes\dots\otimes I

with
L
appearing exactly at position
j
for every
j\in P
.
Let

{\mathbf{X}}=X_{1}\otimes X_{2}\otimes\dots\otimes X_{n}
, let
k+[m]:=\{k+1,k+2,\dots,k+m\}
and let
e_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be the
j
th unit vector. Then, for each
j\in[p]
,

(4.5)

\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{N}+me_{j}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{N}+me_{j}}({\mathbf{X}})=\varphi_{\pi}\left(\left(\delta_{[N_{j}+m]}^{\otimes P_{j}}\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{[N_{i}]}^{\otimes P_{i}}\right)({\mathbf{X}})\right);

after expansion we obtain

(4.6)

\delta_{[N_{j}+m]}^{\otimes P_{j}}=(\delta_{[N_{j}]}+\delta_{N_{j}+[m]})^{\otimes P_{j}}=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\delta_{[N_{j}]}^{\otimes P_{j}\setminus A}\delta_{N_{j}+[m]}^{\otimes A}

and thus (
4.5
) is equal to

(4.7)

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{\pi}\!\left(\left(\delta_{[N_{j}]}^{\otimes P_{j}\setminus A}\delta_{N_{j}+[m]}^{\otimes A}\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{[N_{i}]}^{\otimes P_{i}}\right)({\mathbf{X}})\right).

This is a sum of
\varphi_{\pi}
’s with entries
X_{k}^{(i_{k})},k\in[n]
,
where the indices
i_{k}
with
k\in A
are strictly larger than the indices

i_{k}
with
k\in P_{j}\setminus A
, and therefore we may split the block
P_{j}\in\pi
into two parts:

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}\setminus A,A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}\left(\left(\delta_{[N_{j}]}^{\otimes P_{j}\setminus A}\delta_{N_{j}+[m]}^{\otimes A}\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{[N_{i}]}^{\otimes P_{i}}\right)({\mathbf{X}})\right).

Now by Lemma
3.7

the local shift in
A
can be omitted without changing the value
and we obtain

(4.8)

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}\setminus A,A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}\left(\left(\delta_{[N_{j}]}^{\otimes P_{j}\setminus A}\delta_{[m]}^{\otimes A}\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{[N_{i}]}^{\otimes P_{i}}\right)({\mathbf{X}})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}\setminus A,A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}^{(N_{1},N_{2},\dots,N_{j-1},N_{j},m,N_{j+1},\dots,N_{p})}({\mathbf{X}}).

The analytic extension of this identity is

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}+se_{j}}({\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}\setminus A,A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}^{(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{j-1},t_{j},s,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}({\mathbf{X}})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}}({\mathbf{X}})+\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}\setminus A,A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}^{(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{j-1},t_{j},s,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}({\mathbf{X}}),

and the derivative satisfies the derived identity
(
4.4
):

\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{j}}\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}}({\mathbf{X}})=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\right\rvert_{s=0}\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}+se_{j}}({\mathbf{X}})=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subseteq P_{j}}K_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},P_{j}\setminus A,A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}^{(t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{j-1},t_{j},1,t_{j+1},\dots,t_{p})}({\mathbf{X}}).

In order to prove the first differential equation (
4.3
)
we replace (
4.6
) by the complementary expansion

\delta_{[N_{j}+m]}^{\otimes P_{j}}=(\delta_{[m]}+\delta_{m+[N_{j}]})^{\otimes P_{j}}=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\delta_{[m]}^{\otimes A}\delta_{m+[N_{j}]}^{\otimes P_{j}\setminus A},

and following the lines of (
4.7
)–(
4.8
) we obtain

\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{N}+me_{j}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{\pi}\!\left(\left(\delta_{[m]}^{\otimes A}\delta_{m+[N_{j}]}^{\otimes P_{j}\setminus A}\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{[N_{i}]}^{\otimes P_{i}}\right)({\mathbf{X}})\right)\\
&=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},A,P_{j}\setminus A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}\left(\left(\delta_{[m]}^{\otimes A}\delta_{[N_{j}]}^{\otimes P_{j}\setminus A}\prod_{i\neq j}\delta_{[N_{i}]}^{\otimes P_{i}}\right)({\mathbf{X}})\right)\\
&=\sum_{A\subseteq P_{j}}\varphi_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{j-1},A,P_{j}\setminus A,P_{j+1},\dots,P_{p})}^{(N_{1},N_{2},\dots,N_{j-1},m,N_{j},N_{j+1},\dots,N_{p})}({\mathbf{X}}).\end{aligned}

By analytic continuation, we may replace
m
and
N_{i}
with
s
and
t_{i}
respectively. Taking the derivative with respect to
s
at
0
we obtain (
4.3
).
∎

Remark 4.4
.

In the case of exchangeability both differential equations coincide.

4.2.
Examples from universal products

We consider specializations of the differential
equations of Theorem
4.3
to various spreadability systems arising
from universal products.
In all these examples an expansion of the form (
3.15
)
holds for partitioned expectations
\varphi_{\pi}^{\underline{t}}

and therefore it suffices to consider
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
, i.e.,
the expectation
\varphi^{t}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}):=\varphi((t.X_{1})(t.X_{2})\cdots(t.X_{n}))
.

Example 4.5

(Tensor independence)
.

Consider the tensor spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{T}}
.
It satisfies the factorization property (
2.10
)
and therefore

\varphi_{(A,A^{c})}^{(t_{1},t_{2})}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{A})\,\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{A^{c}}),

so we get

K_{(A,A^{c}),A}^{(1,t_{2})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\right\rvert_{t_{1}=0}\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{A})\,\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{A^{c}})=K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{T}}(X_{A})\,\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{A^{c}}).

The identities in Theorem
4.3
(for
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
) read

(4.9)

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi^{t}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subseteq[n]}K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{T}}(X_{A})\,\varphi^{t}(X_{A^{c}}).

This differential equation can be translated to (exponential)
generating functions as follows.
Given a vector
\underline{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},\dots,u_{n})
of
commuting
indeterminates
and a vector
\underline{X}=(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
of random variables
we define the exponential moment generating function

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{u})

\displaystyle:=1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{n}\\
(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{u_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdots u_{n}^{p_{n}}}{p_{1}!\cdots p_{n}!}\,\varphi^{t}(\underbrace{X_{1},\dots,X_{1}}_{\text{$p_{1}$ times}},\dots,\underbrace{X_{n},\dots X_{n}}_{\text{$p_{n}$ times}})

\displaystyle=[\varphi(e^{u_{1}X_{1}}\cdots e^{u_{n}X_{n}})]^{t}

and the exponential cumulant generating function as the logarithm of the previous

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{u})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{n}\\
(p_{1},\dots,p_{n})\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{u_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdots u_{n}^{p_{n}}}{p_{1}!\cdots p_{n}!}\,K_{p_{1}+\dots+p_{n}}^{\mathrm{T}}(\underbrace{X_{1},\dots,X_{1}}_{\text{$p_{1}$ times}},\dots,\underbrace{X_{n},\dots X_{n}}_{\text{$p_{n}$ times}})

\displaystyle=\log[\mathcal{F}_{\underline{X}}^{1}(\underline{u})].

Then one can prove that

\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{F}_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{u})=\mathcal{L}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{u})\,\mathcal{F}_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{u}),

which is equivalent to (
4.9
). Note that the functions
\mathcal{L}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{u})
and
\mathcal{F}_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{u})
commute.

Example 4.6

(Boolean independence)
.

In the Boolean spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{B}}
we have

\varphi_{(A,A^{c})}^{(t_{1},t_{2})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\prod_{P\in\outintmax(A)}\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{P})\prod_{Q\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{Q}).

where
\outintmax(A)
is the interval partition
constructed in Definition
A.5

and
consists of the contiguous subintervals of
A
.
It follows that

K_{(A,A^{c}),A}^{(1,t_{2})}(X_{1},X_{2}\dots,X_{n})=\begin{cases}K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{B}}(X_{A})\prod_{Q\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{Q}),&\text{if $|\outintmax(A)|=1$},\\
0,&\text{if $|\outintmax(A)|>1$}.\end{cases}

The identities in Theorem
4.3
(for
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
) coincide
and read

(4.10)

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi^{t}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\sum_{A:\text{~interval of~}[n]}K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{B}}(X_{A})\prod_{Q\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t}(X_{Q}).

This differential equation can be interpreted in terms of generating functions
in
noncommuting
indeterminates
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
.
To this end we define noncommutative formal power series

\displaystyle M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}=1}^{n}\varphi^{t}(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}},\dots,X_{i_{m}})\,z_{i_{1}}\cdots z_{i_{m}}

and

\displaystyle K_{\underline{X}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right\rvert_{0}M_{(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})}^{t}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{m}=1}^{n}K_{m}^{\mathrm{B}}(X_{i_{1}},\dots,X_{i_{m}})\,z_{i_{1}}\cdots z_{i_{m}}.

Then differential equation (
4.10
) is equivalent to the identity

\frac{d}{dt}M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z})=M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z})\,K_{\underline{X}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\underline{z})\,M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z}).

Note that
M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z})
and
K_{\underline{X}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\underline{z})
do not commute.

Example 4.7

(Monotone independence)
.

Consider the monotone spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{M}}
.
With the notation
\outintmax(A)
introduced in
Definition
A.5
we have

\varphi_{(A,A^{c})}^{(t_{1},t_{2})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{A})\prod_{B\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{B}),

using monotone independence.
Thus

\displaystyle K_{(A,A^{c}),A}^{(1,t_{2})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\right\rvert_{t_{1}=0}\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{A})\prod_{B\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{B})

\displaystyle=K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{M}}(X_{A})\prod_{B\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{B}).

The first identity in Theorem
4.3
(for
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
) reads

(4.11)

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi^{t}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subseteq[n]}K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{M}}(X_{A})\prod_{B\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{B}),

which is exactly the first identity in
[
HS11a
, Corollary 5.2]
.

On the other hand

\displaystyle K_{(A^{c},A),A}^{(t_{1},1)}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{2}}\right\rvert_{t_{2}=0}\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{A})\prod_{B\in\outintmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{B})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{A})\,K_{|A^{c}|}^{\mathrm{M}}(X_{A^{c}}),&\text{if $|\outintmax(A^{c})|=1$},\\
0,&\text{if $|\outintmax(A^{c})|>1$}.\end{cases}

Condition
|\outintmax(A^{c})|=1
holds if and only if
A^{c}
is an interval. Therefore, the second equality in Theorem
4.3
(for
\pi=\hat{1}_{n}
) reads

(4.12)

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi^{t}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\sum_{B:\text{~interval of~}[n]}K_{|B|}^{\mathrm{M}}(X_{B})\,\varphi^{t}(X_{B^{c}}),

which is exactly the second equality in
[
HS11a
, Corollary 5.2]
.

Results on generating functions in
[
HS11a
]

correspond to these differential equations. For noncommutative indeterminates
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
, we define the cumulant generating function

\displaystyle K_{\underline{X}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle:=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\right\rvert_{0}M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\cdots,i_{m}=1}^{n}K_{m}^{\mathrm{M}}(X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{m}})\,z_{i_{1}}z_{i_{2}}\cdots z_{i_{m}}.

Then it is shown in
[
HS11a
, Theorem 6.3]
that

(4.13)

M_{\underline{X}}^{s+t}(\underline{z})=M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z})\,M_{\underline{X}}^{s}(z_{1}M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z}),\dots,z_{n}M_{\underline{X}}^{t}(\underline{z})).

The partial derivatives of (
4.13
) regarding
s
at
0
and
t
at
0
become (
4.11
) and (
4.12
), respectively.

Example 4.8

(Free independence)
.

Consider the free spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{F}}
.
One can use the formula for products of free random variables

[
NS06
, Theorem 14.4]
to show that
for a nonempty subset
A\subseteq[n]
we can expand

\varphi_{(A,A^{c})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi(X_{A})\prod_{P\in\intmax(A^{c})}\varphi(X_{P})+R,

where

\intmax(A^{c})
is the partition defined in
Definition
A.5

(ii)

and every term in
R
has at least two factors from
A
, i.e., factors of the form
\varphi(X_{k_{1}}X_{k_{2}}\cdots X_{k_{m}})
,
k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{m}\in A
. For example,

\varphi_{\{\{2,4\},\{1,3,5\}\}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{5})=\varphi(X_{2}X_{4})\,\varphi(X_{1}X_{5})\,\varphi(X_{3})+R
,
where every term in
R
contains the factor
\varphi(X_{2})\,\varphi(X_{4})
.
This implies that

\varphi_{(A,A^{c})}^{(t_{1},t_{2})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi^{t_{1}}(X_{A})\prod_{P\in\intmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{P})+O(t_{1}^{2})\qquad\text{as $t_{1}\to 0$},

so by taking the partial derivative
\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial t_{1}}\right\rvert_{t_{1}=0}
we get

K_{(A,A^{c}),A}^{(1,t_{2})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{F}}(X_{A})\prod_{P\in\intmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t_{2}}(X_{P}).

This yields the differential equation

(4.14)

\frac{d}{dt}\varphi^{t}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\sum_{\emptyset\neq A\subseteq[n]}K_{|A|}^{\mathrm{F}}(X_{A})\prod_{P\in\intmax(A^{c})}\varphi^{t}(X_{P}),

which is similar to the monotone case (
4.11
).

Denote by
\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{n})
a vector of noncommuting indeterminates and
by
\underline{X}=(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
a random vector.
In order to obtain a differential equation we need in
addition the two-sided generating function

\tilde{M}^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z},w):=\sum_{p,q=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{p}\in[n]\\
j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{q}\in[n]\end{subarray}}\varphi^{t}(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}},\dots,X_{i_{p}},X_{j_{1}},X_{j_{2}},\dots,X_{j_{q}})\,z_{i_{1}}z_{i_{2}}\cdots z_{i_{p}}wz_{j_{1}}z_{j_{2}}\cdots z_{j_{q}}

as well as the
R
-transform

R_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{j}=1}^{n}K_{j}^{\mathrm{F}}(X_{i_{1}},X_{i_{2}},\cdots,X_{i_{j}})\,z_{i_{1}}z_{i_{2}}\cdots z_{i_{j}}.

After some computations we infer from (
4.14
) that

(4.15)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z})=\tilde{M}^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z},R(z_{1}M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}),z_{2}M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}),\dots,z_{n}M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}))(M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}))^{-1}).

Note that by
[
NS06
, Corollary 16.16]
the second argument can be written as

R(z_{1}M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}),z_{2}M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}),\dots,z_{n}M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}))\,(M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z}))^{-1}=\frac{1-M^{t}_{\underline{X}}(\underline{z})^{-1}}{t}.

When
n=1
the differential equation (
4.15
) is equivalent to the generalized complex Burgers equation (see
[
Voi86
, p. 343]
)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}G^{t}_{X}(z)+\frac{R_{X}(G_{X}^{t}(z))}{G^{t}_{X}(z)}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}G^{t}_{X}(z)=0,

where
G^{t}_{X}
is the Cauchy transform

G^{t}_{X}(z)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\varphi^{t}(\underbrace{X,\dots,X}_{\text{$n$ fold}})\,z^{-n-1}.

5.
Mixed cumulants and sums of independent random variables

5.1.
Vanishing of mixed cumulants in exchangeability systems

In the case of exchangeability systems independence
the
vanishing of mixed cumulants
(Axiom
(E3)
in Definition
3.1
)
is the crucial property which makes cumulants interesting,
since it characterizes independence
[
Leh04
, Prop. 2.10]
.
That is, if the arguments of
K_{n}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
can be split into two mutually
independent families then the cumulant vanishes;
more generally,
K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=0
whenever the entries of one of the blocks of

\pi
splits into two mutually independent subsets. This is the content
of the following proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
be a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps and

\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
an exchangeability system for
\mathcal{A}
.
Given a partition
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and a family

X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathcal{A}
such that there is a block

P\in\pi
which can be partitioned into
P=P_{1}\dot{\cup}P_{2}

such that
\{X_{i}:i\in P_{1}\}
and
\{X_{i}:i\in P_{2}\}
are
independent in the sense of Definition
2.12
,
we have
K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2}\dots,X_{n})=0
.

For further reference we reproduce here a short lattice theoretic proof
due to P. Zwiernik
[
Zwi12
]
,
which will serve as a model for the spreadable case.
It is based on
Weisner’s Lemma

(see
[
Sta97
, Cor. 3.9.3]
for a simple version and

[
BBR86
]
for the full version).

Its generalization will be essential for the understanding of mixed cumulants in the spreadable setting.

Lemma 5.2

(Weisner’s Lemma)
.

In any lattice
(P,\leq)
the Möbius function satisfies the identity

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\\
x\wedge a=c\end{subarray}}\mu(x,b)=\begin{cases}\mu(c,b)&\text{if $a\geq b$}\\
0&\text{if $a\not\geq b$}.\end{cases}

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Let
\rho
be the partition
obtained from
\pi
by splitting the block
P

as indicated in the proposition, then
\rho<\pi
and by assumption

\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\sigma\wedge\rho}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
; hence

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\,\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\sigma\wedge\rho}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\,\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\varphi_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\\
\sigma\wedge\rho=\tau\end{subarray}}\,\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\sigma,\pi).

Now
\rho\not\geq\pi
and the second case of Weisner’s lemma applies.
∎

5.2.
Partial vanishing of mixed cumulants in spreadability systems

Since vanishing of mixed cumulants implies additivity of cumulants
(
1.1
)
for sums of independent random variables,
it cannot hold for general spreadability systems, e.g., monotone convolution
is noncommutative and therefore monotone cumulants are not additive
[
HS11a
]
.

In this section we investigate what remains true in the
general setting and provide a formula expressing mixed cumulants in terms of lower order cumulants.
This question is intimately related to the question of convolution -
determining the distribution of the sum of independent random variables,
which is not commutative in general.

\mathcal{OP}_{n}
with the quasi-meet operation
\curlywedge
is not a lattice
and Weisner’s lemma
5.2
does not hold.
This means that mixed cumulants do not vanish,
yet they can be expanded in terms of lower order cumulants weighted by certain coefficients,
which we will study next.

Definition 5.3
.

For
\tau,\eta\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
let

(5.1)

\displaystyle w(\tau,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\hat{1}_{n}),

(Weisner coefficients)

(5.2)

\displaystyle g(\tau,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\geq\tau\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\zeta}(\tau,\sigma)\,w(\sigma,\eta),

(Goldberg coefficients)

and more generally for
\tau,\eta,\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}

define the partitioned Weisner and Goldberg coefficients

(5.3)

\displaystyle w(\tau,\eta,\pi)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi),

(5.4)

\displaystyle g(\tau,\eta,\pi)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\geq\tau\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\zeta}(\tau,\sigma)\,w(\sigma,\eta,\pi).

Remark 5.4
.

It follows from the properties of the quasi-meet operation
(cf. Proposition
A.21

(i)
and

(ii)
)
that both

w(\tau,\eta,\pi)
and
g(\tau,\eta,\pi)
vanish unless

\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
and
\tau\leq\pi
.

We postpone further combinatorial study of these coefficients to Section
5.3

and first exhibit their role as a substitute for Weisner’s lemma
5.2
in the description of cumulants of independent arguments.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a spreadability system for a given

\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. Let
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
and

(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
be a tuple with kernel

\eta=\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.
Then

(5.5)

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\ldots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\tau\leq\pi\\
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,w(\tau,\eta,\pi)

(5.6)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\tau\leq\pi\\
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}\end{subarray}}K_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,g(\tau,\eta,\pi).

Proof.

We proceed as in the proof of Proposition
5.1
.
Expressing cumulants in terms of moments (see Theorem
3.11
), we have

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\ldots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\sigma}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\ldots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})\,\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\sigma\curlywedge\eta}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}\varphi_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau,\sigma\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}\varphi_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,w(\tau,\eta,\pi)

which is (
5.5
). Now substitute the moment-cumulant
formula (
3.11
) to obtain

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\leq\tau\end{subarray}}K_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\widetilde{\zeta}(\sigma,\tau)\,w(\tau,\eta,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}K_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\tau\geq\sigma\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\zeta}(\sigma,\tau)\,w(\tau,\eta,\pi)\biggr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}K_{\sigma}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,g(\sigma,\eta,\pi).

Finally Remark
5.4
applies and the sums (
5.5
) and (
5.6
)
can be restricted to
\tau\leq\pi
and
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
.
∎

We can now characterize
\mathcal{S}
-independence in terms of the above proposition, that is, “semi-vanishing” of mixed cumulants.

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a spreadability system for a given

\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
. A sequence of subalgebras
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{A}
, where

I\subseteq\mathbb{N}
, is
\mathcal{S}
-independent
if and only if for any tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
, any random variables
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}}\times\mathcal{A}_{i_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{A}_{i_{n}}
and any ordered set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, we have

(5.7)

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\tau\leq\pi\end{subarray}}K_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,g(\tau,\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n}),\pi)=0.

Remark 5.7
.

We can also formulate the theorem in terms of moments; we only need to replace
(
5.7
) by the equation

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\tau\leq\pi\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,w(\tau,\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n}),\pi)=0.

Proof.

Fix a tuple
(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
with kernel

\eta=\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
.
Independence means that

\displaystyle\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\eta}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

for all
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
and by Möbius inversion this is equivalent to the identity

\displaystyle K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

\displaystyle=K_{\pi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})},X_{2}^{(i_{2})},\dots,X_{n}^{(i_{n})})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\tau\leq\pi\end{subarray}}K_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})\,g(\tau,\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n}),\pi)

for all
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

It is not obvious that Theorem
5.6
generalizes Proposition
5.1

(vanishing of mixed cumulants for
\mathcal{E}
-independent subalgebras).
In fact it implies a nontrivial identity: for any
\tau,\eta,\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
such that
\eta\rfloor_{P}\neq\hat{1}_{P}
for some
P\in\pi
,

(5.8)

\sum_{h\in\mathfrak{S}_{|\tau|}}g(h(\tau),\eta,\pi)=0,

where
h(\tau)
is the action of the permutation
h
on the blocks of
\tau
.
Similarly, Remark
5.7
(or Proposition
5.5
)
generalizes the vanishing of mixed cumulants for
\mathcal{E}
-independent
subalgebras. Consequently we must have

(5.9)

\sum_{h\in\mathfrak{S}_{|\tau|}}w(h(\tau),\eta,\pi)=0

under the same assumptions on
\tau,\eta,\pi.

5.3.
Combinatorial evaluation of Weisner and Goldberg coefficients

The combinatorial description of the coefficients introduced
in Definition
5.3

involves certain statistics of multiset permutations.
The first systematic study of these permutation statistics is contained
in the seminal work of MacMahon
[
Mac15
]
, for a modern
treatment see
[
Bón12
]
. Said statistics play a major
role in the theory of free Lie algebras
[
Reu93
]
,
which also seem to play a role in our context, see Section
6
below.

Definition 5.9
.

A
multiset
is a pair
(A,f)
where
A
is the
underlying set
and
f:A\to\mathbb{N}
is a
function. The value
f(a)
is called the
multiplicity
of the element

a\in A
. Informally, a multiset is a set which contains multiple
indistinguishable copies of each of its elements. In the present paper
multisets will always be based on integer segments
A=[n]
and
in this case the tuple
(f(1),f(2),\dots,f(n))
is called the

type
of the multiset.
A
permutation of a multiset
is a rearrangement of all its elements
where all multiplicities are preserved, i.e., a word with a prescribed total number
of occurrences of each letter.
The number of distinct permutations of a multiset of type

(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n})
is given by the multinomial coefficient

\binom{f_{1}+f_{2}+\dotsm+f_{n}}{f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n}}.

A
proper set
is a multiset of type
(1,1,\dots,1)
and we recover
the number of its permutations as
n!
.

We will be interested in the following statistics of multiset permutations.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
\sigma=w_{1}w_{2}\dots w_{s}
be a permutation of a multiset
of type
(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n})
where
s=f_{1}+f_{2}+\dotsm+f_{n}
.
An index
1\leq i\leq s-1
is called a

(i)

descent
(or
drop
or

fall
) if
w_{i}>w_{i+1}
.

(ii)

plateau
(or
level
) if
w_{i}=w_{i+1}
.

(iii)

ascent
(or
rise
) if
w_{i}<w_{i+1}
.

We denote these sets by

\displaystyle\Des(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Des((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})=\{j\in[s-1]\mid w_{j}>w_{j+1}\},

\displaystyle\Plat(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Plat((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})=\{j\in[s-1]\mid w_{j}=w_{j+1}\},

\displaystyle\Asc(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Asc((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})=\{j\in[s-1]\mid w_{j}<w_{j+1}\}

and
the respective cardinalities, i.e.,
the number of descents (ascents, plateaux respectively) of the multiset
permutation
\sigma
,
by

\displaystyle\des(\sigma)=\des((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\Des((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})\right\rvert,

\displaystyle\plat(\sigma)=\plat((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\Plat((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})\right\rvert,

\displaystyle\asc(\sigma)=\asc((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\Asc((w_{i})_{i=1}^{s})\right\rvert.

Remark 5.11
.

1.

Note that some authors also count
i=s
as a descent and
i=0
as an ascent.

2.

Counting permutations by descents and ascents is a classic subject in
combinatorics. In the case of descents of multiset permutations this is also
known as
Simon Newcomb’s problem

[
Rio58
,
DR69
]
.

Clearly every
element except the last is either a descent, an ascent, or a plateau,
and therefore

(5.10)

\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert=\des(\sigma)+\plat(\sigma)+\asc(\sigma)+1.

Definition 5.12
.

To any pair of ordered set partitions
\tau,\eta\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
such that

\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
,
we associate a multiset on
[\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert]
by setting
the multiplicity of
k\in\{1,2,\dots,\left\lvert\eta\right\rvert\}
to be equal to the
number of blocks of
\tau
contained in the
k
-th block of
\eta
.
Replacing every block of
\tau
by the label of the block of
\eta

containing it we obtain a permutation of this multiset.
We denote by
\des_{\eta}(\tau)
,
\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
and
\plat_{\eta}(\tau)

its statistics as defined in Definition
5.10
.
More precisely,
let
\tau,\eta\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
such that
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
. If
\eta=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{e})
, then the blocks of
\bar{\tau}
can be arranged

\bar{\tau}=\{E_{1,1},E_{1,2},\ldots,E_{1,l_{1}},\ldots,E_{e,1},\ldots,E_{e,l_{e}}\},

where each
E_{i}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{l_{i}}E_{i,j}
is a disjoint union taken in
canonical order of the subsets (i.e., sorted according to their minimal
elements). So
\tau
can be written as

\tau=(E_{m_{1},n_{1}},E_{m_{2},n_{2}},\ldots,E_{m_{s},n_{s}}),

which is a permutation of the blocks of
\bar{\tau}
. Then we denote by

\Des_{\eta}(\tau),\Plat_{\eta}(\tau)
and
\Asc_{\eta}(\tau)
respectively,
the sets

\displaystyle\Des_{\eta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\Des((m_{i})_{i=1}^{s}),

\displaystyle\Plat_{\eta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\Plat((m_{i})_{i=1}^{s}),

\displaystyle\Asc_{\eta}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\Asc((m_{i})_{i=1}^{s})

and by
\des_{\eta}(\tau)
,

\plat_{\eta}(\tau)
and
\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
the respective cardinalities.

Example 5.13
.

Consider the partitions
\eta=(E_{1},E_{2},E_{3},E_{4},E_{5})\in\mathcal{OP}_{10}
with blocks

E_{1}=\{5,8\}
,
E_{2}=\{9,10\}
,
E_{3}=\{3,6\}
,
E_{4}=\{1,2,4\}
,
E_{5}=\{7\}
,
and

\tau=(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4},T_{5},T_{6},T_{7},T_{8})\in\mathcal{OP}_{10}

with blocks

T_{1}=\{3\}
,
T_{2}=\{6\}
,
T_{3}=\{1,4\}
,
T_{4}=\{7\}
,
T_{5}=\{10\}
,
T_{6}=\{5,8\}
,

T_{7}=\{9\}
,
T_{8}=\{2\}
. Then
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
. Now
T_{1}\subseteq E_{3}
and thus
w_{1}=3
,
T_{2}\subseteq E_{3}
and thus
w_{2}=3
, etc.; the
multiset permutation thus induced on
\tau
by
\eta
is

(w_{i})_{i=1}^{8}=(3,3,4,5,2,1,2,4),

and the statistics are
\des((w_{i})_{i=1}^{8})=2
,
\plat((w_{i})_{i=1}^{8})=1
and
\asc((w_{i})_{i=1}^{8})=4
.

Definition 5.14
.

Let
\sigma=(w_{i})_{i=1}^{s}
be a multiset permutation. An
ascending
(
resp.
descending) run

is a maximal contiguous subsequence
(w_{i})_{i=l}^{k}
which is strictly
increasing (resp. decreasing).
Similarly,
a level run
is a maximal subsequence
(w_{i})_{i=j}^{k}
such that

w_{j}=w_{j+1}=\cdots=w_{k}
.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
\sigma=(w_{i})_{i=1}^{s}
be a multiset permutation.

(i)

\sigma
can be decomposed uniquely into ascending runs
separated by descents and plateaux. The number
of ascending runs is equal to
s-\asc(\sigma)
.

(ii)

\sigma
can be decomposed uniquely into descending runs
separated by ascents and plateaux. The number
of descending runs is equal to
s-\des(\sigma)
.

(iii)

\sigma
can be decomposed uniquely into level runs
separated by ascents and descents. The number
of level runs is equal to
s-\plat(\sigma)
.

Proof.

We only prove (i).
The claim is clearly true when
\asc\sigma=s-1
, i.e., when the sequence is
monotone increasing. Otherwise replacing any ascent by a descent or plateau
splits an ascending run into two, i.e., increases the number of ascending
runs by one.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

Suppose
\bar{\tau}=\bar{\eta}
, then
the blocks of
\tau
are a permutation of the blocks of
\eta
.
More precisely, if
\eta=(E_{1},E_{2},\dots,E_{e})
then
there is a permutation
h\in\mathfrak{S}_{e}

such that
\tau=h(\eta)=(E_{h(1)},E_{h(2)},\dots,E_{h(e)})
.
Then
\des_{\eta}(\tau)
and

\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
coincide with
\des(h)
and
\asc(h)
,
where the latter quantities are the numbers of descents and ascents of the
permutation
h
, respectively. See

[
Bón12
,
Bre93
,
Reu93
]

and
[
Sta97
, p. 25]

for the uses of
\des(h)
and

\asc(h)
in the context of symmetric groups.

Example 5.17
.

The statistics of the sequence
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{9}=(1,1,3,5,5,5,4,1,4)

are as follows:

\des((m_{i})_{i=1}^{9})=2
,
\plat((m_{i})_{i=1}^{9})=3
and

\asc((m_{i})_{i=1}^{9})=3
.
The decomposition into ascending runs is given by

(1),(1,3,5),(5),(5),(4),(1,4),

the decomposition into level runs is

(1,1),(3),(5,5,5),(4),(1),(4)

and the decomposition into descending runs is

(1),(1),(3),(5),(5),(5,4,1),(4).

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
\eta,\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
such that
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
,
then there is an ordered set partition
\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{\mathrm{asc}}(\tau,\eta)
such that

\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\}=[\tau,\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{\mathrm{asc}}(\tau,\eta)].

and the
restriction of the mapping
\Psi
in Proposition
A.24

establishes a poset isomorphism

\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\}\to\mathcal{I}_{p_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{p_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{p_{t}},

where
1\leq p_{i}\leq n
are the lengths of the ascending runs of the sequence
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{s}
from Definition
5.12
and
t=\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
.

Proof.

Let
\eta=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{e})
and write

\tau=(E_{m_{1},n_{1}},E_{m_{2},n_{2}},\ldots,E_{m_{s},n_{s}})

as in Definition
5.12
.
From Proposition
A.21
we infer that
any partition
\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
such that
\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau

satisfies
\sigma\geq\tau
.
From Proposition
A.24
we infer that there is an interval partition

\lambda=(L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{l})\in\mathcal{I}_{s}
such that

\sigma=\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in L_{1}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\bigcup_{i\in L_{2}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in L_{l}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}}\biggr).

In order that
\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau
it is necessary and sufficient
that every block
L\in\lambda
, say
L=\{a+1,a+2,\ldots,a+b\}
,
induces a strictly increasing sequence
m_{a+1}<m_{a+2}<\cdots<m_{a+b}
.

Let
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{i_{1}},(m_{i})_{i=i_{1}+1}^{i_{2}},\ldots,(m_{i})_{i=i_{t-1}+1}^{s}
be the decomposition of
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{s}
into ascending
runs. This decomposition defines the interval blocks
A_{j}:=\{i_{j-1}+1,i_{j-1}+2,\ldots,i_{j}\}
(
i_{0}=0,i_{t}=s
) and hence defines an interval partition
\alpha=(A_{1},A_{2},\ldots,A_{t})
. The interval partition
\lambda
consists of
increasing intervals and therefore is finer than
\alpha
.
Thus we have an isomorphism

\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\}\to\mathcal{I}_{A_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{A_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{A_{t}}

via the restriction of the map
\Psi\rfloor_{[\tau,\hat{1}_{n}]}
from Proposition
A.24
. The number
t=\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert
is equal to
\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
, the integers
p_{j}
are the cardinalities of
A_{j}
and
\sigma_{\mathrm{max}}^{\mathrm{asc}}(\tau,\eta)
is the ordered set partition corresponding to
\lambda=\alpha
.
∎

With these preparations we can now combinatorially evaluates the Weisner and
Goldberg coefficients from Definition
5.3
.

Proposition 5.19

(Weisner coefficients)
.

(i)

For
\tau,\eta\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
the Weisner coefficient
(
5.1
) is

(5.11)

w(\tau,\eta)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)-1}(1+x)^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\,dx=\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)-1}}{\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert\binom{\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-1}{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}},&\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta},\\
0,&\bar{\tau}\not\leq\bar{\eta}.\end{cases}

(ii)

For
\tau,\eta,\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
the partitioned Weisner
coefficient (
5.3
) is

w(\tau,\eta,\pi)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{P\in\pi}w(\tau\rfloor_{P},\eta\rfloor_{P}),&\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta},~\tau\leq\pi,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

(i)
If
\bar{\tau}\not\leq\bar{\eta}
, then there is no

\sigma
such that
\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau
and the sum is empty.
Let us therefore assume henceforth that
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
.
We take up the end of the proof of Lemma
5.18
where we
established the poset isomorphism

\displaystyle\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{I}_{p_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{p_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{p_{t}}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{B}_{p_{1}-1}\times\mathcal{B}_{p_{2}-1}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{B}_{p_{t}-1}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{B}_{p_{1}+\cdots+p_{t}-t},

where the second isomorphism follows from Proposition
A.4
and
p_{i}
denotes the length of the
i
-th ascending run.
The latter contains
p_{i}-1
rises and therefore the total number of
ascents is

\asc_{\eta}(\tau)=p_{1}+p_{2}+\cdots+p_{t}-t
.
In the identification above, a partition
\sigma
is mapped to a subset

A\subseteq[p_{1}+p_{2}+\cdots+p_{t}-t]

with
\left\lvert A\right\rvert=\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-t
elements and we have

\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\hat{1}_{n})=\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-1}}{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert}=\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert A\right\rvert+t-1}}{\left\lvert A\right\rvert+t}.

Performing the sum we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\hat{1}_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{A\subseteq[\asc_{\eta}(\tau)]}\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert A\right\rvert+t-1}}{\left\lvert A\right\rvert+t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\binom{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}{k}\frac{(-1)^{k+t-1}}{k+t}

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{0}\sum_{k=0}^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\binom{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}{k}x^{k+t-1}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{0}x^{t-1}(1+x)^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\,dx

\displaystyle=(-1)^{t-1}B(t,\asc_{\eta}(\tau)+1),

where
B
is the beta function
B(a,b)=\frac{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a+b)}

which can be written in terms of binomial coefficients as desired.

(ii)
In order for the set
\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\leq\pi,~\sigma\curlywedge\eta=\tau\}
to be nonempty,
it is necessary that
\tau\leq\pi
and
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
. We adopt
the notations from Definition
5.12

and infer from Proposition
A.24
that there exists an
interval partition
\rho=(R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{p})\in\mathcal{I}_{s}
such that

\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})=\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in R_{1}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\bigcup_{i\in R_{2}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\dots,\bigcup_{i\in R_{p}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}}\biggr).

If follows from Lemma
5.18
that
\sigma
belongs to
[\tau,\sigma_{\max{}}^{\mathrm{asc}}(\tau,\eta)]
.
In addition,
\sigma
must satisfy
\sigma\leq\pi
. Hence, the ascending runs considered in Lemma
5.18
are split by the blocks of
\pi
.
More precisely, for each
k\in[p]
we decompose
(m_{i})_{i\in R_{k}}
into
ascending runs, which give rise to the interval partition

\gamma_{k}=(G_{k,1},\dots,G_{k,u_{k}})\in\mathcal{I}_{R_{k}}
where
G_{k,j}

consists of the indices
i
of the
j
-th ascending run of
(m_{i})_{i\in R_{k}}
.
Then

\sigma_{\max{}}^{\mathrm{asc}}(\tau\rfloor_{P_{k}},\eta\rfloor_{P_{k}})=\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in G_{k,1}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\bigcup_{i\in G_{k,2}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\dots,\bigcup_{i\in G_{k,u_{k}}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}}\biggr)

and for each
P\in\pi
, we pick an arbitrary
\sigma_{P}\in\mathcal{OP}_{P}
from the interval

[\tau\rfloor_{P},\sigma_{\max{}}^{\mathrm{asc}}(\tau\rfloor_{P},\eta\rfloor_{P})]
,
concatenate them and obtain

\sigma=\sigma_{P_{1}}\sigma_{P_{2}}\cdots\sigma_{P_{p}}\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.
Since
\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi)
is the product of

\frac{(-1)^{\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P})-1}}{\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P})}
over

P\in\pi
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Examples of Weisner coefficients will be given in Example
5.27
.

Observe that
w(\tau,\eta,\pi)\neq 0
for
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta},\tau\leq\pi

and since in general the expectation values
\varphi_{\tau}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})

among different
\tau
do not cancel each other, we cannot expect the vanishing of cumulants without further assumptions.
However we can express cumulants with independent entries also in terms of cumulants of
lower orders. In this case it turns out that the coefficients are determined by the number of plateaux.

Lemma 5.20
.

Let
\eta,\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
such that
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
, then the
restriction of the map
\Psi\rfloor_{[\tau,\hat{1}_{n}]}
from
Proposition
A.24
establishes a poset isomorphism

(5.12)

\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\geq\tau,\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\eta}\}\to\mathcal{I}_{q_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{q_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{q_{r}},

where
1\leq q_{i}\leq n
are the lengths of the level runs and
the number
r
is equal to
\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-\plat_{\eta}(\tau)
.
In particular, there is an ordered set partition
\sigma_{\max{}}^{\mathrm{pla}}(\tau,\eta)
such that

\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\geq\tau,\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\eta}\}=[\tau,\sigma_{\max{}}^{\mathrm{pla}}(\tau,\eta)].

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of Lemma
5.18
.
Write
\tau=(E_{m_{1},n_{1}},E_{m_{2},n_{2}},\ldots,E_{m_{s},n_{s}})
and

\eta=(E_{1},E_{2},\ldots,E_{e})
as in Definition
5.12
.
Let
\sigma\geq\tau
, then from Proposition
A.24
we infer
that there is an interval partition

\lambda=(L_{1},L_{2},\ldots,L_{l})\in\mathcal{I}_{s}
such that

\sigma=\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in L_{1}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\bigcup_{i\in L_{2}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in L_{l}}E_{m_{i},n_{i}}\biggr).

Let
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{i_{1}},(m_{i})_{i=i_{1}+1}^{i_{2}},\ldots,(m_{i})_{i=i_{r-1}+1}^{s}
be
the decomposition of
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{s}
into level runs. This decomposition
determines interval blocks
B_{j}:=(i_{j-1}+1,i_{j-1}+2,\ldots,i_{j})

(
i_{0}=0,i_{r}=s
) and hence gives rise to an interval partition
\beta=(B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{r})
. In order that
\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\eta}
, each
L_{i}

connects only plateaux, which is equivalent to the condition that
\lambda\leq\beta
.
Denoting by
q_{j}=\left\lvert B_{j}\right\rvert=i_{j}-i_{j-1}
, we get the isomorphism (
5.12
). The ordered
set partition
\sigma_{\max}^{\plat}(\tau,\eta)
corresponds to the
choice
\lambda=\beta
.
∎

Proposition 5.21

(Goldberg coefficients)
.

(i)

For
\tau,\eta\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
the Goldberg coefficient
(
5.2
) evaluates to

g(\tau,\eta)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1}{q_{1}!q_{2}!\cdots q_{r}!}\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des_{\eta}(\tau)}(1+x)^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}P_{q_{j}}(x)\,dx,&\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta},\\
0,&\bar{\tau}\not\leq\bar{\eta},\end{cases}

where
r,q_{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{r}
are the integers from Lemma
5.20
,

P_{q}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{q}k!\,S(q,k)\,x^{k-1}=E_{q}(x,x+1),

S(q,k)
are the Stirling numbers of the second kind
and

E_{n}(x,y)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}}x^{\des\sigma}y^{\asc\sigma}

are the

homogeneous Eulerian polynomials

[
Reu93
, p. 62]
.
The first few polynomials are

P_{1}(x)=1,\quad P_{2}(x)=2x+1,\quad P_{3}(x)=6x^{2}+6x+1,\quad\dots

(ii)

For
\tau,\eta,\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
the partitioned Goldberg coefficient
coefficient (
5.4
) is

g(\tau,\eta,\pi)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\prod_{P\in\pi}g(\tau\rfloor_{P},\eta\rfloor_{P}),&\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta},~\tau\leq\pi,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 5.22
.

The name “Goldberg coefficients” originates from the
Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula, see Section
6
below,
in particular Theorem
6.17
.
Some examples of
g(\tau,\eta)
will be computed in Example
5.26
.

Remark 5.23
.

The expansion of
P_{q}(x)
in terms of Stirling coefficients was first proved
by Frobenius
[
Com74
, Theorem E, p. 244]
.

Proof.

(i)

If
\bar{\tau}\not\leq\bar{\eta}
, then
w(\sigma,\eta)=0
for all
\sigma\geq\tau
and so
g(\tau,\eta)=0
. Assume hereafter that
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
.
From Lemma
5.20
we have the isomorphism

\displaystyle\{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\mid\sigma\geq\tau,\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\eta}\}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{I}_{q_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{q_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{q_{r}},

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\mapsto(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r}).

Since
r=\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-\plat_{\eta}(\tau)=\des_{\eta}(\tau)+\asc_{\eta}(\tau)+1
, we
have
\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)-1=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\left\lvert\sigma_{i}\right\rvert-1)+\des_{\eta}(\tau)
. Note also that
[\tau:\sigma]!=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\prod_{S\in\sigma_{i}}\left\lvert S\right\rvert!
. Since
\sigma
just connects the
blocks of a level run of
(m_{i})_{i=1}^{\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert}
, it does not change the
number of ascents:
\asc_{\eta}(\sigma)=\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
and we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\geq\tau\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\zeta}(\tau,\sigma)\,w(\sigma,\eta)\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\geq\tau,\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\eta}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{[\tau:\sigma]!}\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)-1}(1+x)^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\,dx\\
&=\sum_{(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\ldots,\sigma_{r})\in\mathcal{I}_{q_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{q_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{q_{r}}}\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des_{\eta}(\tau)}(1+x)^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{x^{\left\lvert\sigma_{i}\right\rvert-1}}{\prod_{S\in\sigma_{i}}\left\lvert S\right\rvert!}dx\\
&=\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des_{\eta}(\tau)}(1+x)^{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left(\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{I}_{q_{i}}}\frac{x^{\left\lvert\rho\right\rvert-1}}{\prod_{R\in\rho}\left\lvert R\right\rvert!}\right)dx.\end{aligned}

For
q\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{I}_{q}}\frac{x^{\left\lvert\rho\right\rvert-1}}{\prod_{R\in\rho}\left\lvert R\right\rvert!}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=q\\
n_{i}\geq 1,k\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{x^{k-1}}{n_{1}!\cdots n_{k}!}.

For each fixed
k
, the sum
\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=q\\
n_{i}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{q!}{n_{1}!\cdots n_{k}!}
is the number of ways of
distributing
q
distinct objects among
k
nonempty urns, so it equals
k!S(q,k)
and the proof is complete.

(ii)
The idea of the proof is similar to Proposition
5.19
(ii)
and we omit the proof.
∎

Some Goldberg coefficients
g(\tau,\eta)
are known to vanish even when

\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
(see
[
VBV16
]
), while the Weisner coefficients

w(\tau,\eta)
do not vanish whenever
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
.
The following Proposition describes two sufficient criteria for vanishing Goldberg
coefficients .

Proposition 5.24
.

Suppose
\tau,\eta\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
are such that
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
.

(i)

If
\des_{\eta}(\tau)=\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
and
\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert
is even then
g(\tau,\eta)=0
.

(ii)

If
|\tau|
is prime then
g(\tau,\eta)\neq 0
.

Proof.

(i)
By
[
Reu93
, Corollary 3.15]
, the coefficient
g(\tau,\eta)
of
K_{\tau}(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
vanishes if
q_{1}+\cdots+q_{r}+\des_{\eta}(\tau)+\asc_{\eta}(\tau)-r
is odd and
\des_{\eta}(\tau)=\asc_{\eta}(\tau)
. Since
\sum_{i=1}^{r}q_{i}=r+\plat_{\eta}(\tau)
and
\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert=\des_{\eta}(\tau)+\asc_{\eta}(\tau)+\plat_{\eta}(\tau)+1
, the conclusion follows.

(ii)
The idea of the proof is taken from
[
VBV16
]
. Let
p:=\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert
. By definition and Proposition
5.19
we have

\displaystyle g(\tau,\eta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\geq\tau\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\zeta}(\tau,\sigma)\,w(\sigma,\eta)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma\geq\tau\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{[\tau:\sigma]!}\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\sigma)-1}}{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert\binom{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-1}{\asc_{\eta}(\sigma)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{p-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)-1}}{p\binom{p-1}{\asc_{\eta}(\tau)}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\sigma>\tau\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{[\tau:\sigma]!}\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\sigma)-1}}{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert\binom{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-1}{\asc_{\eta}(\sigma)}}.

Since the number
[\tau:\sigma]!\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert\binom{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-1}{\asc_{\eta}(\sigma)}
never contains
p
as a
factor for any
\sigma>\tau
,
g(\tau,\eta)
is nonzero.
∎

Remark 5.25
.

It is a difficult problem to characterize vanishing Goldberg coefficients.
The criterion
(i)
from Proposition
5.24
above
does not cover all cases,
for example one can show that
g(\tau,\eta)=0
for

\tau=(\{3\},\{4\},\{2\},\{1\}),\eta=(\{1,2,3\},\{4\})
,
although the pair
(\tau,\eta)
does not satisfy the assumption of the criterion.
More information about Goldberg coefficients can be found in

[
Reu93
,
Tho82
]

and in particular
[
VBV16
, Section IV]
concerning the question of vanishing coefficients.

Example 5.26

(Goldberg coefficients and partial vanishing of cumulants)
.

We will write the ordered kernel set partition
\kappa(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
simply
as
i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{n}
.

1.

Take
\eta=12
. We compute
g(12,12)
. Now
\tau=\eta
so
m_{1}=1,m_{2}=2,r=2,q_{1}=q_{2}=1
. Hence
\des_{\eta}(\tau)=0,\asc_{\eta}(\tau)=1
and so

g(12,12)=\frac{1}{1!1!}\int_{-1}^{0}(1+x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2}.

Similarly we get
g(21,12)=-\frac{1}{2},g(12,21)=-\frac{1}{2},g(21,21)=\frac{1}{2}
and hence

\displaystyle K_{11}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}K_{12}(X,Y)-\frac{1}{2}K_{21}(X,Y),

\displaystyle K_{11}(X^{(2)},Y^{(1)})=-\frac{1}{2}K_{12}(X,Y)+\frac{1}{2}K_{21}(X,Y).

In the most popular spreadability systems, like the tensor, free, Boolean or
monotone spreadability systems, partitioned cumulants factorize, e.g.,

K_{12}(X,Y)=K_{1}(X)K_{1}(Y)
.
Hence we get
K_{11}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)})=K_{11}(X^{(2)},Y^{(1)})=0
.

2.

We then consider the case
\eta=112=(\{1,2\},\{3\})
. The Goldberg coefficients can be nonzero only when
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}
, so
\tau
is one of

112,221,123,132,213,231,312,321.

If
\tau=112
then
g(112,112)=\frac{1}{2}
by the same calculation as
g(12,12)
. If
\tau=221
then again
g(221,112)=-\frac{1}{2}
.
If
\tau=123
then
m_{1}=1,m_{2}=1,m_{3}=2,r=2,q_{1}=2,q_{2}=1
. So
