g(123,112)=\frac{1}{1!2!}\int_{-1}^{0}(1+x)P_{2}(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{12}.

If we take
\tau=132
then
m_{1}=1,m_{2}=2,m_{3}=1
, so
r=3,q_{1}=q_{2}=q_{3}=1
,
\asc=1,\des=1
. Thus we get

g(132,112)=\frac{1}{1!1!1!}\int_{-1}^{0}x(1+x)\,dx=-\frac{1}{6}.

If we take
\tau=231
then
m_{1}=2,m_{2}=1,m_{3}=1
. So
r=2,q_{1}=1,q_{2}=2
,
\asc=0,\des=1
. Hence

g(231,112)=\frac{1}{1!2!}\int_{-1}^{0}xP_{2}(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-1}^{0}x(2x+1)\,dx=\frac{1}{12}.

Similarly we can compute the remaining Goldberg coefficients and get

K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)},Z^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}K_{112}-\frac{1}{2}K_{221}+\frac{1}{12}K_{123}-\frac{1}{6}K_{132}\\
+\frac{1}{12}K_{213}+\frac{1}{12}K_{231}-\frac{1}{6}K_{312}+\frac{1}{12}K_{321},

where
X,Y,Z
are omitted for simplicity. We can see that (
5.8
) holds (now
\pi=111
):

\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=0,\qquad\frac{1}{12}-\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{12}+\frac{1}{12}-\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{12}=0.

Again if the cumulants factorize then
K_{112}=K_{221}=K_{11}(X,Y)K_{1}(Z)
and

K_{123}=\cdots=K_{321}=K_{1}(X)K_{1}(Y)K_{1}(Z)
, so the mixed cumulant

K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)},Z^{(2)})
vanishes.

Similarly one can compute
g(\tau,121)
for all
\tau
such that
\bar{\tau}\leq\overline{121}
and get

K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Z^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}K_{121}-\frac{1}{2}K_{212}-\frac{1}{6}K_{123}+\frac{1}{12}K_{132}\\
+\frac{1}{12}K_{213}+\frac{1}{12}K_{231}+\frac{1}{12}K_{312}-\frac{1}{6}K_{321}.

Now in the tensor, free or Boolean spreadability system the mixed cumulant
vanishes. However in the monotone spreadability system it does not:
K_{212}

vanishes identically since
212
is not a monotone partition (see Proposition

3.24
) and therefore, in the monotone case we have

K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Z^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}K_{121}(X,Y,Z)=\frac{1}{2}K_{11}(X,Z)K_{1}(Y)

which does not vanish in general.

The calculation of these cumulants in terms of moments is easier.

Example 5.27

(Weisner coefficients and partial vanishing of cumulants)
.

We can reuse some results from Example
5.26
.

1.

If we take
\tau=\eta=12
then
\asc_{\eta}(\tau)=1
and so

w(12,12)=\frac{(-1)^{2-1-1}}{2\binom{1}{1}}=\frac{1}{2}.

2.

Similarly if
\tau=21
and
\eta=12
then
m_{1}=2,m_{1}=1
so
\asc_{\eta}(\tau)=0
.
Therefore we get
w(21,12)=-\frac{1}{2}
. Similarly,
w(12,21)=-\frac{1}{2},w(21,21)=\frac{1}{2}
. So

\displaystyle K_{11}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{12}(X,Y)-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{21}(X,Y),

\displaystyle K_{11}(X^{(2)},Y^{(1)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{12}(X,Y)+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{21}(X,Y).

In factorizing spreadability systems we have

\varphi_{12}(X,Y)=\varphi_{21}(X,Y)=\varphi_{1}(X)\,\varphi_{1}(Y)
and hence
K_{11}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)})=K_{11}(X^{(2)},Y^{(1)})=0
.

3.

One can show that
w(112,112)=\frac{1}{2}=-w(221,112)
by the same calculation as
w(12,12)
and
w(21,12)
.
If
\tau=123,\eta=112
then
m_{1}=1,m_{2}=1,m_{3}=2
, so
\asc=1
and

w(123,112)=\frac{(-1)^{3-1-1}}{3\binom{2}{1}}=-\frac{1}{6}.

If we take
\tau=231
then
m_{1}=2,m_{2}=1,m_{3}=1
and so
\asc=0
. Hence

w(231,112)=\frac{(-1)^{3-0-1}}{3\binom{2}{0}}=\frac{1}{3}.

Similarly we can compute the remaining Weisner coefficients and get

K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)},Z^{(2)})=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{112}-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{221}-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{123}-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{132}\\
-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{213}+\frac{1}{3}\varphi_{231}-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{312}+\frac{1}{3}\varphi_{321},

where
X,Y,Z
are omitted for simplicity. We can see that (
5.9
) holds (now
\pi=111
):

\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}=0,\qquad-\frac{1}{6}-\frac{1}{6}-\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{6}+\frac{1}{3}=0.

In factorizing spreadability systems the mixed cumulant
K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(1)},Z^{(2)})
vanishes by using the factorization of partitioned moments.

4.

Similarly one can compute
w(\tau,121)
for all
\tau
such that
\bar{\tau}\leq\overline{121}
and get

K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Z^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{121}-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{212}-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{123}-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{132}\\
+\frac{1}{3}\varphi_{213}-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{231}+\frac{1}{3}\varphi_{312}-\frac{1}{6}\varphi_{321}.

In the tensor, free or Boolean spreadability system mixed cumulants
vanishes, but in the monotone case in general they don’t:
indeed
\varphi_{212}=\varphi(X)\varphi(Y)\varphi(Z)
while

\varphi_{121}=\varphi(XZ)\varphi(Y)
. Therefore, in the monotone case we have

K_{111}(X^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Z^{(1)})=\frac{1}{2}(\varphi_{121}-\varphi_{212})=\frac{1}{2}(\varphi(XZ)-\varphi(X)\varphi(Z))\varphi(Y)

which does not vanish in general.

6.
Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula and Lie polynomials

The material of the preceding section resembles some results from the theory of
free Lie algebras, cf. the book by C. Reutenauer

[
Reu93
]
already cited above.
In particular,
the
Goldberg coefficients

g(\tau,\eta)
from
Proposition
5.21
coincide with
the coefficients of the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff series, i.e.,

(6.1)

\log(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\dotsm e^{a_{n}})=\sum_{w:\text{word}}g_{w}w

when it is expanded in the ring of noncommutative formal power series,
see
[
Gol56
]
.
In this section we will provide a new probabilistic interpretation of the
coefficients of the CBH formula in terms of a certain variant of the tensor spreadability system.

6.1.
The unshuffle spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}

Given a
unital
algebra
\mathcal{A}

we introduce an operator-valued spreadability system with the following ingredients.

1.

Put
\varphi=\Id:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
.

2.

\mathcal{U}:=\otimes_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{A}
is the algebraic tensor
product, cf. Section
2.3.1
.

3.

\iota^{(j)}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{U}
is the natural embedding of
\mathcal{A}
into the
j^{\mathrm{th}}
component of
\mathcal{U}
:

\iota^{(j)}(X):=1^{\otimes(j-1)}\otimes X\otimes 1^{\otimes\infty}.

4.

\tilde{\varphi}:=\conc_{\infty}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{A}
is the concatenation product.
The resulting functional on
\mathcal{U}
gives rise to a
rearrangement of the letters

(6.2)

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(i_{1})}X_{2}^{(i_{2})}\cdots X_{n}^{(i_{n})}):=\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=X_{P_{1}}X_{P_{2}}\cdots X_{P_{k}},

where
\pi=\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})=(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{k})
and
the order of the letters inside each block is preserved according to (
2.1
).

Thus
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}
are “unshuffled” by
\tilde{\varphi}
accordingly to the upper indices
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
. For example

\tilde{\varphi}(X_{1}^{(5)}X_{2}^{(2)}X_{3}^{(3)}X_{4}^{(2)}X_{5}^{(3)}):=X_{2}X_{4}X_{3}X_{5}X_{1}.

Proposition 6.1
.

The triple
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(\mathcal{A})=(\mathcal{U},\conc_{\infty},(\iota^{(j)})_{j=1}^{\infty})
defined above constitutes a spreadability system for
(\mathcal{A},\Id)
.

Proof.

Clearly
\tilde{\varphi}\circ\iota^{(i)}=\Id
on

\mathcal{A}
. The symmetry condition (
2.3
) holds too since the value of

\tilde{\varphi}
only depends on the ordered kernel set partition of the upper indices.
∎

We call the triple
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
the
unshuffle spreadability system
.
The corresponding cumulants
K_{\pi}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}

satisfy (ordered) multiplicativity like the tensor cumulants, cf. Proposition
3.24

(i)
.

Proposition 6.2

(Multiplicativity of partitioned cumulants)
.

For
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, we have

K_{\pi}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=K_{|P_{1}|}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{P_{1}})\,K_{|P_{2}|}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{P_{2}})\dotsm K_{|P_{p}|}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{P_{p}}).

Proof.

For
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,

\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\dots,N.X_{n})\\
\begin{aligned} &=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in[N]}\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})\\
&=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in[N]}X_{\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\rfloor_{P_{1}}}X_{\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\rfloor_{P_{2}}}\cdots X_{\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\rfloor_{P_{p}}}\\
&=\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{k}\in[N]\\
k\in P_{1}\end{subarray}}X_{\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\rfloor_{P_{1}}}\biggr)\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{k}\in[N]\\
k\in P_{2}\end{subarray}}X_{\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\rfloor_{P_{2}}}\biggr)\,\cdots\,\biggl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{k}\in[N]\\
k\in P_{p}\end{subarray}}X_{\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})\rfloor_{P_{p}}}\biggr)\\
&=\tilde{\varphi}\biggl(\prod_{i\in P_{1}}N.X_{i}\biggr)\,\tilde{\varphi}\biggl(\prod_{i\in P_{2}}N.X_{i}\biggr)\,\cdots\,\tilde{\varphi}\biggl(\prod_{i\in P_{p}}N.X_{i}\biggr).\end{aligned}

We conclude by observing that the coefficient of
N^{p}
is the product of the
linear coefficients of the factors.
∎

After having established multiplicativity it suffices to compute

K_{n}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
.
Theorem
3.11
reads as follows.

Proposition 6.3
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have

K_{n}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\sum_{\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{k})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}\frac{(-1)^{|\pi|-1}}{|\pi|}X_{P_{1}}X_{P_{2}}\cdots X_{P_{k}}.

Example 6.4
.

The reader can easily verify that

\displaystyle K_{1}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X)

\displaystyle=X,

\displaystyle K_{2}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[X_{1},X_{2}],

\displaystyle K_{3}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}(X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}+X_{3}X_{2}X_{1})

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{1}{6}(X_{1}X_{3}X_{2}+X_{2}X_{1}X_{3}+X_{2}X_{3}X_{1}+X_{3}X_{1}X_{2}).

Remark 6.5
.

We will see in Section
6.2
that
K_{n}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}~(n\geq 2)
can be expressed
as a sum of commutators, i.e., a Lie polynomial.

We can now express the CBH formula
(
6.1
)
on
\mathcal{A}
in terms of cumulants.

Theorem 6.6

(CBH formula)
.

As formal power series on
\mathcal{A}
we have the identity

\log(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})\\
=\sum\frac{1}{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}K^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}_{p_{1}+p_{2}+\cdots+p_{n}}(\underbrace{a_{1},a_{1},\ldots,a_{1}}_{\text{$p_{1}$ times}},\underbrace{a_{2},a_{2},\ldots,a_{2}}_{\text{$p_{2}$ times}},\ldots,\underbrace{a_{n},a_{n},\ldots,a_{n}}_{\text{$p_{n}$ times}}).

where the sum runs over all
n
-tuples
(p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{n}

with the exception of the tuple
(0,0,\dots,0)
.

Proof.

First observe that the right hand side of the claimed identity
is the coefficient of
N
in the series

(6.3)

1+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{n},\\
(p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n})\neq(0,0,\dots,0)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}\tilde{\varphi}((N.a_{1})^{p_{1}}(N.a_{2})^{p_{2}}\cdots\,(N.a_{n})^{p_{n}})\\
\begin{aligned} &=\tilde{\varphi}(e^{N.a_{1}}e^{N.a_{2}}\cdots\,e^{N.a_{n}})\\
&=\tilde{\varphi}(e^{a_{1}^{(1)}+a_{1}^{(2)}+\cdots+a_{1}^{(N)}}e^{a_{2}^{(1)}+a_{2}^{(2)}+\cdots+a_{2}^{(N)}}\cdots\,e^{a_{n}^{(1)}+a_{n}^{(2)}+\cdots+a_{n}^{(N)}}).\end{aligned}

Our construction implies that
a_{i}^{(j)},j=1,2,3,\dots
mutually commute for
different
j
and so

e^{a_{i}^{(1)}+a_{i}^{(2)}+\cdots+a_{i}^{(N)}}=e^{a_{i}^{(1)}}e^{a_{i}^{(2)}}\cdots e^{a_{i}^{(N)}}.

Thus (
6.3
) can be factorized as follows:

\tilde{\varphi}(e^{a_{1}^{(1)}+a_{1}^{(2)}+\cdots+a_{1}^{(N)}}e^{a_{2}^{(1)}+a_{2}^{(2)}+\cdots+a_{2}^{(N)}}\cdots e^{a_{n}^{(1)}+a_{n}^{(2)}+\cdots+a_{n}^{(N)}})\\
=\tilde{\varphi}((e^{a_{1}^{(1)}}e^{a_{1}^{(2)}}\cdots e^{a_{1}^{(N)}})(e^{a_{2}^{(1)}}e^{a_{2}^{(2)}}\cdots e^{a_{2}^{(N)}})\cdots(e^{a_{n}^{(1)}}e^{a_{n}^{(2)}}\cdots e^{a_{n}^{(N)}})).

Now
\tilde{\varphi}
simply rearranges the factors
according to the upper index and
the last expression equals

(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})^{N}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})^{N}

\displaystyle=e^{N\log(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})}

\displaystyle=1+N\log(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})+\frac{N^{2}}{2!}(\log(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\cdots e^{a_{n}}))^{2}+\dots

and
we conclude by comparing the coefficient of
N
in this series with the one in
(
6.3
).

∎

Remark 6.7
.

Theorem
6.6
is similar to the well-known formula for the generating
function of multivariate cumulants from classical probability theory,

\log\IE[e^{z_{1}X_{1}+z_{2}X_{2}+\cdots+z_{n}X_{n}}]\\
=\sum\frac{z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots z_{n}^{p_{n}}}{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}K^{\mathrm{T}}_{p_{1}+p_{2}+\cdots+p_{n}}(\underbrace{X_{1},X_{1},\ldots,X_{1}}_{\text{$p_{1}$ times}},\underbrace{X_{2},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2}}_{\text{$p_{2}$ times}},\ldots,\underbrace{X_{n},X_{n},\ldots,X_{n}}_{\text{$p_{n}$ times}}),

where
X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathbb{C}
-valued classical random variables and
z_{1},z_{2},\dots,z_{n}
are commuting indeterminates.

Proposition 6.8
.

(i)

A sequence
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
of subalgebras of
\mathcal{A}
is

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
-independent if and only if the subalgebras
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\dots
commute mutually. This is obviously equivalent to

K_{2}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X,Y)=0
whenever
X\in\mathcal{A}_{i},Y\in\mathcal{A}_{j}

with
i\neq j
.

(ii)

For fixed
n\geq 2
, if
\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}\}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
splits into
two mutually commuting families then

K_{n}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=0.

Remark 6.9
.

1.

Additivity of Lie polynomials in commuting variables is well known,
see
[
Reu93
, p. 20]
.

2.

We have thus shown that

\text{$\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}$-independence $\iff$ commutativity $\iff$ vanishing of mixed cumulants}.

This example illustrates that in general vanishing of mixed cumulants does not imply exchangeability.

Proof of Proposition
6.8
.

(i)
Suppose that
(\mathcal{A}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
is
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
-independent. Let
\pi=\hat{1}_{2},\rho=(\{2\},\{1\})\in\mathcal{OP}_{2}
and let
X\in\mathcal{A}_{i},Y\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
for fixed
i>j
. Then
\kappa(i,j)=\rho
and

\varphi_{\pi}(X,Y)=XY,\qquad\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\rho}(X,Y)=YX,

and by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}
-independence these two must coincide, so
XY=YX
. This shows that
[\mathcal{A}_{i},\mathcal{A}_{j}]=0
. Conversely, if the
subalgebras
\mathcal{A}_{1},\mathcal{A}_{2},\dots
mutually commute then
for any
i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n}\in\mathbb{N}
, any
X_{k}\in\mathcal{A}_{i_{k}},k=1,2,\dots,n
and any
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
we have

\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\rho}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=(X_{P_{1}\cap R_{1}}X_{P_{1}\cap R_{2}}\cdots X_{P_{1}\cap R_{r}})\cdots(X_{P_{p}\cap R_{1}}X_{P_{p}\cap R_{2}}\cdots X_{P_{p}\cap R_{r}})

where
\rho=\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=(R_{1},R_{2},\dots,R_{r})
.
Here
X_{\emptyset}
is understood as the unit
\hat{1}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
Now if
\{X_{k}\mid k\in R_{i}\}
and
\{X_{k}\mid k\in R_{j}\}
mutually commute
for distinct
i,j
then for each
i=1,2,\dots,p
we have

X_{P_{i}\cap R_{1}}X_{P_{i}\cap R_{2}}\cdots X_{P_{i}\cap R_{r}}=X_{P_{i}},

which shows that
\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\rho}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2}\dots,X_{n})
.

(ii)
Suppose that
\{X_{i}\mid i\in I\}
and
\{X_{i}\mid i\in I^{c}\}
commute with
each other and
\emptyset\subsetneq I\subsetneq\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. Then, for
commuting indeterminates
z_{1},\dots,z_{n}
, we have

(6.4)

\log(e^{z_{1}X_{1}}e^{z_{2}X_{2}}\cdots e^{z_{n}X_{n}})=\log\Bigl(\prod_{i\in I}e^{z_{i}X_{i}}\Bigr)+\log\Bigl(\prod_{i\in I^{c}}e^{z_{i}X_{i}}\Bigr),

where the products
\prod_{i\in I},\prod_{i\in I^{c}}
preserve the natural
orders on
I,I^{c}
, respectively. On the other hand, by Theorem
6.6

we have

(6.5)

\log(e^{z_{1}X_{1}}e^{z_{2}X_{2}}\cdots e^{z_{n}X_{n}})\\
=\sum\frac{z_{1}^{p_{1}}z_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots z_{n}^{p_{n}}}{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}K^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}_{p_{1}+p_{2}+\cdots+p_{n}}(\underbrace{X_{1},X_{1},\ldots,X_{1}}_{\text{$p_{1}$ times}},\underbrace{X_{2},X_{2},\ldots,X_{2}}_{\text{$p_{2}$ times}},\ldots,\underbrace{X_{n},X_{n},\ldots,X_{n}}_{\text{$p_{n}$ times}}).

Comparing the coefficients of
z_{1}z_{2}\cdots z_{n}
in (
6.4
) with those
of (
6.5
) we conclude that
K_{n}^{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=0
.
∎

6.2.
Specialization to free algebras

We restrict the unshuffle spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}

to the case when the underlying algebra
\mathcal{A}
is a free algebra.
Our aim here is to show that in this case the cumulants are Lie
polynomials
[
Reu93
]
and to indicate further
connections to the theory of Hopf algebras.
This section was rewritten after
[
LNT20
]
was published,
to which we refer for further developments in this direction.

Let
{\mathbf{A}}
be an alphabet (a set), whose elements be denote by

a_{1},a_{2},\dots
.
Let
\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
be the unital free associative algebra generated by

{\mathbf{A}}
,
i.e., the unital polynomial ring in noncommuting indeterminates
{\mathbf{A}}
.
As a vector space it is spanned by the elements of the free monoid
{\mathbf{A}}^{\ast}

generated by
{\mathbf{A}}
endowed with the concatenation product.
An element
w=a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}\in{\mathbf{A}}^{\ast}
with
a_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}}
is called a
word

and
n
is its
length
.
The length of the unit
1
is understood to be 0.

Definition 6.10
.

The specialization
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{\shuffle}}(\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle)

of the unshuffle spreadability system
from Section
6.1
for the ncps
(\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle,\text{Id})

is called the
free Lie spreadability system
and denoted by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{FL}}({\mathbf{A}})
.

The free algebra
\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
also carries a Hopf algebra structure

[
CP21
]
.
Indeed, the
unshuffle coproduct

is the homomorphism
\delta:\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle\otimes\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle

for which every generator is a primitive element

\delta(a)=a\otimes 1+1\otimes a.

For an arbitrary word of length
n
it yields the sum over all unshuffles

\delta(w)=\sum_{I\sqcup J=[n]}w\rfloor_{I}\otimes w\rfloor_{J},

i.e., splittings into two subwords which keep the order.
This coproduct is obviously cocommutative.

Denote further by
\delta_{m}:\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle^{\otimes m}

its
(m-1)
-fold iteration
which is uniquely determined by the values

\delta_{m}(a)=\sum_{k=1}^{m}1^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes a\otimes 1^{\otimes(m-k)},\qquad a\in{\mathbf{A}}

on the generators (compare Definition
3.3
)
and in general yields the sum over all
m
-unshuffles, or equivalently, ordered
pseudopartitions (see Definition
A.17
)

(6.6)

\delta_{k}(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots)\in\mathcal{OPP}_{n}\\
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert=k\end{subarray}}a_{P_{1}}\otimes a_{P_{2}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{P_{k}}.

This reproduces the dot operation

N.a=\delta_{N}(a)\otimes 1^{\otimes\infty},\qquad a\in{\mathbf{A}}.

Let
\conc_{k}:\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle^{\otimes k}\to\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
be the multiplication map defined by

\conc_{k}(w_{1}\otimes w_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes w_{k})=w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{k},\qquad w_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}}^{*}

and more generally
for endomorphisms
f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{k}
of
\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
, we define the convolution

f_{1}\ast f_{2}\ast\cdots\ast f_{k}:=\conc_{k}\circ(f_{1}\otimes f_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{k})\circ\delta_{k}.

In particular, the convolution power
\Psi_{k}=\Id^{\ast k}
is called the

k
-dilation
or
Adams operation

[
CP21
, Definition 4.1.1]
,
so

(6.7)

\tilde{\varphi}(N.a_{1},N.a_{2},\dots,N.a_{n})=\Psi_{N}(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}).

The
counit
is the unique linear map
\epsilon:\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}
such that

\epsilon(1)=1,\qquad\epsilon(w)=0,\quad w\in{\mathbf{A}}^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}.

Definition 6.11
.

Let
\Pi:\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle\to\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
be the map defined by

\Pi=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}(\Id-\epsilon)^{\ast k},

where
\epsilon
is regarded as an endomorphism of
\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
.

The following proposition provides an explicit expression for
\Pi(w)

which shows that for any
a_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}}
the value
\Pi(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})

is a finite sum of words and thus indeed
\Pi(\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle)\subseteq\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
.

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\in{\mathbf{A}}
. Then

\Pi(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})=\sum_{\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots)\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}}\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert-1}}{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}a_{P_{1}}a_{P_{2}}\cdots a_{P_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}}.

Proof.

From (
6.6
) we have

\displaystyle\Pi(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}(\Id-\epsilon)^{\ast k}(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}(\conc_{k}\circ(\Id-\epsilon)^{\otimes k}\circ\delta_{k})(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k}\sum_{(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{k})\in\mathcal{OPP}_{n}}\!\!(\Id-\epsilon)(a_{P_{1}})(\Id-\epsilon)(a_{P_{2}})\cdots(\Id-\epsilon)(a_{P_{k}}).

Note that
(\Id-\epsilon)(1)=0
and
(\Id-\epsilon)(w)=w
for

w\in{\mathbf{A}}^{\ast}\setminus\{1\}
. If
P_{i}=\emptyset
for some
i
, then
the product
(\Id-\epsilon)(a_{P_{1}})(\Id-\epsilon)(a_{P_{2}})\cdots(\Id-\epsilon)(a_{P_{k}})
vanishes.
Therefore only proper ordered set partitions contribute to the sum, and in
particular the sum extends over
k\leq n
only. This proves the claim.
∎

Combining Proposition
6.12
with Proposition
6.3
we
conclude the following identity.

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\in{\mathbf{A}}
and let
K^{\mathrm{FL}}_{\pi}
be the cumulants associated to the spreadability system
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{FL}}
. Then

\Pi(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})=K^{\mathrm{FL}}_{n}(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}).

Remark 6.14
.

1.

The space of
Lie polynomials

\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{A}})
is the smallest subspace
of
\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
that contains
{\mathbf{A}}
and is closed with respect to the Lie bracket

[X,Y]=XY-YX
. It is well known that
\Pi
is a Lie projector, i.e.,

\mathcal{L}({\mathbf{A}})=\Pi(\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle)

[
Reu93
, Theorem 3.7]
.
So in the above setting cumulants with entries from
{\mathbf{A}}
are exactly Lie
polynomials.

2.

The values at words of higher order are given by convolution
powers of
\Pi
, i.e.,

\Pi_{k}=\frac{1}{k!}\Pi^{\ast k},\qquad k\in\mathbb{N}

(see
[
Reu93
]
),
and we can show that

\Pi_{k}(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n})=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}\\
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert=k\end{subarray}}K^{\mathrm{FL}}_{\pi}(a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{n}),\qquad a_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}},~i\in[n].

is the coefficient of
N^{k}
appearing in
\tilde{\varphi}((N.a_{1})(N.a_{2})\cdots(N.a_{n}))
by Theorem
3.11
and Proposition
3.16
. In other words,

\tilde{\varphi}((N.a_{1})(N.a_{2})\cdots(N.a_{n}))=\sum_{k=1}^{n}N^{k}\Pi_{k}(a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{n}),\qquad a_{i}\in{\mathbf{A}},~i\in[n].

Now the combination of Theorem
6.6
and Theorem
6.13

reproduces the CBH formula

(6.8)

\log(e^{a_{1}}e^{a_{2}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{n},\\
(p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n})\neq(0,0,\dots,0)\end{subarray}}\Pi\left(\frac{a_{1}^{p_{1}}a_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{p_{n}}}{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}\right);

see
[
Reu93
, Lemma 3.10]
.

6.3.
Coefficients of the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula

We adopt the notations and definitions in the previous subsection.
The coefficients of the Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff formula, when written out in
the monomial basis, were first computed using generating functions in

[
Gol56
]
and are
called
Goldberg coefficients
; a combinatorial proof
can be found in
[
Reu93
, Theorem 3.11]
.
In the following we give another derivation of the Goldberg coefficients;
see also a recent proof using the theory of noncommutative symmetric functions
[
FPT16
]
.

Lemma 6.15
.

Let
\eta=(E_{1},E_{2},\dots,E_{e})
be an interval partition of
[n]
where the blocks are in canonical order, i.e. if
s<t
then
i<j
for all
i\in E_{s},j\in E_{t}
.
Then for any
X_{i}\in\mathbb{C}\langle{\mathbf{A}}\rangle
and any
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, we have

\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\eta}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})=\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n}).

Proof.

The statement holds by definition because every block
P\in\pi
is the concatenation
of
P\cap E_{1},P\cap E_{2},\dots,P\cap E_{e}
.
∎

Remark 6.16
.

Thus a sequence of distinct letters satisfies some partial independence, but it is not
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{FL}}
-independent. Indeed,
(a_{1},a_{2})\in{\mathbf{A}}\times{\mathbf{A}}
is
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{FL}}
-independent if and only if
a_{1}=a_{2}
by Proposition
6.8
.

Theorem 6.17

(
[
Gol56
,
Reu93
]
)
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}
be distinct letters from the alphabet
{\mathbf{A}}
, let
r\in\mathbb{N}
and

q_{j},i_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
for
j\in[r]
such that
i_{j}\neq i_{j+1}
for
j\in[r-1]
.
Then the coefficient of the monomial
a_{i_{1}}^{q_{1}}a_{i_{2}}^{q_{2}}\cdots a_{i_{r}}^{q_{r}}
appearing in
\log(e^{a_{1}}\cdots e^{a_{n}})
is given by

\frac{1}{q_{1}!q_{2}!\cdots q_{r}!}\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des(\underline{i})}(1+x)^{\asc(\underline{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}P_{q_{j}}(x)\,dx,

where
P_{q}(x)
are the homogeneous Euler polynomials already encountered in Proposition
5.21
.

Proof.

In order for a monomial
v=a_{i_{1}}^{q_{1}}a_{i_{2}}^{q_{2}}\cdots a_{i_{r}}^{q_{r}}
to
occur as a term in
\Pi(a_{1}^{p_{1}}a_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{p_{n}})
it necessarily
has to be a rearrangement of the word (= permutation of the multiset)

w=a_{1}^{p_{1}}a_{2}^{p_{2}}\dotsm a_{n}^{p_{n}}
,
since the projector in the CBH formula (
6.8
) does not
change multiplicities and therefore
every letter must occur the same number of times in
v
and
w
.

Thus
p_{k}=\sum_{j\in B_{k}}q_{j}
where
B_{k}=\{j:i_{j}=k\}
.
Let
p=p_{1}+p_{2}+\dots+p_{n}
be the total length of
w

and
\eta=(A_{1},A_{2},\dots,A_{n})\in\mathcal{OI}_{p}
be the ordered interval partition
corresponding to the composition
(p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{n})
, i.e.,

A_{j}=\{p_{1}+p_{2}+\dots+p_{j-1}+1,p_{1}+p_{2}+\dots+p_{j-1}+2,\dots,p_{1}+p_{2}+\dots+p_{j}\}

and
\left\lvert A_{j}\right\rvert=p_{j}
.

From Theorem
6.13
we infer

\displaystyle\Pi(a_{1}^{p_{1}}a_{2}^{p_{2}}\cdots a_{n}^{p_{n}})

\displaystyle=K^{\mathrm{FL}}_{p}(\underbrace{a_{1},a_{1},\ldots,a_{1}}_{\text{$p_{1}$ times}},\underbrace{a_{2},a_{2},\ldots,a_{2}}_{\text{$p_{2}$ times}},\ldots,\underbrace{a_{n},a_{n},\ldots,a_{n}}_{\text{$p_{n}$ times}})

\displaystyle=:K_{p}^{\mathrm{FL}}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}}).

Note that by Lemma
6.15
we have

\varphi_{\pi}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}})=\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\eta}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}})

for any
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{p}

and
thus

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{FL}}_{p}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{p}}\varphi_{\sigma}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}})\,\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\hat{1}_{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{p}}\varphi_{\sigma\curlywedge\eta}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}})\,\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\hat{1}_{p})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{p}\\
\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta}\end{subarray}}\varphi_{\tau}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}})\,w(\tau,\eta)

by Proposition
5.19
.
Thus in order to determine the coefficient of
v
we must
collect all ordered set partitions
\tau
such that

T_{v}=\{\tau\in\mathcal{OP}_{p}:\bar{\tau}\leq\bar{\eta},\varphi_{\tau}(a_{1}^{\dots p_{1}},a_{2}^{\dots p_{2}},\dots,a_{n}^{\dots p_{n}})=v\}

and then sum up the corresponding values of
w(\tau,\eta)
.
Let us now investigate the structure of this set.
First note that
T_{v}
is an order ideal: if
\tau\in T_{v}
and
\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau
,
then
\tau^{\prime}\in T_{v}
, because every block of
\tau
contains only repetitions
of one letter and further refinement of
\tau
leaves the end result

v
invariant.
Moreover
T_{v}
is the disjoint union of the principal ideals

\downset\tau_{0}=\{\tau:\tau\leq\tau_{0}\}

where
\tau_{0}\in T_{v}
is maximal.

The maximal partition
\tau_{0}
arises as follows:
After application of
\varphi_{\tau}
for
\tau\in T_{v}

each factor
a_{j}^{p_{j}}
is divided into pieces

a_{j}^{q_{l}}
,
l\in B_{j}
and the number of such subdivisions is
the multinomial coefficient

\binom{p_{j}}{q_{l}:l\in B_{j}}.

These subdivisions are in one-to-one correspondence with
ordered set partitions of
A_{j}
which, pieced together in the order of
j
,
give rise to a maximal ordered set partition
\tau_{0}\in T_{v}
.
Thus in total there are as many maximal ordered set partitions
as there are subdivisions, namely

\binom{p_{1}}{q_{l}:l\in B_{1}}\binom{p_{2}}{q_{l}:l\in B_{2}}\dotsm\binom{p_{n}}{q_{l}:l\in B_{n}}=\frac{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}{q_{1}!q_{2}!\cdots q_{r}!}.

By Proposition
A.23
the principal
ideal
\downset\tau_{0}
generated by a maximal ordered set partition
\tau_{0}

is isomorphic to

\mathcal{OP}_{q_{1}}\times\mathcal{OP}_{q_{2}}\times\dotsm\mathcal{OP}_{q_{r}}

and in particular, all principal ideals are isomorphic.
Moreover the number of ascents is
\asc_{\eta}(\tau)=\asc(\underline{i})

and does not depend on the choice of
\tau\in T_{v}
.
Thus all ideals
\downset\tau_{0}
deliver the same contribution
and as a consequence of the discussion above we are left with

\sum_{\tau\in T_{v}}w(\tau,\eta)=\frac{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}{q_{1}!q_{2}!\cdots q_{r}!}\sum_{\tau\leq\tau_{0}}w(\tau,\eta)

for one fixed maximal element
\tau_{0}\in T_{v}
.

A canonical representative
\tau_{0}
is obtained by concatenating
consecutive subintervals of length
q_{j}
from
A_{i_{j}}
,
j=1,2,\dots,r
.
Let us now turn to the value of
w(\tau,\eta)
.
As seen above, the numbers of both ascents and descents of
\tau

only depend on those of the sequence
\underline{i}

and since
\underline{i}
has no plateaux we infer from (
5.10
)
that
r=\des(\underline{i})+\asc(\underline{i})+1
.
On the other hand, if we denote by
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\dots,\tau_{r})

the image of
\tau\in\downset\tau_{0}

under the isomorphism of Proposition
A.23

then the first exponent in formula (
5.11
) of Proposition
5.19
becomes

\left\lvert\tau\right\rvert-\asc_{\eta}(\tau)-1=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left\lvert\tau_{j}\right\rvert-\asc(\underline{i})-1=\sum_{j=1}^{r}(\left\lvert\tau_{j}\right\rvert-1)+\des(\underline{i})

and thus

w(\tau,\eta)=\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des(\underline{i})}(1+x)^{\asc(\underline{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}x^{\left\lvert\tau_{j}\right\rvert-1}\,dx

and summing over the cartesian product yields the total value

\sum_{\tau\in T_{v}}w(\tau,\eta)=\frac{p_{1}!p_{2}!\cdots p_{n}!}{q_{1}!q_{2}!\cdots q_{r}!}\int_{-1}^{0}x^{\des(\underline{i})}(1+x)^{\asc(\underline{i})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\left(\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{OP}_{q_{j}}}x^{\left\lvert\rho\right\rvert-1}\right)dx.

Finally note that

\sum_{\rho\in\mathcal{OP}_{q}}x^{\left\lvert\rho\right\rvert-1}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathcal{P}_{q}}\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert!\,x^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-1}=\sum_{k=1}^{q}k!\,S(q,k)x^{k-1}.

is indeed the homogeneous Euler polynomial as claimed,
see Remark
5.23
.
∎

Remark 6.18
.

The authors were not able to prove Theorem
6.17

as a corollary of Propositions
5.5
and (
5.21
) although Goldberg coefficients appear in both formulas.

7.
Central limit theorem

Cumulants provide a natural framework to understand central limit theorems.
Speicher and Waldenfels studied central limit theorems in a general setting
of noncommutative probability assuming a certain
singleton
condition

[
SW94
]
(see also
[
AHO98
]
).
In this section we will see that a similar approach also applies
in the setting of a spreadability system, provided that an appropriate
singleton condition holds.

Definition 7.1
.

(i)

An element
k\in[n]
is called a singleton of
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
if
\{k\}\in\pi
.

(ii)

Let
\mathcal{S}=(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
be a
spreadability system for a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
.
We will say that the
singleton condition holds for
\mathcal{S}
if

\varphi_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})=0
for every tuple
(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{n})
and
every partition
\pi
containing a singleton
\{k\}
such that

\varphi(X_{k})=0
.

Under this assumption we can show the following type of central limit theorem.

Theorem 7.2
.

Assume that a spreadability system
(\mathcal{U},\tilde{\varphi},(\iota^{(i)})_{i\geq 1})
for a
\mathcal{B}
-ncps
(\mathcal{A},\varphi)
satisfies the singleton condition.
Assume
\varphi(X)=0
and let
Y_{N}:=\frac{N.X}{\sqrt{N}}
. Then, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\varphi_{\rho}(Y_{N},Y_{N},\dots,Y_{N})=\begin{cases}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}^{(2)}\\
\pi\leq\rho\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!}\varphi_{\pi}(X,X,\ldots,X),&n\text{~is even},\\
0,&n\text{~is odd}.\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{OP}_{n}^{(2)}
is the set of pair ordered set partitions, i.e. every block of
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}^{(2)}
contains exactly 2 entries.

Proof.

Note that the following holds: if
\pi
has a singleton at
k
and
\varphi(X_{k})=0
, then

K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})=0.

This holds because
K_{\pi}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})
is the coefficient of
N
in
\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\ldots,N.X_{n})
, and

\varphi_{\pi}(N.X_{1},N.X_{2},\ldots,N.X_{n})=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}\in[n]}\varphi_{\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})}(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{n})=0

because each

\pi\curlywedge\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
has a singleton at
k
.

Now multilinearity and extensivity of cumulants imply

K_{\pi}(Y_{N},Y_{N},\ldots,Y_{N})=N^{-\frac{n}{2}+\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}K_{\pi}(X,X,\ldots,X).

If
\pi
has a singleton, this is zero. If
\pi
does not have a singleton nor
\pi
is not a pair ordered set partition, then
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert<\frac{n}{2}
. Therefore,

\lim_{N\to\infty}K_{\pi}(Y_{N},Y_{N},\ldots,Y_{N})=\begin{cases}K_{\pi}(X,X,\ldots,X),&\text{if~$n$ is even and~}\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}^{(2)},\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

If
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}^{(2)}
, then
K_{\pi}(X,X,\ldots,X)=\varphi_{\pi}(X,X,\ldots,X)

from Theorem
3.11
because the expectations
\varphi_{\sigma}(X,X,\ldots,X)
all
vanish for
\sigma<\pi
from the singleton condition. Finally, from Theorem

3.11
, we obtain the conclusion.
∎

Thus the use of cumulants simplifies the proof of the central limit
theorem. It may happen that the moments of the limit distribution are not
uniquely determined only by the variance of
X
alone, because in
general
\varphi_{\pi}(X,X,\dots,X)
cannot be written in terms of
\varphi(X^{2})
.
For example, the limit distribution for the c-monotone spreadability system is
characterized by the moments

\lim_{N\to\infty}\varphi(Y_{N}^{n})=\begin{cases}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}}\frac{1}{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!}\alpha^{2\left\lvert\mathrm{Outer}(\pi)\right\rvert}\beta^{2\left\lvert\mathrm{Inner}(\pi)\right\rvert},&n\text{~is even},\\
0,&n\text{~is odd},\end{cases}

where
\alpha^{2}=\varphi(X^{2})
,
\beta^{2}=\psi(X^{2})
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}
is the set
of monotone pair partitions; the reader is referred to Theorems 4.7, 5.1 of

[
Has11
]
. The limit moments are not uniquely
determined by
\alpha^{2}
but also depend on the second linear map
\psi
.
A natural condition to ensure uniqueness is a calculation rule (Definition
2.10
); then the limit distribution of the central limit theorem is determined only by the variance and by the constants
s(\bar{\pi};\pi)
. More precisely, we deduce from the calculation rule (
2.13
) that
\varphi_{\pi}(X,X,\ldots,X)=s(\bar{\pi};\pi)\alpha^{2\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
for
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}^{(2)}
provided
\varphi(X)=0
, where
\alpha^{2}=\varphi(X^{2})
. The limit moments can therefore be written as

\lim_{N\to\infty}\varphi_{\rho}(Y_{N},Y_{N},\dots,Y_{N})=\begin{cases}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}^{(2)}\\
\pi\leq\rho\end{subarray}}\frac{s(\bar{\pi};\pi)}{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert!}\alpha^{2\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert},&n\text{~is even},\\
0,&n\text{~is odd}.\end{cases}

8.
Open Problems

1.

Find a unified proof of the occurrence of Goldberg coefficients in Theorem
6.17
and Proposition
5.5
.

2.

Find the values of the Möbius function on the poset of monotone partitions.

3.

Compute the cumulants for the V-monotone spreadability system and compare
them with the operadic cumulants of Jekel and Liu
[
JL20
]
.

4.

Define a notion of multifaced spreadability systems and the associated cumulants, cf. Remark
2.7
.

Appendix A
Ordered set partitions

A.1.
Set partitions

Let
\mathbb{N}
denote the set of natural numbers
\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
, and let
[n]

denote the finite set
\{1,2,\ldots,n\}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
.

Definition A.1

(Set Partitions)
.

A
set partition
, or simply partition, of a finite set
A
is a set of mutually disjoint subsets

\pi=\{P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{k}\}
such that
\cup_{i=1}^{k}P_{i}=A
.
The number
k
is the
size
of the partition and denoted by
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert
.
The elements
P\in\pi
are called
blocks
of
\pi
.
The set of partitions of
A
is denoted by
\mathcal{P}_{A}.

We are mostly concerned with the case
A=[n]

and in this case
\mathcal{P}_{[n]}
is abbreviated to
\mathcal{P}_{n}

and called (set)
partitions of order
n
.
As is well known there is a one-to-one
correspondence between set partitions
\pi
of
[n]
and equivalence relations
on
[n]
by defining for
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
i,j\in[n]
the
relation
i\sim_{\pi}j
to hold if and only if there is a block
P\in\pi

such that both
i,j\in P
.

The set partitions of fixed order
n
form a lattice under
refinement
order
:

Definition A.2

(Refinement Order)
.

For partitions
\pi
and
\sigma
we write
\pi\leq\sigma
if for any block

P\in\pi
, there exists a block
S\in\sigma
such that
P\subseteq S
.
In other words, every block of
\sigma
is a union of blocks of
\pi
.
The minimal element of this lattice is

\hat{0}_{n}=\{\{1\},\{2\},\dots,\{n\}\}
and the maximal element

\hat{1}_{n}=\{[n]\}
.

We proceed with the description of several classes of set partitions.

Definition A.3

(Classes of Set Partitions)
.

Let
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
be a set partition.

(i)

Two (distinct) blocks
B
and
B^{\prime}\in\pi
are said to be
crossing
if
there are elements
i<i^{\prime}<j<j^{\prime}
such that
i,j\in B
and
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\in B^{\prime}
.

\pi
is called
noncrossing
if there are no crossing blocks,
i.e., if there is no
quadruple of elements
i<j<k<l
s.t.
i\sim_{\pi}k
,
j\sim_{\pi}l
and

i\not\sim_{\pi}j
. The noncrossing partitions of order
n
form a sublattice
which we denote by
\mathcal{NC}_{n}
.

(ii)

Two blocks
B,B^{\prime}
of a noncrossing partition
\pi
are said to form a

nesting
if there are
i,j\in B
such that
i<k<j
for any
k\in B^{\prime}
.
In this case
B
is called the
outer block
of the nesting and

B^{\prime}
is called the
inner block
of the nesting.

(iii)

A block
B
of a noncrossing partition
\pi
is
inner
if

B
is the inner block of a nesting of
\pi
.
If this is not the case
B
is called an
outer block
of
\pi
.
The set of inner blocks of
\pi
is denoted by
\mathrm{Inner}(\pi)

and the set of outer blocks of
\pi
by
\mathrm{Outer}(\pi)
.

(iv)

An
interval partition
is a partition
\pi
for which every block is an interval.
Equivalently, this means that
\pi
is noncrossing and has no nestings.
The set of interval partitions of
[n]
is denoted by
\mathcal{I}_{n}
.

Analogous definitions apply to any finite totally ordered set
A
and the corresponding sets of partitions are denoted
\mathcal{P}_{A}
,
\mathcal{NC}_{A}
,
\mathcal{I}_{A}
etc.

Examples of partitions are shown in Fig.
1
.

a noncrossing partition

an interval partition

Figure 1.
Examples of partitions

The lattice of interval partitions plays a central role in this paper
and has a particularly simple structure.

Proposition A.4
.

The lattice of interval partitions
\mathcal{I}_{n}
is anti-isomorphic
to the Boolean lattice
\mathcal{B}_{n-1}
via the lattice anti-isomorphism

(I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{p})\mapsto\{r_{1},r_{2},\ldots,r_{p-1}\}\subseteq[n-1],

where the blocks
I_{i}
are uniquely determined by their maximal elements
r_{i}
;
note that always
r_{p}=n
.

The following construction inverts the previous bijection
in a certain sense.

Definition A.5
.

Fix a number
n\in\mathbb{N}
and a subset
A\subseteq[n]
.

(i)

Among all noncrossing partitions
containing
A^{c}
as an outer block there is a maximal one,
which we denote by
\outncmax(A)
. Removing
A^{c}
we obtain
an interval partition of
A
which we denote by
\outintmax(A)
;
in other words, the blocks of
\outintmax(A)
consist
of the maximal contiguous subintervals of
A
.
Yet in another interpretation, the blocks of
\outintmax(A)

are the connected components of the graph induced on
A

from the integer line.

(ii)

Among all noncrossing partitions
containing
A^{c}
as a block there is a maximal one,
which we denote by
\ncmax(A)
. Removing
A^{c}
we obtain
a noncrossing partition of
A

which we denote by
\intmax(A)
;
in other words, the blocks of
\intmax(A)
consist
of the maximal contiguous subintervals of
A
when we consider
it on the circle, i.e.,
the blocks of
\intmax(A)

are the connected components of the graph induced on
A

from the Cayley graph of
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
.

See Figure
2
for examples.

\outncmax(A)

\ncmax(A)

Figure 2.
The partitions
\outncmax(A)
and
\ncmax(A)

for
A=\{1,3,4,6,8,9\}
(fat).

Remark A.6
.

1.

Construction
(i)

occurs in some examples, see, e.g., Examples
4.6
and
4.7
.
It gives rise to the unshuffle coproduct of Ebrahimi-Fard and Patras

[
EFP15
]
.

2.

Construction
(ii)
occurs in the recursion
(
4.1
) for free cumulants and
Example
4.8
.

The lattices considered so far have the following structural property.
It is easy to see for both
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}
while for
\mathcal{NC}_{n}
it is proved in

[
Spe94
]
.

Proposition A.7
.

Let
P_{n}
be one of
\mathcal{P}_{n}
,
\mathcal{NC}_{n}
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}
.
Then for any pair of elements
\sigma,\pi\in P_{n}
such that

\sigma\leq\pi

there are uniquely determined numbers
k_{j}
such that
the interval
[\sigma,\pi]
is isomorphic (as a lattice) to
the direct product

P_{1}^{k_{1}}\times P_{2}^{k_{2}}\times\dotsm\times P_{n}^{k_{n}}.

A.2.
Ordered set partitions

Definition A.8

(Ordered set partitions)
.

(i)

An
ordered set partition
of a set
A
is a sequence

(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{p})
of distinct blocks such that
\{P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{p}\}

is a set partition of
A
. In other words, it is a set partition
with a total ordering of its blocks.
The set of ordered set partitions of
A
is denote by
\mathcal{OP}_{A}
and
\mathcal{OP}_{[n]}

is abbreviated to
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.

(ii)

Ordered set partitions with a fixed number
p
of blocks
are in bijection with surjective functions from
[n]
to
[p]
.
This bijection is implemented by identifying
an ordered set partition

\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}

with the function mapping an element
i\in[n]

to the label
k
of the block
P_{k}
such that
i\in P_{k}
,
which we denote by
\pi(i):=k
.
This function can be represented by
a
packed word

[
NT06
]
,
i.e., the sequence of the images
\pi(1)\pi(2)\dots\pi(n)
.
When
\pi
consists of singletons only, i.e.,
\#P_{i}=1

for all
i\in[p]
then
\pi
can be identified with
a permutation of
[n]
, and the notations introduced above are consistent
with the familiar notations for permutations.

Remark A.9
.

Ordered set partitions are also known under the name of
set compositions
, see,
e.g.,
[
BZ09
]
,
pseudopermutations

[
KLN+00
]
.
In particular, packed words can be represented as multiset
permutations and this point of view is crucial in Section
5
.

Definition A.10
.

(i)

The
ordered kernel set partition

\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
of a multiindex

(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
is defined as follows.
First, pick the smallest value, say
p_{1}
,
from
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}
and define the block
P_{1}=\{k\in[n]\mid i_{k}=p_{1}\}
.
Next, pick the second smallest value
p_{2}
from
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n}
and define the block
P_{2}=\{k\in[n]\mid i_{k}=p_{2}\}
. By
repeating this procedure, we obtain an ordered set partition
(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots)
,
which we denote by
\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
.

(ii)

The
kernel set partition

\bar{\kappa}(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})
of a sequence
of indices is defined as the underlying set partition

\overline{\kappa(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{n})}
of the corresponding ordered kernel set partition.
In other words, it is the equivalence relation such that by
p\sim q
if and
only if
i_{p}=i_{q}
.

Definition A.11

(Order Dropping Map)
.

Let
\pi\mapsto\bar{\pi}
be the map from
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
onto
\mathcal{P}_{n}
which
drops the
order on blocks, that is,

(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots)\mapsto\{P_{1},P_{2},\ldots\}.

We say that an ordered set partition is
in canonical order

if the blocks are sorted in ascending order according to their minimal
elements.

It will be convenient to transfer as much structure as possible from ordinary
set partitions to ordered set partitions when no confusion can arise.
For example, the notation
P\in\pi
indicates that
P
is a block of
\bar{\pi}
.
Let us next introduce a natural partial order relation on
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.

Definition A.12

(Refinement Order)
.

Given two ordered set partitions
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})
and
\sigma=(S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{s})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
we define the order relation
\pi\leq\sigma
by the following requirements:

(i)

\bar{\pi}\leq\bar{\sigma}
as set partitions.

(ii)

If
P_{i}\subseteq S_{k},P_{j}\subseteq S_{l}
for
i<j
, then
k\leq l
.

Remark A.13
.

In other words,
\pi\leq\sigma
if every block of
\sigma
is a union of
a contiguous sequence of blocks of
\pi
.
The elements dominated by a given ordered set partition
\sigma
are obtained
as follows:

1.

pick a label
i

2.

split the block with label
i
into two

3.

label one of the pieces with
i
and the other one with
i+1

4.

increment by one all other labels larger than
i
.

See Figure
3
for examples.

.

(\mathcal{OP}_{A},\leq)
a poset (but not a lattice as will be seen

\mathcal{OP}_{3}
is shown in Figure

;

Note that
\pi\leq\sigma
implies

\bar{\pi}\leq\bar{\sigma}
, but not vice versa.

We consider the following subclasses of ordered set partitions on
[n]
.

Definition A.14

(Classes of Ordered Set Partitions)
.

(i)

An ordered set partition
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
is called
noncrossing

if the underlying set partition
\bar{\pi}
has this property.
The set of ordered noncrossing partitions of
[n]
is denoted by
\mathcal{ONC}_{n}
.
Outer blocks and inner blocks of an ordered noncrossing partition are defined according to the case of noncrossing partitions.

(ii)

An
interval ordered set partition
of
[n]
is an ordered set partition
\pi
such that
\bar{\pi}\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
. The set of interval ordered set partitions of
[n]
is denoted by
\mathcal{OI}_{n}
.

(iii)

A
noncrossing ordered set partition

\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert})
is called
monotone partition
if
for every nesting the outer
block precedes the inner block;
in other words, the order of the blocks implements a linearization of the partial order given by the nesting relation.
The set of monotone partitions is denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{n}
.

.

shows some examples of monotone and

.

Remark A.16
.

Interval partitions are characterized by the property
that the canonical order defined in Definition
A.11

extends to the entire blocks, i.e.,
the blocks of an interval partition
\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{p}\}
can be uniquely ordered so that
i<j
whenever
i\in I_{s}
,
j\in I_{t}
,
s<t
.
This ordering provides a natural embedding
\mathcal{I}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{OI}_{n}

and we may write
(I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{p})\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
rather than

\{I_{1},I_{2},\dots,I_{p}\}\in\mathcal{I}_{n}
.

In section
6
, we need the following extension of ordered set partitions.

Definition A.17
.

An
ordered pseudopartition
of
[n]
is a sequence

(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})
of disjoint subsets of
[n]

such that
\cup_{i=1}^{p}P_{i}=[n]
with empty blocks allowed.
We keep the notation
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert=p
for the length,
now including empty blocks.
The set of ordered pseudopartitions of
[n]
is denoted by
\mathcal{OPP}_{n}
.

Lemma A.18

(see, e.g.,
[
BS96
]
)
.

The poset of ordered set partitions
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
is isomorphic to the poset of nonempty chains in the boolean lattice
2^{n}
with the reverse refinement order, i.e., a chain
\emptyset=A_{0}\subset A_{1}\subset\dotsm\subset A_{k}=[n]
is smaller than a chain
\emptyset=B_{0}\subset B_{1}\subset\dotsm\subset B_{l}=[n]
if it is finer, i.e., as sets
\{B_{0},B_{1},\dots,B_{l}\}\subseteq\{A_{0},A_{1},\dots,A_{k}\}
.

Proof.

The bijection is given by the map

\Phi_{n}:(A_{1}\subset A_{2}\subset\dotsm\subset A_{k})\mapsto(A_{1},A_{2}\setminus A_{1},A_{3}\setminus A_{2},\dots,A_{k}\setminus A_{k-1}).

∎

Moreover, when the empty chain is added,

\mathcal{OP}_{n}
becomes a lattice isomorphic to the face lattice
of the
permutohedron

[
BS96
,
Ton97
]
.
With this alternative picture it is now easy to see a join
semilattice structure, namely the join operation corresponds
to the intersection of chains in the chain poset.

We denoted by
\hat{1}_{n}:=([n])
the unique maximal ordered set partition.
On the other hand there are several minimal elements, namely
all permutations of the minimal set partition

\hat{0}_{n}:=(\{1\},\{2\},\dots,\{n\})
.
Consequently there is no meet operation, but we define the following associative but noncommutative replacement.
It turns
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
into a
band
, i.e., a semigroup in which
every element is an idempotent; however it is not a skew lattice.

Definition A.19
.

(i)

For an ordered set partition
\sigma=(S_{1},S_{2},\dots,S_{s})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}

and a nonempty subset

P\subseteq[n]
, let the
restriction

\sigma\rfloor_{P}\in\mathcal{OP}_{P}

be the ordered set partition
(P\cap S_{1},P\cap S_{2},\dots,P\cap S_{s})\in\mathcal{OP}_{P}
, where empty sets are dropped; see
[
PS08
]
, where this
operation arises in the context of Hopf algebras.

(ii)

For
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{p}),\sigma=(S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{s})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
we define the
quasi-meet operation

\pi\curlywedge\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
to be
(P_{1}\cap S_{1},P_{1}\cap S_{2},\ldots,P_{1}\cap S_{s},P_{2}\cap S_{1},P_{2}\cap S_{2},\ldots,P_{2}\cap S_{s},\dots,P_{p}\cap S_{s})
, where empty
sets are skipped. In other words, the quasi-meet
is the concatenation of the restrictions
\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\sigma\rfloor_{P_{1}}\sigma\rfloor_{P_{2}}\dotsm\sigma\rfloor_{P_{p}}
.

Remark A.20
.

The quasi-meet operation is associative and coincides with the multiplication operation
in the Solomon-Tits algebra of the symmetric group

[
Sol76
]
which resurfaced recently
in the domain of Markov chains

[
Bro00
,
Bro04
]

and Hopf algebras of noncommutative quasi-symmetric functions

[
BZ09
]
.

Proposition A.21
.

(i)

The quasi-meet operation on
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
is compatible with the meet
operation on
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, in the sense that

\overline{\pi\curlywedge\sigma}=\bar{\pi}\wedge\bar{\sigma}
.

(ii)

\pi\curlywedge\sigma\leq\pi
for any
\pi,\sigma\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.

(iii)

If
\sigma\leq\rho\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, then
\sigma\curlywedge\pi\leq\rho\curlywedge\pi
for any
\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
.

(iv)

\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\pi

\iff

\bar{\pi}\leq\bar{\sigma}
.

(v)

\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\sigma

\iff

\sigma\leq\pi
.

Proof.

The first three items are immediate from the definition.
To see
(iv)
,
assume first
\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\pi
. Then by
(i)

also

\bar{\pi}=\bar{\pi}\wedge\bar{\sigma}\leq\bar{\sigma}
. Conversely,
if
\bar{\pi}\leq\bar{\sigma}
, then
\bar{\pi}\wedge\bar{\sigma}=\bar{\pi}

and by
(ii)

\pi\curlywedge\sigma\leq\pi
. Since the number of blocks of

\bar{\pi}\wedge\bar{\sigma}
and
\pi\curlywedge\sigma
are equal,
we must have
\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\pi
.

As for
(v)
, if
\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\sigma

then it follows from
(ii)
that
\sigma\leq\pi
.
On the other hand, if
\sigma\leq\pi
and
\pi=(B_{1},B_{2},\dots,B_{k})
,
then
\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\sigma\rfloor_{B_{1}}\sigma\rfloor_{B_{2}}\dotsm\sigma\rfloor_{B_{k}}

and the order of the blocks of
\sigma
remains unchanged, so

\pi\curlywedge\sigma=\sigma
.
∎

To describe
the interval structure of the poset
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
we start with principal ideals.

Definition A.22
.

Let
P
be a poset.
A
down-set
or
order ideal

is a subset
I
such that

x\in I
and
y\leq x
implies
y\in I
.
The
principal ideal generated by
x
,
denoted by
\downset x
, is the smallest down-set containing
x
, i.e.,

\downset x=\{y\in P:y\leq x\}.

The following proposition is immediate.

Proposition A.23
.

The principal ideal generated by an element

\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
is canonically isomorphic to

\mathcal{OP}_{P_{1}}\times\mathcal{OP}_{P_{2}}\times\dotsm\times\mathcal{OP}_{P_{p}}

via the map

\sigma\mapsto(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{1}},\sigma\rfloor_{P_{2}},\dots,\sigma\rfloor_{P_{p}})

There is no direct analogue of Proposition
A.7

for ordered set partitions; instead the next proposition shows
that the interval structure can be expressed in terms of lattices
of interval partitions.

Proposition A.24
.

Let
\sigma,\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
be ordered set partitions of
size
\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert=s
and
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert=p
respectively
such that
\sigma\leq\pi
.
If
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots P_{p})
, let
k_{j}
be the number of blocks of
\sigma

contained in
P_{j}
,
j\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
.
Then
k_{1}+k_{2}+\dots+k_{p}=s
and as a poset the interval

[\sigma,\pi]
is canonically isomorphic to

\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{k_{p}}
.
More precisely, if
\sigma=(S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{s})\leq\pi\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
,
then there exists a unique
\tau=(T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{p})\in\mathcal{I}_{s}
such that

\pi=\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in T_{1}}S_{i},\bigcup_{i\in T_{2}}S_{i},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in T_{p}}S_{i}\biggr).

The map
\Phi:\mathcal{I}_{T_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{T_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{T_{p}}\to[\sigma,\pi]
by

(\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\ldots,\tau_{p})\mapsto\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in T_{1,1}}S_{i},\bigcup_{i\in T_{1,2}}S_{i},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in T_{1,k_{1}}}S_{i},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in T_{p,1}}S_{i},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in T_{p,k_{p}}}S_{i}\biggr),

where
\tau_{i}=(T_{i,1},T_{i,2},\ldots,T_{i,k_{i}})
, is a bijection, and so its inverse establishes a bijection

\Psi:=\Phi^{-1}:[\sigma,\pi]\to\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{k_{p}}

with
k_{i}:=\left\lvert T_{i}\right\rvert
.
The composition
(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{p})
is called the
type

of the interval
[\sigma,\pi]
.

The example in
Figure
6
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{I}_{2}\times\mathcal{I}_{3}
.

Figure 6.
An interval isomorphic to
\mathcal{I}_{2}\times\mathcal{I}_{3}
.

Proof.

Let
\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\ldots,P_{p})
. Each block of
\pi
is the union of
blocks of
\sigma
, and so we can find
A\subseteq[s]
such that

P_{1}=\cup_{i\in A}S_{i}
. We show that there exists
k
such that
A=[k]
. Suppose that there are
1\leq u<v\leq p
such that
u\notin A
and

v\in A
. Then there is
j\geq 2
such that
S_{u}\subseteq P_{j}
. This
contradicts the fact that
u<v
,
S_{v}\subseteq P_{1}
and
\sigma\leq\pi
.
Hence
A=[k]
for some
k
.
Removing the first block of
\pi
and the first
k
blocks of
\sigma

we can repeat the argument with the ordered set partitions

(S_{k+1},S_{k+2},\ldots,S_{s})\leq(P_{2},P_{3},\ldots,P_{p})

and find

P_{2}=\cup_{k+1\leq i\leq k+l}S_{i}
for some
l
.
After a finite number of iterations we thus
construct a unique interval partition

\tau=(T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,T_{p})\in\mathcal{I}_{s}
such that

\pi=\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in T_{1}}S_{i},\bigcup_{i\in T_{2}}S_{i},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in T_{p}}S_{i}\biggr).

Clearly the image of
\Phi
is contained in
[\sigma,\pi]
and
\Phi
is injective and it remains to show surjectivity.
To this end pick an arbitrary
\rho\in[\sigma,\pi]
.
From the first part of the proposition we infer that

\rho\geq\sigma
is of the form

\rho=\biggl(\bigcup_{i\in G_{1}}S_{i},\bigcup_{i\in G_{2}}S_{i},\ldots,\bigcup_{i\in G_{t}}S_{i}\biggr)

for some
\gamma=(G_{1},G_{2},\ldots,G_{t})\in\mathcal{I}_{s}
. Since
\rho\leq\pi
, the partition
\gamma
must be finer than
\tau
. Hence, each restriction
\gamma\rfloor_{T_{i}}\in\mathcal{I}_{T_{i}},i\in[p]
, consists of sequence of consecutive blocks of
\gamma
without splitting any original block
G_{1},G_{2},\dots,G_{t}\in\gamma
.
Hence we obtain
\Phi((\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{p}))=\rho
and
the map
\Phi
is indeed bijective.
∎

A.3.
Incidence algebras and multiplicative functions

Let
(P,\leq)
be a (finite) partially ordered set.
The incidence algebra
\IncAlg(P)=\IncAlg(P,\mathbb{C})
is the algebra of
functions supported on the set of pairs
\{(x,y)\in P\times P:x,y\in P;x\leq y\}

with convolution

f*g(x,y)=\sum_{x\leq z\leq y}f(x,z)\,g(z,y)

For example, if
P
is the
n
-set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
with the natural order,
then
\IncAlg(P)

is the algebra of
n\times n
upper triangular matrices.
The algebra
\IncAlg(P)
is unital with the Kronecker function
\delta(x,y)

serving as the unit element and a function
f\in\IncAlg(P)

is invertible if and only if
f(x,x)
is nonzero for every
x\in P
.
An example of an invertible function is the
Zeta function
,
which is defined as
\zeta(x,y)\equiv 1
.
Its inverse is called the
Möbius function
of
P
,
denoted
\mu(x,y)
.
For functions
F,G:P\to\mathbb{C}
we have the fundamental equivalence
(“Möbius inversion formula”)

\left(\forall x\in P:F(x)=\sum_{y\leq x}G(y)\right)\qquad\iff\qquad\left(\forall x\in P:G(x)=\sum_{y\leq x}F(y)\,\mu(y,x)\right)

A function
f\in\IncAlg(\mathcal{P}_{n})
(actually a family of functions)
is called
multiplicative
if there is a
characteristic sequence

(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
such
that for any pair
\sigma,\pi\in\mathcal{P}
we have

f(\sigma,\pi)=\prod f_{i}^{k_{i}}

where
k_{i}
are the structural constants of the interval
[\sigma,\pi]

from Proposition
A.7
.
It can be shown
[
DRS72
]
that the
multiplicative functions form a subalgebra of the incidence algebra

\IncAlg(\mathcal{P}_{n})
.
For example, the Zeta function is multiplicative with characteristic sequence

(1,1,\dots)
and the Möbius function is multiplicative as well
with characteristic sequence
\mu_{n}=(-1)^{n-1}(n-1)!
,
cf.
[
Sch54
,
Rot64
]
;
more precisely, if
\pi=\{P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p}\}
then

(A.1)

\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\sigma,\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{k_{i}-1}(k_{i}-1)!

where
k_{i}=\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}})
.
Multiplicative functions on the lattice of set partitions provide
a combinatorial model for Faà di Bruno’s formula
which expresses the Taylor coefficients of a composition of exponential
formal power series in terms of the coefficients of the original functions,
see

[
DRS72
,
Sta99
]
;
in the case of noncrossing partitions the convolution is commutative
and can be modeled as multiplication of certain power series (“
S
-transforms”), see
[
NS06
]
.
The lattice of interval partitions combinatorially models the composition
of ordinary formal power series, see
[
Joy81
]
.
From Proposition
A.24
one might guess that convolution
on the poset of ordered set partitions is also related to some kind
of function composition, and Proposition
A.26
below shows that this is indeed the case for a certain class of functions to be defined next.

Definition A.25
.

Denote by
\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\infty}
the set of finite sequences of positive integers:

\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\infty}=\bigcup_{p=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{N}^{p}.

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
let
F_{\mathcal{OP}^{m}}
be the set of
\mathbb{C}
-valued functions
f
on the set of
m
-chains

\{(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{m})\in\cup_{n=1}^{\infty}\underbrace{(\mathcal{OP}_{n}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{OP}_{n})}_{\text{$m$ fold}}\mid\sigma_{1}\leq\sigma_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\sigma_{m}\}.

We are concerned with different levels of reduced incidence algebras.

(i)

A function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
is said to be
adapted

if
there is a family
(f_{\underline{k}})_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\infty}}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
such that

f(\sigma,\pi)=f_{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}},

where
(k_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
is the type of the interval
[\sigma,\pi]
as
defined in Proposition
A.24
. The family
(f_{\underline{k}})_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\infty}}
is called the
defining family
.

(ii)

To any adapted function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
we associate its multivariate generating function

\underline{Z}_{f}(\underline{z})=\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\infty}}f_{\underline{k}}\underline{z}^{\underline{k}},\qquad\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},\dots)

with the usual multiindex convention
\underline{z}^{\underline{k}}=z_{1}^{k_{1}}z_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots
,
where
z_{1},z_{2},\dots
are commuting indeterminates.

(iii)

A function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
is said to be
multiplicative
if it is adapted and moreover the defining family satisfies

f_{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}f_{k_{i}}.

If
f
is multiplicative, then the sequence of values
f_{n}=f(\hat{0}_{n},\hat{1}_{n})
is called the
defining
sequence
of
f
.

(iv)

To a multiplicative function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
we associate the (univariate)
generating function

Z_{f}(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}f_{n}z^{n}.

(v)

A function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{3}}
is said to be
quasi-multiplicative
if
there is an array of coefficients
(f_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathbb{C}
such that

f(\sigma,\rho,\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\prod_{G\in\gamma_{i}}f_{i,\left\lvert G\right\rvert},

where
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{p})
is the image of
\rho
under the map
\Psi
in Proposition

A.24
. The array
(f_{jk})_{j,k=1}^{\infty}
is called the
defining array
of
f
.

(vi)

To a quasi-multiplicative function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{3}}
we associate a sequence of (univariate) generating functions

Z_{f}^{(j)}(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}f_{jk}z^{k},\qquad j\in\mathbb{N}.

(vii)

For
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{3}}
and
g\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
we define the convolution

(f\otriangle g)(\sigma,\pi):=\sum_{\rho\in[\sigma,\pi]}f(\sigma,\rho,\pi)\,g(\rho,\pi),\qquad\sigma\leq\pi.

This latter provides a combinatorial model for the composition
of multivariate functions.

Proposition A.26
.

If a function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{3}}
is quasi-multiplicative and
g\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
is adapted then
f\otriangle g\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
is adapted and

\underline{Z}_{f\otriangle g}(\underline{z})=\underline{Z}_{g}(Z_{f}^{(1)}(z_{1}),Z_{f}^{(2)}(z_{2}),\dots),\qquad\underline{z}=(z_{1},z_{2},\dots).

Proof.

We use the notations in the statement of Proposition

A.24
. Pick any
\rho\in[\sigma,\pi]
and let

(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{p}):=\Psi(\rho)
be its image under
\Psi
.
Thus
\gamma_{i}=(G_{i,1},G_{i,2},\ldots,G_{i,r_{i}})\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{i}}
is
an interval partition and we have the bijective images

\displaystyle\Psi([\sigma,\rho])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert G_{1,1}\right\rvert}\times\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert G_{1,2}\right\rvert}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert G_{1,r_{1}}\right\rvert}\times\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert G_{2,1}\right\rvert}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert G_{p,r_{p}}\right\rvert},

\displaystyle\Psi([\rho,\pi])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert\gamma_{1}\right\rvert}\times\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert\gamma_{2}\right\rvert}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{\left\lvert\gamma_{p}\right\rvert}.

Hence

\displaystyle(f\otriangle g)\,(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in[\sigma,\pi]}f(\sigma,\rho,\pi)\,g(\rho,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\ldots,\gamma_{p})\in\mathcal{I}_{k_{1}}\times\mathcal{I}_{k_{2}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{I}_{k_{p}}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\prod_{G\in\gamma_{i}}f_{i,\left\lvert G\right\rvert}\right)g_{\left\lvert\gamma_{1}\right\rvert,\left\lvert\gamma_{2}\right\rvert,\dots,\left\lvert\gamma_{p}\right\rvert}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r_{i}\in[k_{i}],1\leq i\leq p}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(n_{ik})_{i\in[p],k\in[r_{i}]}\\
n_{i1}+n_{i2}+\cdots+n_{ir_{i}}=k_{i},1\leq i\leq p\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{r_{i}}f_{i,n_{ik}}\right)g_{r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{p}}

\displaystyle=:(f\otriangle g)_{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{p}}.

This shows that for every pair
(\sigma,\pi)
the
value
f\otriangle g(\sigma,\pi)
is determined by the structural sequence

(k_{1},\dots,k_{p})
and thus
f\otriangle g
is adapted.
Now multiplying the terms with
z^{\underline{k}}
and summing over
\underline{k}
we obtain

\displaystyle\underline{Z}_{f\otriangle g}(\underline{z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{\underline{k}=(k_{1},k_{2},\dots)\in\mathbb{N}_{\mathrm{fin}}^{\infty}}(f\otriangle g)_{\underline{k}}\underline{z}^{\underline{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\geq 1}\sum_{\underline{r}=(r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(n_{i1},\dots,n_{ir_{i}})\in\mathbb{N}^{r_{i}}\\
1\leq i\leq p\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{r_{i}}f_{i,n_{ik}}z_{i}^{n_{ik}}\right)g_{r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p\geq 1}\sum_{\underline{r}=(r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}g_{r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}Z_{f}^{(i)}(z_{i})^{r_{i}}

\displaystyle=\underline{Z}_{g}(Z_{f}^{(1)}(z_{1}),Z_{f}^{(2)}(z_{2}),\dots).

∎

Corollary A.27
.

If
f,g\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
are multiplicative, then so is
f\ast g
and

Z_{f\ast g}(z)=Z_{g}(Z_{f}(z)).

Proof.

Given a multiplicative function
f\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
,
we lift it to a quasi-multiplicative function
\tilde{f}\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{3}}

via its defining family
\tilde{f}_{jk}=f_{k}
, i.e.,

\tilde{f}(\sigma,\rho,\pi):=f(\sigma,\rho)
and the generating functions
are
Z_{\tilde{f}}^{(j)}(z)=Z_{\tilde{f}}(z)
for all
j\geq 1
.
On the other hand,
g
is multiplicative, therefore adapted with

g_{k_{1},\dots,k_{p}}=g_{k_{1}}\cdots g_{k_{p}}
and has generating function

\underline{Z}_{g}(z_{1},z_{2},\dots)=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{p})\in\mathbb{N}^{p}}g_{k_{1}}g_{k_{2}}\dotsm g_{k_{p}}z_{1}^{k_{1}}z_{2}^{k_{2}}\cdots z_{p}^{k_{p}}=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}Z_{g}(z_{1})Z_{g}(z_{2})\dotsm Z_{g}(z_{p}).

By Proposition
A.26

\underline{Z}_{\tilde{f}\otriangle g}(z_{1},z_{2},\dots)=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}Z_{g}(Z_{f}(z_{1}))Z_{g}(Z_{f}(z_{2}))\dotsm Z_{g}(Z_{f}(z_{p})).

This shows that
(\tilde{f}\otriangle g)_{k_{1},k_{2},\dots,k_{p}}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}h_{k_{i}}
, where
h_{k}:=\frac{1}{k!}\frac{d^{k}}{dz^{k}}\big|_{z=0}Z_{g}(Z_{f}(z)).
Therefore
\tilde{f}\otriangle g=f\ast g
is multiplicative and
Z_{f\ast g}=Z_{g}(Z_{f}(z))
.
∎

A.4.
Special functions on the poset of ordered set partitions

We define several special functions in the case of ordered set partitions, and compute their generating functions.

Definition A.28
.

Given ordered set partitions
\sigma\leq\rho\leq\pi=(P_{1},P_{2},\dots,P_{p})\in\mathcal{OP}_{n}
, a sequence
\underline{t}=(t_{1},t_{2},\dots)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathbb{N}}
and a number
t\in\mathbb{R}
we define

\displaystyle\beta_{\underline{t}}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{t_{i}}{\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}})},

\displaystyle\beta_{t}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\beta_{(t,t,\dots)}(\sigma,\pi),

\displaystyle\gamma_{\underline{t}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\prod_{G\in\gamma_{i}}\binom{t_{i}}{\left\lvert G\right\rvert},

where
\binom{t}{n}
is the generalized binomial coefficient and
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{p})
is the image of
\rho
by the map
\Psi
in Proposition

A.24
.
Moreover, for
\sigma\leq\pi
we define

\displaystyle[\sigma:\pi]

\displaystyle=\prod_{P\in\bar{\pi}}\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P}),

(A.2)

\displaystyle[\sigma:\pi]!

\displaystyle=\prod_{P\in\bar{\pi}}\#(\sigma\rfloor_{P})!,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[\sigma:\pi]!},

(A.3)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}}{[\sigma:\pi]}.

Remark A.29
.

(i)

The combinatorial significance of the numbers (
A.2
) is

(A.4)

[\sigma:\pi]!=\#\{\rho~|~\rho\leq\pi\text{ and }\bar{\rho}=\bar{\sigma}\}

(ii)

The values
\beta_{\underline{t}}(\sigma,\pi)
and
\gamma_{\underline{t}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)

depend only on the first
\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert
elements of
\underline{t}
.

(iii)

\beta_{\underline{t}},\beta_{t},\gamma_{\underline{t}}
are related via

\beta_{\underline{t}}(\sigma,\pi)=\gamma_{\underline{t}}(\sigma,\pi,\pi)\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{\underline{t}}(\sigma,\rho,\pi)=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\beta_{t_{i}}(\sigma\rfloor_{P_{i}},\rho\rfloor_{P_{i}}).

Since every interval in
\mathcal{OP}_{n}
is isomorphic to a product of lattices of
interval partitions (Proposition
A.24
)
and thus is a Boolean lattice, it follows that the semilattice of ordered set partitions is Eulerian,
i.e., its Möbius function only depends on the rank and
\mu_{\mathcal{OP}}(\sigma,\pi)=(-1)^{\left\lvert\sigma\right\rvert-\left\lvert\pi\right\rvert}
,
see
[
Rot64
, Proposition 3 and its Corollary]
.
Hence if
\sigma\leq\pi
we may write,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}(\sigma,\pi)=\frac{\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\sigma},\bar{\pi})}{[\sigma:\pi]!},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(\sigma,\pi)=\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{OP}}(\sigma,\pi)}{[\sigma:\pi]}=\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\sigma},\bar{\pi})}{[\sigma:\pi]!}=\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\bar{\sigma},\bar{\pi})\,\tilde{\zeta}(\sigma,\pi),

where the Möbius function
\mu_{\mathcal{P}}
was defined in formula (
A.1
).

As a consequence of observation (
A.4
) we have the
following connection between the Möbius inversion on
\mathcal{OP}
and
\mathcal{P}
.

Proposition A.30
.

Let
f:\mathcal{OP}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function which is invariant under permutations of
the blocks, i.e.,
f(\pi)=f(\pi^{\prime})
whenever
\overline{\pi}=\overline{\pi^{\prime}}

and denote by
\bar{f}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{C}
the corresponding function on
\mathcal{P}
,
then the convolutions

f*_{\mathcal{OP}}\tilde{\zeta}

and

f*_{\mathcal{OP}}\tilde{\mu}
are invariant as well and are given by

\displaystyle f*_{\mathcal{OP}}\tilde{\zeta}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\bar{f}*_{\mathcal{P}}\zeta_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{\pi})

\displaystyle f*_{\mathcal{OP}}\tilde{\mu}(\pi)

\displaystyle=\bar{f}*_{\mathcal{P}}\mu_{\mathcal{P}}(\overline{\pi}),

i.e.,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\mathcal{OP}\\
\rho\leq\pi\end{subarray}}f(\rho)\,\tilde{\zeta}(\rho,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}\\
\sigma\leq\overline{\pi}\end{subarray}}\bar{f}(\sigma)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\mathcal{OP}\\
\rho\leq\pi\end{subarray}}f(\rho)\,\tilde{\mu}_{\mathcal{OP}}(\rho,\pi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathcal{P}\\
\sigma\leq\overline{\pi}\end{subarray}}\bar{f}(\sigma)\,\mu(\sigma,\overline{\pi}).

Proposition A.31
.

(i)

The function
\beta_{\underline{t}}\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
is adapted with defining family

(\beta_{\underline{t}})_{\underline{k}}=\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{t_{i}}{k_{i}}.

(ii)

The function
\gamma_{\underline{t}}\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{3}}
is quasi-multiplicative with defining array

(\gamma_{\underline{t}})_{jk}=\binom{t_{j}}{k}.

(iii)

The functions
\beta_{t},\widetilde{\mu},\widetilde{\zeta}\in F_{\mathcal{OP}^{2}}
are
multiplicative with defining sequences

\displaystyle\beta_{t}(\hat{0}_{n},\hat{1}_{n})=\binom{t}{n},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}(\hat{0}_{n},\hat{1}_{n})=\frac{1}{n!},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}(\hat{0}_{n},\hat{1}_{n})=\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}.

Proof.

The claims follow by definition and by Proposition
A.24
.
∎

Corollary A.32
.

(i)

The inverse function (with respect to the convolution
\ast
) of
\widetilde{\mu}
is
\widetilde{\zeta}
.

(ii)

For
\underline{s},\underline{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
we have

\gamma_{\underline{s}}\otriangle\beta_{\underline{t}}=\beta_{\underline{s}\circ\underline{t}},

where
\underline{s}\circ\underline{t}=(s_{1}t_{1},s_{2}t_{2},\dots)
.

(iii)

\beta_{t}
satisfies the semigroup property
\beta_{s}\ast\beta_{t}=\beta_{st}
for
s,t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

(i)
Since
Z_{\widetilde{\mu}}(z)=\log(1+z)
and
Z_{\widetilde{\zeta}}(z)=e^{z}-1
, we have
Z_{\widetilde{\mu}}(Z_{\widetilde{\zeta}}(z))=z
.

(ii)
We have

\displaystyle Z_{\gamma_{\underline{s}}}^{(j)}(z)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\binom{s_{j}}{k}z^{k}=(1+z)^{s_{j}}-1,

\displaystyle\underline{Z}_{\beta_{\underline{t}}}(\underline{z})=\sum_{\underline{k}}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\binom{t_{i}}{k_{i}}z_{i}^{k_{i}}=\sum_{p\geq 1}\prod_{i=1}^{p}\left((1+z_{i})^{t_{i}}-1\right).

By Proposition
A.26
,
\underline{Z}_{\gamma_{\underline{s}}\otriangle\beta_{\underline{t}}}(\underline{z})=\underline{Z}_{\beta_{\underline{t}}}(Z_{\gamma_{\underline{s}}}^{(1)}(z_{1}),Z_{\gamma_{\underline{s}}}^{(2)}(z_{2}),\dots)
, which equals
\underline{Z}_{\beta_{\underline{s}\circ\underline{t}}}(\underline{z})
.

(iii)
We can use
Z_{\beta_{t}}(z)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\binom{t}{n}z^{n}=(1+z)^{t}-1
and Corollary
A.27
.
∎
